18.6 Convergence of the candidate's fits (probe, autumn, as the candidate, 12000 instead of 4000 steps; run ca): loss 6938 -> 6930, in-sample MAE
  1.271 -> 1.255 ct; forecasts curve alone 1.69 / 1.98 -> 1.68 / 1.93, with layer 1.59 / 1.85 -> 1.59 / 1.83, candidate 1.31 / 1.63 -> 1.32 / 1.63
  (+1% [-0..+1] at night). Not worth three times the fitting time; closed.
18.7 Decision by the rule of 18.2 (b passed in sign, c no harm): the gas-share prior is ADOPTED in the candidate's curve.
  New candidate = (structural curve with GS=1 and its layer, runs kw / ks / ka) averaged 50/50 with Timestral's price: out/candk_<period>.npz,
  0.79 / 1.28 (summer), 1.30 / 1.62 (autumn), 0.67 / 0.87 (winter); before 0.79 / 1.28, 1.31 / 1.63, 0.67 / 0.88.
  What adoption means, in plain terms:
  - No accuracy gain to speak of: candidate -1% [-2..-0] in autumn, unchanged in winter and summer.
  - Frozen, curve alone: better only where gas is far from its level at the freeze date. Autumn -7% (January, 4000 steps), -3% [-17..+12]
    (January, 12000 steps), -16% (March, both). Summer: January -2% [-4..+1] at 4000 steps and -11% [-21..+0] at 12000, against the 10-25%
    expected, so that expectation FAILED for summer; March +1% and +2%, i.e. slightly worse.
  - Frozen, with the online layer (the configuration the candidate actually runs in): -2%..+1%. In that configuration the prior does not serve
    the purpose it was proposed for; the layer does. For the two freeze dates run, frozen + layer equals refitting in autumn and is 7-10% worse
    at night in summer.
  - The January result is not "an under-response to gas fixed". At 4000 steps the baseline already sat at or above the target (60 GW: +4.7 sd)
    and its autumn curve was too high (night bias -0.66 -> -0.35 with the prior); at 12000 steps the baseline was too low (+1.18 -> -0.27). The
    prior pulls both towards one split.
  - Stability: the narrowing of the gas share (0.40..0.80 -> 0.63..0.67) is by construction. The one diagnostic that could fail, the difference
    between forecasts of the same hour from consecutive daily fits, did fail (0.44 -> 0.45 ct).
  - Scope: research scripts only (exp/smodel.py, option GS=1). The repository's main branch runs the tree models; nothing installed changes.
  Not covered: the convergence probe (18.6) is autumn and the baseline arm only.
  Caveats: the prior's quantity and tightness were chosen on the index model after seeing 2026 data (16.6); the January frozen effect is 3-7%,
  below the 10-25% expected, and at 12000 steps its interval includes zero; the frozen curve's largest error (the March level) is not a gas-share
  matter and the prior does not fix it.


19. FIT ASSESSMENT IN PICTURES (user: "you are not following bayes workflow ... not a single summary statistic but visualization derived fit
    assessment")
=====================================================================================================================================
19.0 The adoption of the gas-share prior in 18.7 is PROVISIONAL. It was decided on night-MAE rules. It is re-assessed here from what the fitted
  model looks like against data in the dimensions it is built on. No pass / fail score is attached to this section.
19.1 The panels, and what each would show, written BEFORE drawing
  P1 In sample, one fit (2026-09-18): price against effective residual load, data coloured by gas price, the two fitted curves on top.
     Expect: the arms cannot be told apart in sample (equal error at equal steps; they agree at the origin's gas price). Shown once.
  P2 The weekly price-scale path against the weekly gas price, both arms, same window.
     Prior right: the baseline's scale path follows gas (the unexplained drift is carrying gas), the prior's does not.
     Prior wrong (passes too much gas through): the prior's scale path runs AGAINST gas, undoing its own gas term.
     Irrelevant: the two paths coincide.
  P3 A frozen curve in raw space: night prices against the gas price of the day within load bands, published loads, points coloured by month
     (gas rose with the calendar in 2026 and the level drifted at flat gas, so month must be visible), with each arm's frozen line.
     Prior right: within a month and across months the points follow the prior arm's slope. Prior wrong: they follow the baseline's flatter slope.
     Confounded: months sit on parallel, shifted lines, and no slope can be read off.
     The January fit at 4000 steps is labelled: its split sat at the target by accident (18.3).
  P4 Where the prior comes from: 2021-2023 price against gas-fired cost in a load band, coloured by year, pooled line and one line per year.
     If the tight sd (0.02 on the share) is supported, the years lie along one line. If the pooled slope is a between-year effect, the per-year
     lines differ (0.67 / 1.11 / 0.51 at 25-35 GW) and the tightness is not supported by its own source.
  P5, P6 Sampling of the tempered posterior at one origin, chains drawn separately in the plane base curve at 30 GW against gas term at 30 GW,
     with r-hat and effective sample size on the panel; baseline and prior arm. Earlier runs of the baseline did not mix (r-hat 1.3-4.3, ESS 2-9),
     so no bands are drawn from them. Identification problem: the baseline's chains lie along a ridge or disagree; the prior arm's chains overlap.
     If the prior arm does not mix either, the split is not the reason the sampler fails.
  P7 Prior predictive, sampled with the likelihood weight at zero so that the flat-top and gas-share terms are included: price curves at one gas
     price and the gas share against load, with and without the gas-share term. Check: are these plausible price curves before any data?
  P8 Replicated data at the day level: daily mean residual, observed against replicated from the fitted noise model, by load band including below
     15 GW. The replicates have independent hourly noise; the real errors are whole nights. Expect the observed day-level spread to exceed the
     replicated one; how much, and in which band, says what the noise model is missing.
  The decision on the prior is then written from P2-P6. If they cannot tell, it rests on interpretability, not fit, and is said so.
  P9 (added after seeing P2 and P4, before running): prior sensitivity. The same fit with the prior's sd widened 3, 10 and 30 times, and the two
     structural read-outs of P2 for each: how much the weekly price scale still follows gas, and where the gas share lands. If a prior ten times
     wider still takes gas out of the drift, the tightness of 18.1 is not needed and the prior can be as wide as its source (P4) supports. If the
     decoupling needs the tight version, the prior is doing the data's job and that has to be said.
  P5b (added after seeing P5, before running): the four chains of each sampling run were all started at that model's own fitted mode, so their
     agreement on the gas share is not evidence that the share is identified. The model WITHOUT the prior is sampled again with its chains
     started at the fit WITH the prior (share 0.65). If the split is a flat ridge the chains stay near 0.65; if the window identifies a low
     share they move back towards 0.46 within the run.
  P10 (added after P5b, before drawing): the form of the gas response in raw data. Same season a year apart, inside the window of the 2026-09-18
     fit: 20 Jul - 17 Sep 2025 (gas about 33) against 20 Jul - 17 Sep 2026 (gas about 70), by band of effective residual load. Ratio of the
     median prices, with what each form implies: proportional to gas-fired cost (one horizontal line, the ratio of the two periods' gas-fired
     cost), and the candidate's form, a fixed base plus gas times a gas term, for the fit without and with the prior (no weekly drift).
     If the observed ratio is flat near the cost ratio, prices scaled with gas-fired cost at every load, the additive gas term on a fixed base is
     the wrong form, and the weekly drift is where the fit puts the rest. If the observed ratio follows one arm's curve, that arm's split is the
     better description.
  P11 (added after P10, before drawing): the response to gas by time scale. Hours at 25-50 GW; the price level of each day relative to the load
     curve (log price minus log of the fitted curve's shape at a fixed gas price, daily mean); the log of gas-fired cost split into a slow part
     (28-day trailing mean) and a fast part (the day's deviation from it). Two scatters, slow and fast, with the elasticity of each.
     If prices follow slow moves in gas-fired cost about one for one and fast moves much less, the two findings fit together: P10 (a year apart:
     proportional) and P5b (within the window the model identifies a weak response and lets the weekly drift carry the rest). The modelling
     consequence would then be neither the tight prior nor the present form, but gas entering as a smoothed cost that scales the price.
     If both elasticities are near one, the weak response the model finds is an artefact of the model, and the prior is simply right.
19.2 What the pictures show (figures out/fit_gas_1.png, fit_gas_2.png, fit_gas_3.png; one fit, 2026-09-18, unless stated)
  P1  In sample the two fits are the same curve (error over the last 8 weeks 1.73 and 1.70 ct). As expected; nothing in sample can choose.
  P2  Without the prior the weekly price scale follows gas: +4.9% per 10 EUR of gas (correlation 0.75, scale 0.73..1.01). With the tight prior
      +1.2% (0.26; 0.85..1.08). By the reading written beforehand this is the "prior right" pattern: the unexplained drift was carrying gas.
  P9  Only the tight prior does that. Prior sd x3: +2.2%, share at 30 GW 0.59; x10: +4.2%, 0.47; x30: +4.8%, 0.44; none: +4.9%, 0.43.
  P4  The prior's source does not support that tightness: slope of price on gas-fired cost at 25-35 GW 0.67 (2021), 1.11 (2022), 0.51 (2023),
      0.81 pooled. The years do not share one line; the pooled slope is partly a between-year effect.
  P5  Sampling of the tempered posterior does not mix in either arm (worst r-hat 2.18 without the prior, 1.71 with it; smallest effective
      sample 3 and 4), so no posterior bands are drawn from it. The chains of each run were started at that model's own mode. P5b: the model
      WITHOUT the prior, chains started at the fit WITH the prior (share 0.65): by the first 30 kept draws they are at 0.45-0.49 and end at
      0.43-0.45, like the chains started at the mode. For THIS fit (one window, a tempered likelihood, chains that do not mix, warm-up draws not
      kept so the walk back is inferred from where they end) the split is not a flat ridge: it sits at a low gas share (about 0.46). That does
      not carry to other windows: in January the fitted split moved with the step count (0.65 at 4000 steps, 0.53 at 12000) and through winter
      the fit-by-fit shares were 0.55-0.80. ("Five posterior spreads" in an earlier version is withdrawn: unmixed chains give no posterior sd.)
  P6  With the tight prior the chains sit at 0.65 (spread 0.011); the rest of the model still does not mix.
  P7  Prior predictive, sampled with the likelihood weight at zero (flat-top and gas-share terms included; 85-107 divergent transitions in the
      prior itself): the candidate's priors allow a price at 30 GW and gas 41 of -3.9 / 6.9 / 102.6 ct (5 / 50 / 95%) and a gas share whose prior
      median is above 1 (negative base). They were never checked as statements about prices. With the gas-share term: 2.6 / 10.2 / 33.0 ct.
  P8  Day-level errors against replicates from the fitted noise model (independent hours): observed / replicated root mean square of the daily
      mean residual 2.78 / 1.63 ct below 15 GW (1.7 x), 0.94 / 0.47 at 15-35 GW (2.0 x), 1.45 / 0.54 above 35 GW (2.7 x). Hour by hour the noise
      model is right above 15 GW (median |residual| 0.57 / 0.60 and 0.68 / 0.72) and too narrow below (1.70 / 1.35). What is missing is a
      component common to the hours of a day.
  P3  A curve frozen on 31 March against the nights that followed: the points lie above both arms' lines already in April-June at unchanged
      gas, and further above later. The level moves at flat gas; the slope against gas cannot be read off (slopes within single months
      0.04..0.32 ct per EUR). The "confounded" case of 19.1.
  P10 Same season a year apart (20 Jul - 17 Sep; gas 33 -> 66, carbon 72 -> 82, gas-fired cost 88 -> 152, factor 1.73): observed price ratio
      1.73 1.72 1.70 1.72 1.75 1.68 at 27.5 .. 52.5 GW (5-95% about +-0.07), 1.82 at 22.5, 1.96 at 17.5, 2.91 at 12.5 GW. From 25 GW up prices
      rose by the factor of gas-fired cost at every load level. The candidate's gas term alone gives 1.37-1.44 and the weekly scale 1.25 on top;
      with the tight prior 1.58-1.64 and 1.08.
  P11 Response by time scale, hours at 25-50 GW, 488 days: elasticity of the day's price level to the 28-day mean of gas-fired cost 1.02; to
      the day's deviation from it 0.77. (The week-block bootstrap intervals first given, 0.96..1.09 and 0.62..0.94, are withdrawn: a 28-day mean
      of one gas rise holds far fewer independent pieces than weeks, the same overcounting found in the tight prior.) With a 7 / 14 / 56-day mean: slow 0.98 / 1.00
      / 1.10, fast 0.63 / 0.71 / 0.70. The candidate's fitted gas share of 0.46 is an elasticity of 0.63 to gas-fired cost: the fast response.
      Caveat: the slow part and P10 both compare 2026 with 2025; anything else that rose proportionally at all loads is in there too. The
      2021-2023 pooled slopes (0.81 at 25-35 GW, 1.08 at 35-50) give a long-run elasticity of 0.9-1.0 from an independent period.
  P12 (check after review: is the slow elasticity of one separable from a non-gas rise?) Daily price level at 25-50 GW against gas-fired cost
      by month. April -> June 2025: level +0.10, cost +0.07, net +0.03. April -> June 2026: level +0.09, cost 0.00, net +0.09: the 2026 spring
      rise at flat gas is NOT seasonal. Year on year, same month, level change / cost change: 0.96 0.97 1.35 1.02 1.09 0.88 (April..September);
      across those six months slope 0.86 with intercept +0.05. Level net of cost at elasticity one, by month over the 18 months: within +-0.06
      except March 2026 (-0.13, the month of the gas spike); at elasticity 0.8 it trends to +0.14 by August 2026.
      So: "one for one" fits (five of six months near 1, a flat net level), and so does "0.86 plus a few percent of something else". The slow
      elasticity is 0.85-1.0; the cost-scaled form below is a HYPOTHESIS with that range, not a finding.
19.3 Reading and decision
  The pieces fit together. Over months prices from 25 GW up scale with gas-fired cost one for one (P10, P11 slow, 2021-2023 pooled). A day's
  move in gas reaches them at 0.6-0.8 (P11 fast; P4 within years). The candidate's form has one gas term driven by the day's gas price and a
  weekly multiplicative drift; the fit gives the gas term the fast response (P5b: share 0.46 = elasticity 0.63) and lets the drift carry the
  rest (P2). The tight prior forces the slow response onto the day's gas price. Its centre is right for slow moves and wrong for what that term
  is asked to do, its tightness is not supported by its source (P4), and it works only by overriding the window (P9, P5b).
  DECISION: the adoption of 18.7 is WITHDRAWN. The candidate stays as it was (runs hs2 / ha / hw2).
  What the decision rests on, by 19.1: NOT on fit. P1 says fit cannot choose. P2 and P10 favour the prior's direction. P4 and P9 say its
  tightness is imposed. So this is a decision about what is supported: a prior that tight is not. The frozen-fit result of section 18 is
  unchanged, and the prior stays available for the case of a curve that cannot be refreshed.
  CORRECTION to 18.3, for the September fit: "the window does not identify the split between b and gas x h, and the prior chooses it" is wrong
  there. Equal in-sample error is not a flat posterior; started elsewhere, the chains end at the low split (P5b).
  P8 quantifies a known design choice (the likelihood is down-weighted by 1/8 for exactly this dependence); it is a size, not a new defect.
  Not yet looked at this way: the trough term, the supply-side port, the load-range blend weight and the state layer were all decided on scores.
  What the pictures say the model should be instead (not built yet): gas-fired cost, with carbon in it, as a scale on the price from about 25 GW
  up, at elasticity one for its slow level and about 0.7 for the day's deviation. Then the weekly drift has nothing of gas left to carry, and P2
  (drift against gas) becomes the check of that, with no tight prior. Three further defects are on the table from the same figures and are not
  about gas: priors that allow absurd price curves (P7); a noise model without a day-level component (P8), which is also why every interval
  from this curve would be too narrow by a factor of two or more at the day level; a parameterisation the sampler cannot traverse (P5, P6), so
  no posterior uncertainty from it can be trusted yet. And the level that moves at flat gas (P3) is still unexplained.


20. ZOOMING IN (user: "like going from google maps fully zoomed out with globe to zooming in bit by bit ... get rough model locked in then
    work on increasing model detail / DGP faithfulness")
=========================================================================================================================
20.0 How this section works
  One model per zoom level, each a strict refinement of the one before. A level is LOCKED when its pictures show that it describes what it claims
  to describe, including on data it was not fitted to; its structure then stays, and the next level adds one thing. What is added next is read
  off the residual pictures of the locked level (the largest structure left), not chosen in advance and not chosen by a score.
  Every level gets the same four looks: what its priors imply before data; the fit with full sampling (it must mix, or the level is not done);
  data against replicated data in the dimensions the level models; residuals against the dimensions it leaves out.
  Fitting data: 2024-03-01 .. 2025-11-30 (gas 28..55 EUR/MWh). Held out for the look: 2025-12-01 .. 2026-11-08 (gas 30..105). The held-out
  year is the one in which gas doubled, so "does the locked picture still hold there" is a real question at every level.
20.1 Level 0, the globe: price = c x gas-fired cost (gas-fired cost L = 1.85 gas + 0.37 carbon).
  Expect: price and L move together over the months; price / L is of order one but not constant, because it ignores load.
20.2 Level 1: price = L x k(residual load), k rising, piecewise linear on knots every 10 GW; the ratio price / L has Student-t noise of one size.
  Priors: k at -10 GW ~ N(0, 0.15); rise per 10 GW ~ half-normal(0.35), so k at 70 GW is about 2.2 +- 0.6 before data.
  Expect, if the level is right: one curve fits both the fitting period and the held-out year although gas doubled (that is the claim "prices
  scale with gas-fired cost"); the residuals against the gas price are flat. Expect to be wrong about: the noise (one size, independent hours),
  low loads (prices near zero and below do not scale with anything), and everything the level leaves out (hour, season, neighbours, weather).
  The residual panels are drawn on one scale so that the size of what is left can be compared across dimensions by eye.
20.3 Levels 0 and 1: what the pictures show (out/zoom_01.png)
  Level 0. Price and gas-fired cost move together over all 32 months; the monthly median of price / L is 0.81-1.05 in the fitting period and
    0.88-1.16 in the held-out year in which gas went from 30 to over 100. One number, c = 0.96. It leaves out load: the ratio runs from zero
    below 5 GW to above 1.5 at 55 GW. LOCKED as the top of the map: the price is a multiple of gas-fired cost.
  Level 1. Sampling mixes (worst r-hat 1.002, smallest effective sample 1234 of 2400), which the detailed curve never did. k at -10..80 GW:
    -0.24 -0.04 0.08 0.71 0.96 1.13 1.36 1.72 3.79 4.03. Before data its priors give 0.46 / 1.09 / 1.89 at 30 GW (5 / 50 / 95%).
    The curve fitted on Mar 2024 - Nov 2025 (gas 28-55) lies through the held-out year (gas 30-105) with the same spread: 50% and 90% bands hold
    49% / 91% of hours in the fitting period and 48% / 89% in the held-out year; median absolute residual 0.120 and 0.124. It sits low
    there (mean residual +0.11, median +0.09 in ratio units; see 20.6, this is not small). LOCKED: the SHAPE, a rising function of residual
    load that survives a doubling of gas. The level is open.
    Where level 1 is visibly wrong: (i) one noise size: observed 5-95% width 0.79 0.69 0.87 0.69 0.54 0.81 1.65 4.44 by 10-GW band against 0.73
    for the model, too narrow at both ends and too wide in the middle; (ii) whole-day errors are 2.2 / 2.0 / 2.9 times what independent hours
    give (below 15 / 15-35 / above 35 GW); (iii) the top two knots (70, 80 GW) rest on a handful of hours.
    What it leaves out, range of the binned median residual, fitting period | held-out year, all on one scale:
       hour of day 0.29 | 0.13     month of the year 0.21 | 0.29     calendar month 0.26 | 0.29
       gas price 0.17 | 0.23       neighbours' residual load 0.14 | 0.14     temperature 0.21 | 0.23
    Reading of the panels: the hour of day has the largest and the most clearly shaped structure in the fitting period (morning and evening up,
    midday down; the evening bump is also there in the held-out year). Season and temperature come next and are the most alike across the two
    periods (winter low, summer high; about +0.01 per degree). Against gas the fitting period falls (short gas spikes are passed on less than
    one for one) while the held-out year does not. Neighbours are the smallest.
    NEXT (level 2): the daily cycle, as a shift of the load at which the hour is priced. Then season / temperature.
20.4 Level 2, written BEFORE fitting: price = L x k(residual load + shift[hour] + shift[day type]).
  Shifts in GW; hours sum to zero; priors N(0, 4 GW) per hour and per day type (Saturday, Sunday or holiday). Everything else as level 1.
  Expect, if right: the hour-of-day residual panel flattens in both periods; k hardly moves (the coarse picture stays); the shifts look like a
  daily cycle (evening and morning ramps priced as if load were higher, midday as if lower). If the hour effect is not a load shift (for instance
  a premium that does not go through the curve), the residual by hour will still show structure within load bands.
20.5 Level 2: what the pictures show (out/zoom_02.png)
  Sampling mixes (worst r-hat 1.002, smallest effective sample 1599 of 2400).
  Prior predictive: the hour shifts look like +-5..10 GW noise, except hour 23, which explodes. It is defined as minus the sum of the other 23,
    so its prior sd is 4 x sqrt(23) = 19 GW. A defect of the prior, found by drawing it; to be replaced by a centred set of 24 at the next step.
  The daily cycle it finds: morning ramp +3.9 GW (07 h), midday -4.7 GW, evening +7.8 GW (19-20 h); bands at most 1.5 GW wide. Saturday -0.5,
    Sunday or holiday -1.2 GW.
  The coarse picture stayed: k at 20..60 GW 0.78 0.96 1.12 1.35 1.60 against 0.71 0.96 1.13 1.36 1.72 at level 1. The top end, which rested on
    a few evening hours, came down from about 4 to about 2 once those hours are placed at a higher load.
  Fitting period: the hour-of-day residual flattens overall (range 0.29 -> 0.06) and at 20-40 GW (0.29 -> 0.08). It does NOT flatten below 20 GW
    (0.21 -> 0.25, new dips of -0.14 at the ramp hours) and only halves above 40 GW (0.44 -> 0.23, nights still -0.13). So the daily cycle is a
    shift of the load in the middle of the curve and something else at both ends.
  Held-out year: the hour-of-day residual does not flatten (0.13 -> 0.14). The cycle fitted on 2024-25 over-corrects 2026: midday is left
    +0.13 too high in the residual, the evening at zero. Coverage of the 50% / 90% bands 46% / 88% (fitting 49% / 91%); mean residual +0.10.
  Why (two further looks, level-1 residual at 20-45 GW):
    evening minus midday, by quarter:  2024  +0.25 +0.28 +0.24 +0.09   2025  +0.15 +0.34 +0.20 +0.06   2026  +0.12 +0.17 +0.12 +0.09
    In ratio units the cycle is larger in the summer half-year and smaller in 2026. CORRECTION after review: "about half in 2026" is an
    artefact of the unit. In money (residual x that hour's gas-fired cost) the same contrast is, by quarter,
       2024  +18 +23 +22 +10 EUR/MWh     2025  +18 +32 +18 +5     2026  +12 +20 +17 +17
    while gas-fired cost went 71 84 90 105 | 116 92 88 88 | 106 113 148 196. The cycle is closer to a fixed amount of money than to something
    that scales with cost. Level 2 writes it as a load shift inside a cost-scaled curve, so its effect grows with cost; that alone over-corrects
    2026. "Halved" is withdrawn.
    Against the hour's residual load minus the day's mean the residual rises steadily: slope per 10 GW +0.137 (Apr-Sep) and +0.085 (Oct-Mar)
    to November 2025, +0.067 and +0.041 after. One number does about what 23 clock shifts do (correlation with the residual 0.52 against 0.49
    in the fitting period, 0.38 against 0.37 after), and it halves in the held-out year just the same.
  Status: NOT locked as specified. What holds is that there is a daily cycle, that in the middle of the curve it acts as a shift of the load,
    and how large it was in 2024-25. What does not hold is that it is a fixed clock pattern: it scales with the season and has roughly halved.
    The unchanged parts: season / temperature is now the largest thing left (0.19 | 0.25 and 0.19 | 0.19), the calendar wander 0.25 | 0.25,
    whole-day errors 2.2 / 1.8 / 2.9 times independent hours.
  NEXT (revised after review; the earlier plan of a strength "allowed to change slowly over time" is dropped, it would have been the drift patch
    again): level 2 again with the daily cycle as an amount of money on top of the cost-scaled curve, not as a load shift inside it, and looked
    at the same way, including within load bands.
20.6 Level 1's level is OPEN (correction to 20.3, which called the held-out offset "a little low")
  Median level-1 residual by month of the year, one line per year, ratio units:
            Jan   Feb   Mar   Apr   May   Jun   Jul   Aug   Sep   Oct   Nov   Dec
     2024                -0.01  0.00 -0.02 -0.06 -0.05  0.00 +0.03 -0.14 -0.01 +0.01
     2025  -0.07 -0.14 -0.12 -0.03 +0.04 +0.04 +0.11 +0.10 +0.11 +0.11 +0.03 +0.06
     2026  +0.05  0.00 -0.09 -0.01 +0.08 +0.15 +0.17 +0.20 +0.14 +0.09 +0.09
  At the same month the ratio at a given load is higher each year: July -0.05 / +0.11 / +0.17, August 0.00 / +0.10 / +0.20, January -0.07 / +0.05.
  The rise starts inside the fitting period (mid-2025, with gas flat), so it is not about gas. In money it is not small: the held-out year's mean
  offset of +0.11 is 1.4 ct at that year's cost; 0.6 ct in Dec-Feb, 1.9 ct in Apr-Aug, 2.6 ct in Sep-Nov 2026.
  What is locked at level 1 is the SHAPE (a rising function of residual load that survives a doubling of gas). The LEVEL is open.
  Season is not established either: 2024 is flat through the year, 2025 and 2026 are high in summer, and with under three years a summer effect
  cannot be told from that year-on-year rise. So "season / temperature next" (20.3, 20.5) is withdrawn until the level is looked at.
  The statement made in passing that the cost-adjusted level is "flat to within 5% over all 18 months" holds for April 2025 - September 2026,
  the window of the fit it was measured against, not for 2024.
  Two limits on everything in this section: (i) the posterior bands (for instance hour shifts "at most 1.5 GW wide") come from a noise model with
  independent hours, while errors come as whole days, so they are too narrow by a factor of two or more; (ii) the number above each residual
  panel is not comparable across panels: hour bins pool hundreds of days, while gas, calendar-month and temperature bins hold a few runs of
  days. A noise envelope per panel (the residual series shifted by random whole weeks) is needed before the next dimension is chosen by eye.
  And the held-out year now informs every design choice here while containing all three forecast periods; it is no longer a test.
20.7 The CO2 weight (user: "ok but gas plus co2 credit prices?"), written BEFORE looking
  Gas-fired cost in levels 0-2 is 1.85 x gas + 0.37 x carbon: gas plus CO2 allowances for a combined-cycle plant. The 0.37 t/MWh is that
  plant's. Hard coal carries about 0.85 and lignite about 1.0 t/MWh, so where they set the price CO2 should count more than the levels assume,
  and carbon rose from about 60 to 85 EUR/t over the period. Could that be the open level of 20.6?
  Looks: (a) the two parts of gas-fired cost over time; (b) the level-1 residual against the carbon price, by load band, with a noise envelope
  (the residual series shifted against carbon by random whole weeks), since carbon moves slowly and a few runs of days fill each bin;
  (c) level 1 refitted with the CO2 weight free (prior centred on 0.37, wide enough to reach lignite's 1.0), same fitting period;
  (d) the residual by month of the year, one line per year, with the weight at 0.37 and at its fitted value.
  If CO2 is the open level: the residual rises with carbon, more at low and middle loads than at high ones; the fitted weight is clearly above
  0.37; and the year-on-year rise of (d) shrinks, including in the held-out year. If not: the winter of 2025 (carbon about 73, residual -0.1)
  and the summer of 2025 (carbon about 72, residual +0.1) stay apart whatever the weight.
20.8 The CO2 weight: what the pictures show (out/zoom_co2.png)
  Gas-fired cost is gas plus CO2 allowances; the CO2 part is 16-36% of it by month (largest when gas is cheap).
  Fitted CO2 weight (level 1 with the weight free; prior centred on 0.37, wide; sampling mixes, r-hat at most 1.006; bands too narrow because
  hours are treated as independent):
     fitting period, Mar 2024 - Nov 2025 (gas 28-55; carbon rises with the calendar, correlation +0.65)      1.63
     whole span                                                                                              0.52
     held-out year alone, Dec 2025 - Nov 2026 (gas 29-89; carbon with time +0.30)                            0.37
  Where gas moves enough to separate the two parts of cost, the weight is a gas plant's. In the fitting period, where carbon mostly rose with
  time, the free weight runs to 1.63, above any plant's emission factor: it is absorbing the level rise of 20.6. Carried to the held-out year
  that weight makes things worse (median absolute residual 0.124 -> 0.175, mean +0.11 -> +0.14).
  Residual by month, one line per year, July-September: -0.01 / +0.11 / +0.17 (2024 / 2025 / 2026) at weight 0.37; -0.01 / +0.07 / +0.26 at 1.63.
  Level-1 residual against the carbon price, by load band, against a noise envelope (the residual series shifted against carbon by random whole
  weeks; 5-95%): above 40 GW range 0.08 against an envelope width of 0.17; 20-40 GW 0.14 against 0.15; below 20 GW 0.31 against 0.21, rising to
  +0.26..+0.28 once carbon is above 80 EUR/t.
  Two checks after review.
  (i) What the held-out 0.37 measures. In that year carbon moved only 69-88 while gas moved 29-89, so weight x carbon is nearly a constant.
      Refitted with carbon replaced by its mean of the year: weight 0.33 (0.31..0.34), median absolute residual 0.0991 against 0.0966 with
      carbon as it was. So the fit is pinning the NON-GAS part of cost, 26-29 EUR/MWh, which is what a gas plant's CO2 comes to (0.37 x 79 =
      29); the price's response to CO2 itself cannot be isolated, because CO2 barely moved. The whole-span 0.52 is a mix of the two regimes,
      not a third estimate.
  (ii) The noise envelope first drawn shifted the residuals but not their load band. Shifted together: below 20 GW the observed range is 0.31
      against an envelope width of 0.27, with 4 of 9 bins outside; 20-40 GW 0.14 against 0.14, 3 of 9 outside; above 40 GW 0.08 against 0.23,
      none outside.
  Reading: a single constant CO2 weight cannot fit both 2025 and 2026 (0.37 leaves the year-on-year rise; 1.63 narrows 2024 to 2025 and widens
  2026). The cost scale of levels 0-2, gas plus a non-gas part the size of a gas plant's CO2, stands. Below 40 GW the residual sits higher at
  high carbon prices in some bins beyond what whole-week shifts give, most below 20 GW, where lignite and hard coal (about 1.0 and 0.85 t/MWh)
  would be setting the price; but carbon above 80 occurs only from late 2025, so it cannot be told from the rise over time. A candidate detail
  for a later level (a second cost scale at low load), not a finding.
  For reference: the detailed curve of the candidate has a gas term only; carbon sits inside its base curve and is not a separate input.


21. A GENERATIVE MODEL OF THE MARKET: SUPPLY MEETS DEMAND (user: "why don't we build a generative bayes model of the power market? eg start with
    very rough supply model, very rough demand model which meet and set price? and then step by step make supply and demand more detailed by
    adding in market structure?" and "definitely in supply meets demand should have latent merit order")
=====================================================================================================================================
21.0 What is different from the two earlier attempts (coupled market, sections 1-8; offer curve married to a demand proxy, sections 9-14)
  Those started detailed (nine zones, plant blocks, exchange bounds) and were judged by forecast scores. Here: the roughest market that can
  clear, built up one piece at a time; every level generates PRICES AND QUANTITIES and is looked at in both; the randomness is in explicit
  shocks to supply and demand in GW; one likelihood derived from those shocks; simulate-and-recover before any real fit; decisions from pictures.
  The candidate of section 17 stays the forecaster while this is built. The levels of section 20 are the same market seen without quantities:
  level 1 is "one supply curve scaled by gas-plus-CO2 cost meets demand that does not respond".
21.1 The energy balance (checked before any model; exp output in out/market_hourly.parquet, hourly, Germany)
  realised residual load = thermal (lignite + hard coal + gas) + other plant + pumped storage (generation - pumping) + net imports + gap.
  With trade counted as a source (+ = net import) the gap is +2.9 GW, sd 1.9 (by year 3.0 / 3.6 / 2.1); with the other sign it does not close
  (sd 13). Sizes, mean and sd in GW: residual load 29.2 / 14.8; thermal 16.2 / 7.9 (lignite 7.8 / 3.0, hard coal 3.0 / 1.9, gas 5.5 / 3.7);
  other 8.2 / 0.8; storage -0.3 / 2.5; net imports 2.2 / 6.3. Net imports move with residual load (correlation 0.75), so thermal output moves
  about half as much as residual load does. Prices <= 0 in 7.0% of hours, <= 5 EUR/MWh in 9.7%; thermal output is then still 5.9 GW on average.
  The outputs are realised, so the balance used is the realised one (realised residual load); the price is the day-ahead price.
21.2 Level M0, the roughest market that clears. Written BEFORE fitting.
  Supply, two curves, both in the price relative to gas-plus-CO2 cost r = p / L:
     domestic thermal   T = F_T + K_T Phi((r - m_T) / s_T) + e_T          a must-run floor, then capacity coming on as r passes plants' thresholds
     net imports        X = X_lo + K_X Phi((r - m_X) / s_X) + e_X         from maximum export to maximum import as the German price rises
  Demand for those two:
     N = residual load - B + A(p) + d,   A(p) = K_0 Phi((c_0 - p) / s_0)   B: other plant and the gap, a constant; A: what absorbs surplus when
                                                                           the price nears zero (curtailment, extra consumption), in EUR terms
  Clearing: T + X = N. Three shocks in GW (d, e_T, e_X), Student-t with 4 degrees of freedom, three observables (price, thermal output, net
  imports). The map is one to one, so the likelihood is the three shock densities at
     e_T = T - g_T(p),  e_X = X - g_X(p),  d = T + X - (residual load - B) - A(p)
  times the slope of excess supply at the observed price, g_T' + g_X' - A'. The price is counted once.
  Storage is inside d at this level. Hours are treated as independent (bands too narrow, as before).
  Priors (none truncated): F_T logN(4, 0.4) GW; K_T logN(35, 0.3); m_T N(1.0, 0.3); s_T logN(0.4, 0.5); X_lo N(-12, 4); K_X logN(25, 0.3);
  m_X N(1.0, 0.4); s_X logN(0.5, 0.5); K_0 logN(8, 0.5); c_0 N(0, 10) EUR/MWh; s_0 logN(15, 0.5); B N(11, 3); shock scales half-normal(5 GW).
  Fitting period and held-out year as in section 20. 2021-2023 (out/hist2123.parquet: price, load, output by fuel, storage, trade, gas, coal,
  CO2, gas from 18 to 227) is RESERVED as the test of the form and is not looked at while designing.
  Order of work: prior predictive (simulated markets); simulate from known parameters and recover them; real fit (must mix); the two supply
  curves against the (price, quantity) clouds; the implied price-against-load curve laid over level 1's; replicated against observed; shocks
  against what is left out, with the corrected noise envelopes.
  Expected misfits, known in advance (seeing them confirms the level and names the next ones):
   - prices will be reproduced worse than by level 1: imports depend on the neighbours, which are a shock here (thermal output was a worse axis
     than regional residual load, 1.67-2.02 against 1.37 ct);
   - the must-run floor moves with temperature (heat-led plant), so e_T will follow temperature;
   - plants follow the day's mean price more than the hour's, so e_T will show an hour-of-day pattern;
   - storage inside d will show as a daily cycle in d;
   - lignite and hard coal were not a stable function of cost-adjusted price in Aug-Nov 2026 while gas was, so e_T will drift there.
21.3 Level M1 (next, not built here): the domestic curve split into a merit order of blocks labelled by fuel (lignite, hard coal, gas), each with
  its own cost (heat rates and emission factors FIXED at physical values: 1.0 t/MWh and no gas for lignite, 0.85 for hard coal, 1.85 gas and
  0.37 t for gas plant), and latent capacity, cost offset, spread and floor. The order is latent in the sense that offers are not observed; it
  follows from the costs and can flip when gas and CO2 move. Unlabelled blocks were not identified in section 8, and a free CO2 loading ran off
  in 20.8, hence the labels and the fixed loadings. Gas output never exceeds 17.6 GW, so gas capacity above that comes from its prior.
21.4 M0 fails both checks made before real data; revised to M0b (still before any real fit)
  Prior predictive (200 simulated markets on 3000 real hours of load and cost): median price / cost 0.45 / 0.98 / 1.48 (5 / 50 / 95% over markets;
  observed 0.96), share of hours at or below zero 0.02 / 0.07 / 0.18 (observed 0.07), mean thermal output 12 / 20 / 28 GW (observed 16). But the
  highest price is at the search limit (600 ct) in almost every market: with finite capacity on both supply curves there are hours in which
  no price clears. The draft has no top.
  Simulate and recover (known parameters, 6699 hours, 5% missing plus eight gaps): the sampler does not mix (worst r-hat 12.3, effective sample
  2). The domestic curve is recovered (floor 4.94 for 5.0, capacity 29.99 for 30, threshold 1.10, spread 0.35), the import curve is not
  (X_lo -44..-10 for -10, K_X 22..61 for 22, s_X 0.6..81 for 0.6): over the range of prices seen, a four-parameter step is a line, and only its
  level and slope are pinned. Absorption and B come out biased (K_0 9.6 for 6, c_0 -10 for -5).
  M0b: net imports as a line in the cost-relative price, X = x0 + x1 (r - 1) + e_X, x0 ~ N(2, 4) GW (net imports when the price equals
  gas-plus-CO2 cost), x1 ~ logN(8, 0.6) GW per unit of r. Two parameters where two are identified, and an elastic backstop: the market always
  clears. Limits on exchange come back later as market structure, with the neighbours. Everything else as 21.2.
21.5 M0b passes both pre-data checks, then fails on real data, and the failure is a piece of market structure
  M0b before data: simulated markets have a median price / cost of 0.43 / 0.88 / 1.42, 0 / 10 / 24% of hours at or below zero, peaks of 26 / 84 /
  329 ct; known parameters are recovered (worst r-hat 1.001, effective sample 1677; 12 of 13 inside their 90% bands).
  Real fit (15359 hours): does not mix (worst r-hat 8.1). The chains drive the width of the absorption curve towards zero (s_0 2.3, one chain
  0.08 EUR/MWh): a step at a price of zero, where the likelihood is unbounded. The data: 2.74% of hours within 0.1 EUR/MWh of zero, against 0.58%
  in (0.1, 1] and 0.97% in (-1, -0.1]; among quarter-hours 986 at exactly 0.00, 617 at -0.01. Prices pile up at zero. A market with a smooth
  excess-supply curve cannot produce a pile-up at one price; a supply block that withdraws at exactly zero does.
  Also seen: thermal + imports - residual load is -11.6 GW (sd 4.0) above 20 EUR/MWh, -6.4 at 0.1-20, -5.5 at zero, -4.3 below zero: about 5 GW
  is absorbed already at low positive prices and about 7 below zero.
  M0c: as M0b, plus a shelf: K_s GW of supply (renewables that switch off) withdraw at a price of zero. Hours within 0.1 EUR/MWh of zero are
  "the market cleared on the shelf": for them the price is not a continuous outcome, the withdrawn amount is unknown between 0 and K_s and is
  integrated out, so their likelihood is the probability that the demand shock falls in that range (times the two supply-shock densities, no
  slope term). Below the shelf the full K_s is withdrawn. The demand shock becomes logistic (a closed-form distribution function is needed);
  the supply shocks stay Student-t. K_s ~ logN(3, 0.6) GW. Both pre-data checks are rerun.
21.6 M0c: what the pictures show (out/market0_before.png, out/market0_data.png)
  Before data. Simulated markets look like a power market: a rising cloud of price / cost against residual load, some hours at zero. The real
  market sits inside the simulated ones (share of hours at or below zero at their 37th percentile, on the shelf 64th, median price / cost 60th,
  mean thermal output 22nd). Peaks 24 / 66 / 275 ct (5 / 50 / 95% over markets; observed 94). Known parameters are recovered (worst r-hat 1.001;
  13 of 14 inside their 90% bands).
  Real fit (15359 hours, Mar 2024 - Nov 2025): mixes (worst r-hat 1.001, effective sample 906 of 2400, no divergences).
     domestic thermal   floor 3.6 GW, then 30.6 GW coming on around price = 1.08 x cost, spread 0.53
     net imports        +4.6 GW at price = cost, 10.9 GW more per unit of price / cost
     shelf at zero      2.6 GW withdraw at a price of zero
     rest of system     B 18.0 GW; the "absorption" curve came out as 10.4 GW centred at 102 EUR/MWh with a width of 32 (its prior put it
                        near zero): the fit uses it for everything else that responds to price (pumped storage, other plant, the gap), not
                        for surplus near zero
     shocks (sd)        demand 2.5 GW, thermal 5.2 GW, imports 3.8 GW
  Against quantities and prices:
   - both supply curves pass through their clouds, and the held-out year's points lie on the same curves in cost-relative terms;
   - the rest-of-system curve is in EUR and the held-out year sits off it (prices doubled, the response did not move with them);
   - the price implied with all shocks at zero lies on level 1's curve from 15 to 60 GW at both cost levels, and apart below 10 GW, where the
     data sit on a shelf about 10 GW of residual load wide and the model's shelf is 2.6 GW;
   - simulated against observed by load band (median absolute gap of the five quantiles): prices 0.05 in ratio units, thermal output 1.4 GW,
     net imports 0.9 GW; hours on the shelf 3.2% observed against 2.0% simulated, below zero 5.0% against 5.5%;
   - for reference only: price with all shocks at zero, MAE 1.67 ct in the fitting period and 3.23 in the held-out year (level 1: 1.64 / 2.27).
  Shocks that are not shocks (median by bin against the envelope from whole-week shifts; all four were expected in 21.2):
     thermal shock against temperature                     +9 GW at 0 C to -3.5 GW at 16-20 C; range 13 GW; 4 of 6 bins outside
     thermal shock against the hour's price minus the day's mean   flat at +2 GW, then down to -8 GW in hours priced well above the day; 7 of 10 outside
     import shock against the neighbours' residual load    +2 GW to -4 GW; range 6 GW; 7 of 9 outside
     demand shock against the hour of day                  +1.5 GW at night, -1.3 GW in the evening; range 3 GW; 9 of 24 outside
  Reading: M0c is a market that clears, generates prices and two quantities, can be simulated and recovered, and lands on level 1 where level 1
  was locked. It is rough in exactly the named ways. In GW the largest things it leaves out are, in order: thermal output that depends on
  temperature (heat-led plant), thermal plant that follows the day's price and not the hour's, imports that depend on the neighbours, and
  storage. The merit order by fuel (M1) comes next because it is what lets the first two be attributed to the plant that causes them.
  Not locked yet: the rest-of-system curve (wrong units), the width of the shelf.

21.7 M0c after review: one statement withdrawn, and the open level gets an address (out/market0_rest.png, out/market0_level.png;
     exp/market0_rest.py, exp/market0_level.py)
  WITHDRAWN from 21.6: "the held-out year lies on both supply curves in cost-relative terms". It was read off 20-GW-wide clouds. Binned
  medians with bands from resampling whole weeks say otherwise for imports: mean import shock -0.23 GW in the fitting period, -3.61 GW in the
  held-out year, below the curve in every load band from 5 to 55 GW. Thermal: -0.18 / +1.20 GW. Third shock: -0.01 / +3.83 GW.
  Also to be said plainly about 21.5-21.6:
   - "passes its pre-data checks" holds for the model as specified. The data then put the centre of the price-responsive rest about ten prior
     sds from its prior (Normal(0, 10) -> 102 EUR/MWh) and B more than two (11 +- 3 -> 18). That is a conflict between prior and data, the
     fitted component is not the one that was checked, and recovery was never tested in that region.
   - "on level 1 from 15 to 60 GW" is the fitting period. Held-out year, price with shocks at zero: MAE 3.23 ct against level 1's 2.27.
   - demand at this level is the realised residual load, taken as given, inelastic except for the shelf, with no shock of its own. The third
     shock is the residual of the rest of the system (storage, other plant, the unmeasured gap), not a demand shock.
   - 2021-2023: prices against gas were already used (pass-through slopes, section 16-19). What is unseen there is the quantities and the
     form of the market model.
  The rest of the system is observed (realised residual load - thermal - net imports), so the question "does it transfer in EUR or in
  price / cost" has a picture (market0_rest.png). Held-out minus fitting period, binned medians: -4.25 GW in EUR, -2.87 GW in price / cost;
  bands apart in 11 of 12 and 10 of 11 bins. Neither transfers as a whole. Split into its parts, in price / cost:
     pumped storage   the two periods coincide (4 of 11 bins apart, -0.4 GW); in EUR they do not (-1.3 GW). Storage belongs in price / cost.
     other plant      a level shift of -0.8 GW at every price; no change of shape.
     balance gap      -1.85 GW, all of it below price = cost: a floor of 2.5-3 GW in the fitting period, none in the held-out year.
  Year by year, months March to October only (all three years have them; no model involved; market0_level.png, top row):
     net imports at the same price / cost            2024 -> 2025: -2.1 GW;  2025 -> 2026: -2.6 GW   (below price / cost of about 1.3; the
                                                     years meet at 9-10 GW above 1.5)
     thermal output + balance gap                    +0.0 GW; -1.3 GW (2026 lower below price / cost 0.5, the same above)
     other plant                                     -1.1 GW; -0.4 GW
     pumped storage                                  -0.2 GW; -0.1 GW (one curve)
  By month: the import shock is lower than a year before in 7 of 10 shared months in 2025 and 10 of 10 in 2026. The balance gap fell by
  2.2 GW from April 2026 while measured thermal output rose by 2.5 GW against its curve over the same months (gas output in the summer of
  2026 is 1.2 GW above the two summers before although gas cost more). The sum barely moved. Reading, not established: plant that used to be
  unmeasured is reported from April 2026. Consequence for M1 whatever the cause: model thermal + gap together, or the split by fuel inherits
  a break in April 2026.
  Candidate cause on the import side, looked at, not established: neighbours' residual load did not rise (81.0, 78.5, 78.8 GW) and the
  years stay apart at the same neighbours' residual load; French hydro output fell 7.0 -> 5.1 -> 4.7 GW (the size of the first step only);
  neighbours' mean price relative to German cost rose 0.79 -> 0.85 -> 0.86 (France 0.52 -> 0.52 -> 0.68, Switzerland 0.73 -> 0.96 -> 0.97).
  What the import shift does to the price (accounting inside the model, not a test: the shocks are residuals given the price; 2024 and 2025
  are in the fitting period): observed minus model, median price / cost, March to October:
                                         2024     2025     2026     rise 2024 -> 2026
     model, no shocks                   -0.05    +0.07    +0.23     0.28
     imports shifted by the year's mean  0.00    +0.06    +0.15     0.15
     and other plant                    +0.01    +0.05    +0.12     0.11
  So about half of the two-year rise in price relative to cost that level 1 could only carry as an offset is located, in this model, in net
  imports (+1.8, -0.4, -2.6 GW against the curve), a further 0.04 in other plant, and 0.11 is not located. Part of what is left in 2026 is
  the model's own defect (rest-of-system curve in EUR: at 2026's prices it puts the rest at its maximum).
  Why this matters for the design: the level that every earlier model had to carry in a parameter fitted to prices (weekly scale drift in
  the candidate, offset in level 1) is here partly a QUANTITY in GW that is observed directly, a day late, moves slowly, and reaches the
  price through market clearing. That is the user's rule (level through functions of parameters, not through parameters).
  Decisions for the next level, from these pictures:
   - storage becomes its own block, in price / cost;
   - other plant is a level with a slow drift, no curve needed at this zoom;
   - the balance gap is modelled together with thermal output;
   - the import curve's level is a slowly moving quantity, not a constant; its shape (no shift above price / cost 1.5) is not a parallel
     shift, so "x0 drifts" is the rough version and is expected to misfit at the top.

21.8 Level M1, the merit order by fuel. Written BEFORE any M1 fit (exp/market1.py). Supersedes the sketch in 21.3 where they differ.
  What is added to M0c: the single domestic curve becomes three blocks labelled by fuel, each with its own cost; the rest of the system is no
  longer one residual curve in EUR: storage is its own observed block in price / cost, other plant is taken as given, and the unmeasured
  part of the balance closes the system. Nothing else changes (imports, shelf as M0c).
  Given each hour (not modelled): D = realised residual load - other plant; gas price; CO2 price; L = 1.85 gas + 0.37 CO2.
  Blocks, f in {lignite, hard coal, gas}:
     cost            c_lignite = a_l + 1.00 CO2      (emission factor fixed; a_l = fuel and running cost, latent, constant)
                     c_coal    = a_c + 0.85 CO2      (emission factor fixed; a_c latent, constant: THERE IS NO COAL PRICE SERIES HERE. Named
                                                      omission; harmless only while coal prices are calm; to be fixed before the 2021-23 test)
                     c_gas     = m_g L               (heat rate and emission factor of a modern plant fixed in L; m_g = where the gas fleet's
                                                      thresholds are centred relative to that plant, latent)
     curve           g_f(p) = C_f [ phi_f + (1 - phi_f) kap_f Phi( (p / c_f - 1) / s_f ) ]
                     C_f installed capacity, FIXED from the plant register and rounded (lignite 18, hard coal 12, gas 33 GW);
                     phi_f the must-run share, kap_f the share of the remainder that ever comes on, s_f the spread of thresholds relative to cost.
     shock           on the logit of utilisation: logit(q_f / C_f) = logit(g_f / C_f) + e_f, e_f ~ Student-t(4) scale sg_f.
                     Why this form, decided from the data's look and from support, not from a score: at a given price the spread of each fuel's
                     output grows with its level (sd in GW: lignite 0.7 -> 2.4, coal 0.5 -> 2.0, gas 0.5 -> 4.5 from the lowest to the middle
                     price bands; sd of log output roughly constant at 0.2-0.36, 0.6-0.8, 0.3-0.57) and narrows again near the top. An additive
                     shock in GW gives negative output (checked in the prior predictive, see below); a shock on log output ignores the ceiling
                     (coal: 5% of hours above 3.2 x the curve). The logit keeps output inside (0, C_f), which is its physical support.
  Imports (as M0c): X = x0 + x1 (p / L - 1) + e_X, additive Student-t. KNOWN to be wrong in level (21.7); not addressed at this level.
  Storage: S = K_st [ 2 Phi( (p / L - m_st) / s_st ) - 1 ] + e_S, additive Student-t (signed quantity).
  Unmeasured part: gap = B_g + eps, eps logistic; it is the block whose quantity is implied by the balance, so it closes the system.
  Shelf (as M0c): K_s GW of supply withdrawn at a price of zero.
  Clearing: lignite + coal + gas + imports + storage + gap - withdrawal = D. Six shocks, six observables (three fuel outputs, imports,
  storage, price). Likelihood = shock densities x slope of realised excess supply at the observed price; shelf hours get a probability mass.
  Priors (from what the things are, not from M0c's posterior): phi_f ~ Beta(2, 12); kap_f ~ Beta(4, 2); a_l ~ Normal(15, 8) EUR/MWh;
  a_c ~ Normal(35, 12); m_g ~ LogNormal(log 1.3, 0.3); s_f ~ LogNormal(log 0.3, 0.5); sg_f ~ HalfNormal(1); x0 ~ Normal(2, 4);
  x1 ~ LogNormal(log 8, 0.6); K_st ~ LogNormal(log 5, 0.3); m_st ~ Normal(1, 0.3); s_st ~ LogNormal(log 0.4, 0.5); B_g ~ Normal(3, 2) (the
  balance gap averages 2.9 GW, 21.1); K_s ~ LogNormal(log 3, 0.6); shock scales HalfNormal(5) (imports), HalfNormal(3) (storage, gap).
  Sequence, unchanged: prior predictive; simulate from known parameters and recover; real fit must mix; pictures in prices AND quantities;
  shocks against what is left out, with envelopes from whole-week shifts; held-out year as binned medians with week-resampled bands (the
  lesson of 21.7: a cloud hides a 3.6 GW shift).
  EXPECTED, written before the fit. M1 is judged on these pictures, not on a price error:
   E1 temperature pattern (thermal shock +9 GW at 0 C to -3.5 GW at 16-20 C in M0c): should sit mostly in GAS (heat-led plant), partly in
      hard coal, little in lignite.
   E2 day's-price pattern (plant follows the day's price, not the hour's): should sit mostly in LIGNITE, then hard coal, least in gas.
   E3 the test of the form inside this data: in the held-out year gas costs 30-130% more and CO2 about the same, so lignite and coal should
      run more at the same price / L. M0c's single curve showed this as a held-out thermal shock of +1.2 GW. If each fuel's held-out output
      sits on its own curve in p / c_f, the split explains it. If lignite and coal sit above their curves anyway, it does not.
   E4 gas above 17.6 GW has never been seen: kap_g, m_g and s_g should come back correlated and wide; recovery of kap_g should be poor.
   E5 imports: no better than M0c (shift of -2 to -2.6 GW a year). Unmeasured part: a break in April 2026 (-2.2 GW), mirrored in gas.
   E6 hours on the shelf: still too few (2.0% simulated against 3.2% in M0c); the shelf is unchanged.
   E7 prior-data conflict is to be reported for every parameter (posterior location in prior sds), after the lesson of c_0 in M0c.

21.9 The user's question: "is it sensible to be building a full year model for a forecasting task that cares about next 7 days, and with strictly
     decreasing priority as timeframe goes out?" -- and: "15min slot accuracy is what matters" -- and: "it's not actually clear to me that a good
     market model isn't the right route to getting the near term forecast nailed down. near term just means more data less extrapolation...?"
     (exp/market0_horizon.py, market0_innov.py, slot15_check.py; out/market0_horizon.png, market0_innov.png). M1 (21.8) is specified, NOT built.
  All of this uses realised residual load and fuel prices of the target hour. It isolates the price model's state; it is not a forecast test.
  (a) The model's shocks are slow. Correlation of daily means 1 / 7 / 14 days apart: thermal 0.90 / 0.82 / 0.76, third shock 0.91 / 0.81 / 0.80,
      imports 0.71 / 0.50 / 0.49, price error of the bare model 0.67 / 0.52 / 0.48. Share of hourly variance that is the day's mean: 0.64, 0.69, 0.54.
  (b) Carrying the state (mean of the last 7 days by hour of day, known at a 14:00 origin) and clearing again, hourly price MAE in ct:
                                   no state   +1 day   +2     +4     +7 days
        fitting period, shocks in GW   1.68    1.42    1.45   1.47   1.50      (price error carried instead: 1.53 ... 1.60)
        held-out year,  shocks in GW   3.23    1.78    1.82   1.87   1.91      (price error carried instead: 1.97 ... 2.12)
      One day of state is too noisy (worse than none in the fitting period). The state in GW beats carrying the price error by about a tenth
      over all hours and not at all at night. With realised load the error grows 7% from day 1 to day 7: the price model's state is not what
      makes far days hard; the weather is. By load (held-out, +2 days): the state helps below 40 GW and does nothing above 45 GW.
  (c) What the state does NOT know (errors +2 days after the state, against changes relative to the state window; envelope from whole-week
      shifts). Range of binned medians, price error in price / cost units (sd of the hourly error 0.26):
        residual load (level) 0.18; change in residual load 0.14; hour's price minus day's mean 0.13; change in gas-plus-CO2 cost 0.09;
        change in temperature 0.04; neighbours' residual load 0.02; day of week 0.02; hour of day 0.01.
      In GW: imports against the change in residual load 5.1 (-2.1 GW at -30 GW of load, +3.0 GW at +30 GW: imports follow load more than the
      price curve allows, and the model then overstates the price move by up to 0.10); thermal against hour's price minus day's mean 3.4
      (12.6 before the state); thermal against change in temperature 1.9 (the level picture in 21.6 had 13 GW).
      So the order of what is worth modelling changes once the state is carried. Temperature-led plant, the largest omission in the yearly
      picture, is worth 0.04 for a forecast. What remains is the response to a change in load, the shape of the day, and the pass-through of a
      week's change in fuel cost (model overstates it: -0.03 at +14%, +0.06 at -14%). The merit order (M1) bears on the first and third.
  (d) Quarter-hour slots. The day-ahead price is hourly until September 2025 (within-hour deviation exactly zero) and quarter-hourly from
      October 2025. Mean |slot price - hourly mean|: 0.45-0.47 ct Dec-Feb, 0.75-1.06 ct Mar-Oct. On the candidate's target slots the floor of
      ANY hourly forecast is 0.39-0.49 ct (night) and 0.43-0.71 ct (first 14 h). The market model so far is hourly; all of sections 20-21 are.
      M0c + 7-day state, D+2 from the candidate's origins, scored on slots, realised load (candidate with forecast load in brackets):
                    night                                  first 14 h
        winter   1.33 hourly repeated, 1.29 per quarter [0.67]     1.35, 1.31 [0.88]
        summer   1.33, 1.29 [0.79]                                 1.85, 1.76 [1.28]
        autumn   2.54, 2.50 [1.31]                                 2.74, 2.66 [1.63]
      The market model at this level is 50-100% behind the candidate on slots even with the load given. The candidate stays the forecaster.
      Clearing each quarter on the same curves (quarter-hour residual load, the hour's state) gets the direction of the within-hour price right
      (correlation 0.70 with the observed deviation from the hourly mean) and about half its size (observed sd 1.41 ct, model 0.52 ct; slope of
      observed on model 1.9). Reading, to be checked in quantities: within the hour less supply can follow than from hour to hour.
  Answer recorded: the long window is for shapes; the forecast is the same model's prediction CONDITIONAL on the latest observed quantities.
  Near term = more of the state is pinned by data. That needs (1) shocks with memory inside the model, so that conditioning is the model's own
  predictive and the curves are estimated from what changes within weeks, and (2) the model on quarter-hours. Judged from now on by what
  remains after the state, on slots. These two come before further structural detail; the merit order follows and is judged the same way.

21.10 After review of 21.9: corrections, the units fix on its own (M0d), and a reduced-form baseline (exp/market0d.py, market_horizon.py,
      market0d_fig.py; out/market0d_post.npz, market0d_rows.parquet, market0d_where.png). User, on reading 21.9: "there's also nothing illicit
      about carry forward fitting error terms, particularly when they are accurately structurally located (not spongy). also i definitely want
      quarter-hourly predictions where possible".
  CORRECTIONS to what 21.9 said and to what the user was told:
   - "3.23 -> 1.82 ct" overstated what the state is worth. Most of that gain came from summer and autumn 2026, where cost ran 25-130% above the
     fitting median and the rest-of-system curve in EUR is wrong (21.7); a carried state absorbs exactly that kind of defect (the spongy kind).
     With the units fixed the bare model is 2.67 and the state takes it to 1.88.
   - the state result shows that CONDITIONING helps; it does not show that market structure helps. At night, shocks in GW against carried price
     error: 1.40 vs 1.41 (M0c, held-out). See the baseline below.
   - "the weather forecast is what makes far days hard" was not measured. Measured: with the true load given, the price model's error grows 7%
     from day 1 to day 7.
   - "published a day late": the scripts assume quantities up to 12:00 of the origin day. The real lag of the feed has not been checked.
   - within-hour swing: observed sd 1.41 ct, the hourly curves cleared per quarter give 0.52 ct. That is nearer a third than "half".
   - "temperature is worth 0.04": bins stop at +-4.5 C of change against last week; cold snaps are not in it.
   - quarter-hours: with the true within-hour shape GIVEN, the present forecasts improve by about 0.1 ct at night and 0.1-0.2 ct on the first
     14 h (hourly-scored against slot-scored), against a gap to the candidate of 0.6-1.4 ct. Slots are what is scored, always; the within-hour
     structure is built, but it is not where the gap is. Two traps recorded: before October 2025 the price is one number per hour, so the
     likelihood stays hourly there; and all within-hour information lies in the scoring year, so that piece is fitted only on data before each
     origin.
  M0d = M0c with the units fixed and nothing else: storage its own observed block in price / cost (K_st [2 Phi((p/L - m_st)/s_st) - 1],
  additive Student-t shock), other plant taken as given (D = realised residual load - other plant), the unmeasured part closing the system
  (B_g + logistic shock), shelf as before. 15 parameters. No fuel split.

     lies between the 10th and 75th percentile of its prior (M0c had c_0 ten sds out). F_T 3.91, K_T 30.3, m_T 1.09, s_T 0.50, x0 4.62,

21.11 Memory in the shocks (M0m = M0d + memory, exp/market0m.py). Written BEFORE the fit.
  Look at the part first (M0d shocks, fitting period): every block's shock is slow and smooth. Autocorrelation at 1 h / 24 h / 168 h: thermal
  0.92 / 0.81 / 0.78, imports 0.88 / 0.55 / 0.39, storage 0.87 / 0.56 / 0.34, unmeasured 0.89 / 0.44 / 0.33. Two simple pieces take most of it
  out: a level per hour of day that is updated once a day with a gain of about 0.2 (sd 6.3 -> 3.3 GW thermal, 5.1 -> 4.1 imports, 1.8 -> 1.5
  storage, 1.6 -> 1.4 unmeasured), and dependence on the hour before of about 0.85 (-> 1.7, 2.1, 0.8, 0.7 GW). What then remains has
  autocorrelation of at most 0.2 at 1 h and 0.1 at 24 h and 168 h.
  Model, per block k: shock_k(day d, hour h) = rho_k m_k(d, h) + nu;  nu(t) = phi_k nu(t - 1) + omega(t);  m_k(d + 1, h) = rho_k m_k(d, h) + kap_k nu(d, h).
  omega is the independent part (Student-t for thermal, imports, storage; logistic for the closing block). This is a generative process (a level
  that reverts with rho, learns with kap, and an hour-to-hour term), and its likelihood is sequential: each hour given the past. The slope term
  and the shelf are unchanged. Priors: rho ~ Beta(8, 2), kap ~ Beta(2, 6), phi ~ Beta(6, 2) (shares; their support is (0, 1) by meaning).
  A forecast two days ahead uses rho^3 m (the hour-to-hour term has died out); near term = more of the level is known.
  EXPECTED before the fit (the likelihood now weighs changes within days and weeks, not levels across seasons):
   - import slope x1 (11.0): UP. After the state, imports followed load changes more than the curve allowed (+3 GW at +30 GW).
   - thermal slope (K_T / s_T): DOWN. Thermal plant follows the day's price more than the hour's (21.6), so hour-to-hour it responds less.
   - storage: steeper (s_st down); it trades the hour against the day.
   - these pull the model's price response to load in opposite directions; if the fit cannot have both, that is the shape-of-the-day structure
     announcing itself, and it will show as thermal's remaining shock against the hour's price minus the day's mean.
  ACCEPTANCE, in pictures, not a price error: (1) spread of the daily-mean price error, simulated against observed (the standing defect: whole
  days were 2-3 times too variable for independent hours); (2) a strip of weeks, simulated against real; (3) autocorrelation of what remains at
  1 h and 24 h; (4) recovery of rho, kap, phi from simulated data.

21.12 Memory: results. NOT LOCKED. (exp/market0m.py, NOPHI=1 for the second variant; market0m_fig.py; out/market0m_memory.png)
  Prior predictive (100 markets on 182 real days; 5-50-95%; observed): sd of the day's mean price / cost 0.21 0.50 2.1 (0.40); day-to-day
  correlation of the day's mean 0.32 0.53 0.72 (0.58); sd of the hour-to-hour change 0.14 0.46 1.5 (0.27). The real market is inside; the
  upper end of the prior is wild.
  (A) Full memory (level by hour of day + dependence on the hour before). Recovery: r-hat 1.003, 22 of 27 inside their 90% bands; the five
      outside are the shelf (2.79 for 3.0) and the closing block's memory (rho 0.93 for 0.90, kap 0.12 for 0.15), which use a midpoint
      shortcut on shelf hours, plus s_st and sd_X by a hair. Real fit: mixes (r-hat 1.001, no divergences) and is REJECTED:
      phi_T 0.99, floor F_T 15.7 GW (3.9 without memory), responsive capacity K_T 6.1 GW (30.3): prior and data in conflict on all three.
      With thermal output explained by the hour before, what is left for the curve is the hour-to-hour response, and that is small:
      8.7 GW per unit of price / cost at the centre, against 24 without memory. The day-ahead price then falls apart (hourly MAE without
      state 3.26 ct fitting / 6.74 held-out; two days ahead 2.40 / 4.13).
      What it shows: thermal output answers the hour's price about a third as strongly as it answers slower moves. A likelihood that
      predicts each hour from the one before fits the fast response and the model has no slow one to put elsewhere. This is the "plant
      follows the day's price" misfit of 21.6 (12.6 GW), and it has to be in the model before hour-to-hour memory can be.
  (B) Memory from day to day only (phi = 0). Recovery: r-hat 1.002, 22 of 23 inside (first run of this check was botched: it simulated
      with hour-to-hour dependence and fitted without; kept as out/market0m0_recover_hourdep.log because it shows what the real data do to
      this variant: curves roughly right, shock scales doubled, bands far too narrow). Real fit: r-hat 1.002, no conflict with priors.
      rho 0.99 / 0.96 / 0.99 / 0.93 and kap 0.26 / 0.18 / 0.16 / 0.21 (thermal, imports, storage, unmeasured).
      EXPECTATIONS of 21.11 against the fit: import slope UP: yes, 10.96 -> 13.08. Thermal slope DOWN: no, 24 -> 26 (it is down only
      hour to hour, see A). Storage steeper: no, the opposite (9.4 -> 4.7 GW per unit; the level by hour of day takes the regular daily
      cycle and little price response is left). One of three right.
      ACCEPTANCE: (1) spread of the day's mean price error, simulated / observed: 0.085 / 0.165 = 52% with independent hours, 0.071 / 0.166
      = 42% with day-to-day memory. FAILS, and no better than before: the levels are separate per hour of day in the model and move
      together in reality. (2) strip: same range as the real market, visibly rougher from hour to hour and too many dips below zero.
      (3) what remains: autocorrelation 0.80-0.87 at 1 h (by construction), 0.16-0.18 at 24 h. FAILS. (4) recovery: passes.
      Price two days ahead (true load), hourly MAE ct, fitting / held-out: own memory 1.47 / 1.96 (night 1.16 / 1.58); last 7 days carried
      on M0d 1.50 / 1.88 (1.15 / 1.48); plain curve with price error carried 1.47 / 1.93 (1.16 / 1.53). The model's own memory is worth
      nothing over simply carrying the last week. (Not the same information: the carried state uses the origin day's morning.)
  Reading. Both failures have one cause: within a day the hours are not separate. Shocks share a level across the hours of a day, and
  thermal plant is committed against the day's price. The model needs the day as a unit: a shock level common to the day, and a thermal
  block that responds to the day's price with only a share following the hour. The day's mean price then couples the 24 hours, and the
  likelihood's slope term becomes a determinant per day (diagonal plus rank one: tractable). That is the next level, and it is also where
  the remaining price error after the state pointed (hour's price minus day's mean, 0.13; response to load change, 0.14-0.18).
  Until then the forecaster-in-waiting is M0d with the last week carried, and it is level with the plain curve and 75-110% behind the
  candidate on the target slots (21.10).

   - own memory against the carried week, like for like (carried week restricted to data up to the day before the origin, as the filter is):

     shock correlation alone, and the per-day determinant is needed only for thermal's slow response. Those would be two levels, not one.
  QUARTER-HOURS on M0d with the last week carried (true load given; quarter-hour prices from October 2025 only):
   - forecast for a quarter = the hour's forecast + k x (the within-hour swing the curves give when the market is cleared on quarter-hour
     demand with the hour's state). k is estimated at each origin from quarter-hours before it only.
   - who follows a swing in demand (raw quantities since October 2025; shares): from hour to hour thermal 0.28, imports 0.36, storage 0.28,
     unmeasured 0.08; within the hour thermal 0.24, imports 0.25, storage 0.40, unmeasured 0.12. Within the hour storage does more of the
     work and imports less. This is who, not at what price.
   - observed within-hour swing against the curves': about twice for small swings (below 1 ct), bending back toward one for large ones.
     One scale k is therefore not stable: 3.4-4.1 on winter data, 1.9-2.1 once the summer's larger swings are in. The rough form (one k) is
     wrong in shape; a concave relation is the next refinement.
   - D+2 on slots, ct (hourly forecast repeated -> + shape with k from the past -> true shape known):
        winter  night 1.39 -> 1.33 -> 1.28;   first 14 h 1.39 -> 1.33 -> 1.28
        summer  night 1.39 -> 1.33 -> 1.28;   first 14 h 1.93 -> 1.83 -> 1.72
        autumn  night 2.70 -> 2.65 -> 2.59;   first 14 h 2.93 -> 2.84 -> 2.76
     The shape is worth 0.04-0.06 ct at night and 0.06-0.11 ct on the first 14 h, about half of what the true shape would give.
  STATE at the end of this session. Market model: M0d (units fixed) is the working level; memory is not locked; quarter-hour output exists

21.14 The "one cause" test of 21.13, run. Curves and day-to-day memory from variant B; hour-to-hour dependence added to the SIMULATED shocks only,
      innovation scales shrunk by sqrt(1 - phi^2) so the hourly spread is unchanged (12 simulations each, 5-50-95%; fitting period):
                                   sd of the day's mean price error    sd of hour-to-hour change in price / cost    share at or below zero
        observed                        0.166                               0.246                                       0.075
        dependence 0 (variant B)        0.059 0.070 0.077                   0.46 0.49 0.51                              0.081 0.090 0.106
        dependence 0.85                 0.166 0.199 0.254                   0.21 0.22 0.24                              0.044 0.083 0.126
        dependence 0.93                 0.229 0.293 0.353                   0.17 0.18 0.21                              0.054 0.088 0.163
      With dependence of about 0.85 the simulated days vary as much as real ones and the hours are about as smooth. So the whole-day defect
      (and the too-narrow bands that go with it) is correlation of the shocks from hour to hour, and nothing else is needed for it. It is a
      separate matter from thermal's slow response to price, which is what made the joint fit (variant A) collapse. Two levels, not one:
      (i) hour-to-hour dependence in the shocks with the curves protected from it; (ii) thermal against the day's price.

21.15 Where the candidate's edge comes from, from runs that already exist; and its online layer on the market model (exp/mm_layer.py).
  User: "what is current best model" (answered: the candidate = structural curve refitted at every origin on 540 days + online layer that learns
  the curve's recent error as a function of effective load and hour, with forgetting; averaged 50/50 with Timestral's price) and "ok keep working
  on the market model plz".
  CORRECTION to 21.9-21.13: the candidate's target slots start at local midnight of the day AFTER the origin tag (tag 2025-12-01 -> slots from
  2025-12-02 00:00). My "candidate's origins" tables scored the market model on tag + 2 days, one day late. Same periods, not the same slots.
  Redone below on the exact slots (inputs_<period>.npz, "actual").
  Also not used so far by the market model: at 14:00 on day D the prices of D+1 are already published. The candidate's layer learns from them
  (last row of its error history, with its own load forecast for D+1). My carried state stopped at day D.

     demand D (GW)     observed median price    bare model
       -15 .. -10            -15                    -53
       -10 ..  -5             -5                    -16
        -5 ..   0              0                      4
         0 ..   5              4                     30
         5 ..  10             39                     49
     The real price is flat near zero over about 10 GW of demand; the model's curve runs straight through. In quantities, between prices of
     0 and 30 EUR/MWh net imports sit 1.4-3.1 GW above their (linear) curve and thermal output 1.5-2.4 GW above its curve; below zero both
     are back on their curves. Net imports gain 5.2 GW between 0 and about 22 EUR/MWh: four times their price response anywhere else.
  M0e = M0d + a step in supply near a price of zero, in EUR/MWh (not relative to gas cost: what switches off around zero is not gas-priced):
     thermal:      + K_z Phi((p - c_z) / s_z)                net imports:  - K_x [1 - Phi((p - c_z) / s_z)]
     one centre c_z and one width s_z for both; K_z, K_x separate. Priors: c_z ~ Normal(0, 10) EUR/MWh, s_z ~ LogNormal(log 6, 0.6),
     K_z ~ LogNormal(log 2, 0.6), K_x ~ LogNormal(log 4, 0.6). The shelf at exactly zero stays.
  EXPECTED before the fit: c_z between +5 and +10, s_z 5-10, K_x 3-5 GW, K_z 1.5-2.5 GW; the import slope x1 falls (about 8); the bare price
  at D 0-5 GW comes down from 30 toward 4 and at D -10..-5 up from -16 toward -5; with the layer the summer first-14-h error falls from 1.67
  toward 1.3 and the error below 5 GW of residual load falls by more than a third. The step is in EUR, so it should sit at the same price in
  the held-out year; if it sits higher there, it is cost-related after all.

  as M0d; the four new parameters are recovered). Real fit: r-hat 1.004, no divergences.
     step near zero: centre c_z -1.7 EUR/MWh (-2.1 .. -1.3), width s_z 5.7, K_x 4.6 GW in net imports, K_z 5.2 GW in thermal; x1 11.0 -> 9.5;

     price (the layer as the candidate uses it) cannot represent it.
  THE LAYER IN GW. Each known price is inverted into the supply shift it implies: u = demand - what the curves supply at that price (the sum

  learns u as a function of demand and hour; the market is cleared with the predicted u. This carries the error where it is located.
  The choice to learn in GW was made after seeing the scoring year; the layer's settings were not touched.
