21.19 Two inputs from the user, and the walk-forward design. Written BEFORE the refits.
  (a) User: "held out year is interesting, but given that i care about short term forecasting, shouldn't it be more like held out week?"
      Yes. Forecast skill is measured walk-forward: at each issue time use what is known, forecast the next days, score, move on. The held-out
      year tests whether the form transfers; it is not the measure of skill. The candidate is already scored walk-forward (refit at each origin).
      The market model so far: layer walk-forward, curves frozen at November 2025.
      Corrections to what I said in reply: D+1 is not a target (its prices are published before the 14:00 issue; horizons are D+2..D+7); "freezing
      costs almost nothing" quoted only the March freeze of the candidate's curve, the January freeze cost 0.10 ct (summer night) and 0.05 (autumn),
      and the market model's freeze is older; scoring more origins or longer horizons is possible only with the published load given, because
      Timestral's forecasts exist for the 146 origins and 153 slots only. Refitting puts the PARAMETERS out of sample, not the form: curve
      shapes were designed on 2024-25 pictures, the step and the layer in GW on 2026. Only days after 9 November 2026 are unseen by the design.
  (b) User's note on market rules: not all power clears on the exchange, but all schedules are nominated the day before and priced as if they had
      (opportunity cost). Supports modelling total quantities against the day-ahead price. And two stages: price set on what is expected;
      realised quantities = schedules + later adjustments. Checked (M0e shocks against realised - published residual load; sd of that error
      3.2 GW fitting, 3.7 held-out): share taken up after the auction by thermal 0.53 / 0.38, imports -0.17 / -0.01, storage 0.08 / 0.03,
      unmeasured 0.11 / 0.21. Thermal shock sd 6.40 -> 6.16 GW with that part removed: real, located, small. To go into the fuel split.
      The attached PDF is EPEX's FAQ on its rules as a legal framework; nothing on price formation; marked strictly confidential: kept out of
      archive and repository. It says exchange data are for members' internal use and website data "for information only".
  WALK-FORWARD REFIT, fixed now: expanding window from March 2024; refits with data ending at the first of each month (2025-11-01 ...
  2026-11-01); each origin uses the latest fit whose data end no later than midnight starting its issue day; the whole 200-day shift history is
  recomputed with that origin's parameters; same layer, same slots, published-load axis; the plain curve refitted on the same schedule as the
  control; fits run one at a time, r-hat checked on each; judged by paired comparison (cmp_eval.py), refit against frozen.
  EXPECTED: the bare model improves a lot in summer and autumn (the import shift moves into x0); with the layer the gain is 0.05-0.10 ct at
  night in summer and autumn and nothing in winter. Parameter paths: x0 walks down by about 2 GW a year; if M0e's step and floor jump from
  month to month, the unlocked level is showing.

21.20 Market rules supplied by the user (EPEX SPOT operational and exchange rules in force 2026-11-03, list of definitions, fundamentals
      factsheet of Feb 2022). Stored in dl_epex_rules/ (own folder; NOT in the archive, NOT in the repository; copyrighted). What bears on the model:
   - German day-ahead auction: order book closes 12:00 on D-1, preliminary results from 12:55, final by 13:05 (14:20 at the latest in delays);
     nomination by the clearing house and balance responsible parties to the TSO afterwards. So at a 14:00 issue the D+1 prices are known.
   - Tradable contracts: hour, half hour and quarter hour of the day; "orders submitted with different time resolutions are cross matched". An
     hourly order is settled on the four quarters alike ("a Block Order which covers one hour will result in the same profit as four Block Orders
     covering each respective quarter"). => within the hour only supply bid in quarter-hours can follow demand. Explains 21.13: within-hour price
     swing about twice what hourly curves give; storage takes more of a within-hour swing (0.40 vs 0.28), imports less (0.25 vs 0.36).
   - Block orders: one price per order over several expiries; all-or-none down to a minimum acceptance ratio; "a block sell Order not part of a
     block family shall not be executed when its price is higher than the volume-weighted average market clearing prices" of the contracts it
     relates to. Linked families (a parent may be executed out of the money if the family's surplus is positive), exclusive groups, loops.
     Predefined blocks: baseload 00-24, peak 08-20, off-peak 1 00-08, off-peak 2 20-24, sun peak 10-16.
     => capacity offered in blocks answers the AVERAGE price of a span. This is the mechanism behind "thermal follows the day's price" (21.6:
     12.6 GW; 21.12: hour-to-hour response a third of the slow one). Form for the next level: for each thermal block a share answers the span's
     average price (base, peak, off-peak 1 are the natural spans), the rest the hour's price. The hours of a span are then coupled through their
     average; the likelihood's slope term becomes diagonal plus low rank per day (rank = number of spans).
   - Minimum / maximum prices -600 / 4000 EUR/MWh (the data's lowest price is -499). Clearing price to two decimals.
   - Fundamentals sheet: marginal-cost pricing, plants "put on the market by the order of their marginal production cost", CO2 certificates link
     power to carbon, gas and coal prices; spot prices are "a key reference ... also for bilateral trade outside of organised marketplaces".

      eg0e, wg0e)

  THE CANDIDATE'S SIDE. Its curve refitted without the Europe-wide weather term (EU=0; otherwise the configuration of hs2 / ha / hw2), with
  the layer, night / first 14 h:
                 Timestral load: with / without                published load given: with / without
     winter      0.86 / 0.93    0.82 / 0.92                    0.78 / 0.89    0.66 / 0.83

  (calendar shift in its load axis, non-proportional gas term, weekly scale drift: no switches for the first two).
  The part, in the market model (M0e shocks, fitting period): against Europe-wide wind, net imports go from +1.4 GW (calm) to -2.6 GW (windy)
  (6 of 7 bins outside the whole-week-shift envelope) and thermal from -1.5 to +3.1; against Europe-wide temperature thermal +7.5 (cold) to
  -2.9 GW (the heat-led pattern) and imports -3.3 to +1.7. Linear in the four features, thermal shock sd 6.4 -> 4.8 GW, imports 4.8 -> 4.5.
  A first, crude way of giving it to the market model FAILED: regress the supply shift on the features over each origin's 200-day history,
  let the layer learn the rest. All four features: winter 0.81 -> 0.87, summer 1.06 -> 0.99, autumn 1.73 -> 1.90 (night). Wind only:
  0.79, 1.06, 1.81. A 200-day window confounds season with weather (temperature and irradiance are seasonal). The term belongs inside the
  model's fit, in the import and thermal blocks, over a window that spans the seasons. Not done.

21.22 Level M0g = M0e + Europe-wide weather inside the fit (exp/market0g.py). Written BEFORE the fit.
  Thermal output and net imports each get a term linear in the four Europe-wide features (wind, North Sea wind, irradiance, temperature;
  standardised on the fitting period): + Z.kT and + Z.kX, priors Normal(0, 3) GW per sd. Nothing else changes. Window: the whole fitting
  period (21 months, all seasons), which the failed 200-day regression lacked.
  EXPECTED: thermal shock sd 6.4 -> about 5 GW, imports 4.8 -> 4.5; temperature coefficient of thermal strongly negative (heat-led plant),
  wind coefficient of imports negative. With the layer, Timestral load, night: winter slightly worse (0.81 -> 0.83-0.86, as the candidate's
  curve is worse with this term), summer 1.06 -> about 1.00, autumn 1.73 -> about 1.66.

21.23 M0g (Europe-wide weather inside the fit): result. NOT ADOPTED.
  Checks: prior predictive passes; recovery r-hat 1.002, all eight weather coefficients inside their 90% bands (K_T and sd_X out by a hair);
  real fit r-hat 1.003. Thermal coefficients (GW per sd; wind, North Sea wind, irradiance, temperature) -1.5, +2.3, +3.1, -4.7; imports
  -1.0, -0.2, -0.1, +1.2. Shock sd thermal 6.40 -> 4.72 GW, imports 4.82 -> 4.22; bare fitting-period error 1.69 -> 1.55 ct. As expected.
  With the layer, Timestral load, night / first 14 h: winter 0.98 / 1.13 (M0e 0.81 / 0.96; paired +21% [+7..+38]); summer 0.98 / 1.39
  (1.06 / 1.45; -7% [-18..+3]); autumn 1.84 / 2.07 (1.73 / 2.04; +6% [0..+13]). Expected winter slightly worse (it is much worse), summer
  about 1.00 (yes), autumn about 1.66 (no, worse). Wind features only inside the fit: 0.83 / 0.98, 1.05 / 1.47, 1.73 / 2.06 = M0e.
  Reading: the in-sample gain is the temperature term (heat-led plant), which is seasonal; as a fixed linear effect it does not carry into
  the forecast year and the layer already holds the slow part. The candidate's gain from this term (0.08 ct, summer and autumn) is not
  reproduced by adding it to supply blocks. Left as is.

21.24 Level M0h = M0e + thermal capacity answering the day's average price (exp/market0h.py). Written BEFORE the fit.
  From the rules (21.20): a block order is executed on the average price of its span. Rough form, one span (the day):
     thermal = F_T + K_z Phi(step) + K_T [ a Phi((r_hour - m_T)/s_T) + (1 - a) Phi((r_day - m_T)/s_T) ],   a ~ Beta(2, 2) the share bid by the hour,
     r_day the day's mean price / cost. The hours of a day are then coupled through r_day: the slope term of the likelihood becomes the
     determinant of (diagonal + rank one) per day: sum of log own slopes + log(1 + c / n * sum_k 1 / (L_k s_k)), c = (1 - a) K_T phi(z_day) / s_T.
     Clearing a day is a fixed point in r_day (iterated). Days are UTC+1 days (rough). Shelf hours carry no slope and are left out of the sum.
  EXPECTED: a between 0.3 and 0.5 (21.12: thermal answered the hour a third as strongly as slower moves); the thermal shock against the
  hour's price minus the day's mean flattens (12.6 GW range in M0c) to under 5 GW; s_T narrows; with the layer the night slots gain a
  little (about 0.03 ct) because the model's day shape is right for the right reason; nothing in the surplus hours.

21.25 M0h (thermal answering the day's mean price): result. NOT LOCKED, and it points at the fuel split.
  Prior predictive passes (sd of hour minus the day's mean, price / cost: 0.30 0.60 1.54; observed 0.46). Recovery: r-hat 1.002, 18 of 20
  inside, the hourly share a recovered (0.40 for 0.40). Real fit: does NOT mix (r-hat 143): two modes. Short chains from other seeds:
     mode A (3 chains of 4): a 0.67, step near zero as in M0e (c_z -1.6, K_z 4.4), K_T 27.6, s_T 0.30, sd_T 4.59;  log density -169448
     mode B (1 chain of 4):  a 0.00, K_T 6.7 on the day's mean only, and the "step" has become a 23 GW curve centred at 105 EUR/MWh,
                             41 wide, i.e. thermal output answering the price in EUR; sd_T 3.59;                      log density -167378
  The data prefer mode B by about 2000 log units, against a prior that puts its centre ten sds away. In mode A the thermal shock against
  the hour's price minus the day's mean still ranges over 9.6 GW (fitting) / 6.8 GW (held-out): the expectation "under 5 GW" is not met.
  Reading: within the fitting period thermal output is better described in EUR than relative to gas-plus-CO2 cost. Most thermal output is
  lignite and hard coal, whose cost does not move with gas. One curve in price / L cannot say that; the fuel split can. This is evidence
  for the merit order from the likelihood itself (21.18 had none from forecast scores). The day's-mean term is to be revisited inside it.

21.26 Level M1, the merit order by fuel, on top of M0e (exp/market1.py). Spec as 21.8 with these changes: imports keep the step near zero
  (K_x, c_z, s_z); thermal's step K_z is dropped (each fuel has its own must-run share); no day's-mean term yet. Written BEFORE the fit.
  EXPECTED: lignite's and coal's cost offsets a_l about 10-20, a_c about 30-45 EUR/MWh; gas centred above L (m_g 1.2-1.6); kap_g wide and
  prior-driven (gas above 17.6 GW never seen). Held-out year (gas 30-130% dearer): lignite and coal sit on their own curves in p / c_f
  (mean logit shocks near zero), which the single curve could not do (+1.2 GW). Bare price: better than M0e in the held-out year
  (2.38 ct), about equal in the fitting period (1.69). Temperature pattern mostly in gas; day's-price pattern mostly in lignite.

     lignite  cost = 9.4 + CO2,        must-run 18% of 18 GW, 56% of the rest comes on, spread 0.39, logit shock sd 0.36

  Refinement of the reading in 21.27 (numbers, not the picture's eye): at price / own cost above 1.4 the median output is 10.8 -> 9.4 GW for
  lignite and 5.8 -> 4.1 GW for coal (fitting -> held-out), but the 99th percentile of output is unchanged (13.3 / 13.1; 7.6 / 7.0). The plant
  exists and is used less at a given price / cost; "capacity drifted" overstates it. By month of the held-out year, output minus block:
  lignite about -2 to -3 GW from April to August 2026 and back to zero by November; coal -1 to -2 GW from May on; gas +3.6 to +6.2 GW in
  December-February and +6.8 in November (the heat-led pattern: gas runs for heat in winter whatever the price), about zero in summer.
  So two things are mixed in the held-out departure: a seasonal pattern the block has no term for (gas in winter), and lignite and coal
  running less in summer 2026 than their price / cost would suggest.

21.28 New key metric (user): "forecasting at midnight what the midnight to 6am prices will be one day ahead", with the viz-based workflow.
  Reading: issue at 00:00 of day D, target 00:00-06:00 of D+1 (quarter-hour slots 0-23 of the target day). Known at that midnight: prices
  through D (auction of D-1), quantities through D-1. The existing forecasts (issued 14:00 two days before the target, with the D+1 prices in
  the layer's history) use the same prices and older weather and quantities: valid, slightly conservative entries. (exp/key_metric.py,
  key_diag.py; out/key_metric.png, key_diag.png)
  MAE 00:00-06:00, ct (bias), Timestral's load:      winter         summer         autumn

  Pictures: error is flat over the six hours except the first quarter-hour of the day, which is worse for every model. Night by night the
  merit order and the candidate's curve err together (correlation of the night's signed error 0.93 summer, 0.94 autumn): the errors have

  handed to the market model. Next: run Timestral's load forecast from a midnight origin for the 146 nights and rescore.

21.30 Structure side, judged on the key metric (00:00-06:00, midnight issue). Two looks, then level M1h.
  (a) The first quarter-hour of the day. Actual price minus its hour's mean by quarter, hour 00: +0.5 / +0.2 / -0.2 / -0.5 ct (winter),
      +0.9 / +0.2 / -0.3 / -0.8 (summer and autumn); hours 01-05: nothing. The merit-order forecast has a quarter of that (+0.2 .. -0.2);
      the candidate's curve has all of it (its calendar term is by hour and quarter). Within-hour error is 0.17 / 0.25 / 0.29 ct of the slot
      error, but removing ALL of it would take the night MAE only from 0.69 / 0.83 / 1.50 to 0.67 / 0.77 / 1.45.
      One within-hour scale k learned walk-forward from earlier origins (exp/m1_within.py; k 1.8-2.6): 0.70 / 0.81 / 1.50. Small; one k
      cannot make hour 00 four times steeper than the rest. Left for later (a scale per hour of day, or the rule itself: hourly orders are
      flat within the hour, so the quarter-hour price follows the within-hour change of demand against only the quarter-hourly supply).
  (b) M1h = M1 + block orders: lignite and hard coal answer a mix of the hour's price and the day's mean price,
      effective price = al_f p_hour + (1 - al_f) p_day, al_f ~ Beta(2, 2); gas answers the hour. Slope term: diagonal + rank one per day
      (det = prod s_h (1 + mean_h v_h / s_h), v_h the day's-mean slope of lignite and coal at hour h). A day clears by fixed point in p_day.
      Written BEFORE the fit. EXPECTED: al_l well below 0.5 (lignite is the slow plant), al_c around 0.5; logit shock sd of lignite and coal
      down (0.36, 0.68 in M1); one mode this time (the EUR-versus-cost confusion of M0h is gone with the fuel split); on the key metric with
      the layer a small gain at night (0.02-0.05 ct), because at night the day's mean price is far above the hour's and block-bid plant
      should run more than the hour's price says.

21.32 Level M1s = the merit order with block orders on the spans the rules define (exp/market1s.py). Written BEFORE the fit.
  Lignite and hard coal answer  w_hour p_hour + w_day p_day + w_span p_span(hour),  spans off-peak 1 (00-08), peak (08-20), off-peak 2
  (20-24); weights Dirichlet(2, 2, 2) per fuel. Gas answers the hour. Slope term: diagonal + rank four per day (4 x 4 determinant).
  A day clears by fixed point in the day's and the three spans' mean prices. Days and hours on UTC+1 (rough in summer time).
  EXPECTED: a clear weight on the span for both fuels (0.2-0.4) taken mostly from the day's weight; log density above M1h's (-161430) by
  hundreds, not thousands; key metric between M1h (0.78 / 0.88 / 1.68) and M1 (0.69 / 0.83 / 1.50), because the night then answers the
  night span's own mean price and not the whole day's.

21.33 M1s (block orders on spans) and a test of what the day's-mean term really is. (exp/market1s.py, m1h_oracle.py; out/market1s_post.npz)
  M1s: prior predictive passes; recovery r-hat 1.003, 33 of 34 inside, all six weights recovered, one mode; real fit r-hat 1.003, one mode.
     weights hour / day / span: lignite 0.45 / 0.46 / 0.09, hard coal 0.40 / 0.56 / 0.04. Log density -161362 against M1h's -161430: +68
     for four parameters. EXPECTED a span weight of 0.2-0.4: NO. The data put the weight on the day, not on the span. Not scored with the
     layer: with these weights it is M1h.
  Oracle test on M1h (does the night suffer because the target day's mean price is itself a forecast?): clear the night with the TRUE
  day's mean price given, all else as forecast. Night MAE ct: winter 0.78 -> 0.88, summer 0.88 -> 1.10, autumn 1.68 -> 1.85 (M1 without block
  orders: 0.69 / 0.83 / 1.50). Knowing the day's mean makes the night forecast WORSE.
  Reading: the day's-mean term is not acting as a response of block-bid plant. If it were, the true day's mean would help. It is absorbing
  something else that goes with the day's mean price: the shocks of lignite and coal are persistent through the day (hour-to-hour
  correlation about 0.9, 21.11), high-price days are high-output days for slow reasons, and with hours treated as independent the
  likelihood books that as a response to the day's price. This is the hour-to-hour-memory failure of 21.12 in another guise, and the same
  remedy applies: shocks with a day-level component have to be in the model BEFORE a block-order response can be identified. The +4600 log
  density of M1h is therefore not evidence for block orders. M1 (no block orders) stays the working merit-order level.

21.34 Three theories for the shocks (user: "develop three theories for the shocks"). Written BEFORE looking. Shock = a fuel's output minus
  its block in M1, which persists through the day and across days. Each theory says where on the block the shock should be largest and
  what it should move with:
   T1 AVAILABILITY. Units are out (maintenance, faults); the capacity that can run changes for days or weeks. Signature: shock largest at the
      TOP of the block (high price / cost), skewed downward, moves with published available capacity, seasonal (maintenance in summer).
   T2 COST. The block's cost is wrong or moving (no coal price series; hedges; CO2 timing). A shift ALONG the price axis. Signature: shock
      largest in the MIDDLE of the block where it is steep, near zero at floor and top; moves with nothing in quantities; slow.
   T3 COMMITMENT AND MUST-RUN. Plant that is on stays on (start costs, minimum run times), plant runs for heat or grid needs whatever the
      price. Signature: shock largest at the BOTTOM (low price / cost), skewed upward there; moves with temperature and with yesterday's
      price at a given price today; weekday pattern.
  They are not exclusive; the pictures are to say how much of each, per fuel (exp/shock_theories.py).
  WHAT THE PICTURES SAY (out/shock_theories.png; M1 shocks, fitting period, daily means; envelope from whole-week shifts):
                         lignite                 hard coal               gas
   daily shock sd        1.37 GW                 1.16 GW                 2.56 GW
   widest on the block   middle (1-1.2 of cost,  middle (5.0 GW; bottom  TOP (10.9 GW; bottom 3.7); median -3.7 GW at the top
                         5.6 GW; bottom 2.7,     2.0, top 4.2)
                         top 4.3)
   T1 availability       +0.21 GW/GW, 18%        +0.25 GW/GW, 36%        +0.52 GW/GW, 54%
   T3 heat (temperature) -0.09 GW/C, 11%         -0.14 GW/C, 34%         -0.43 GW/C, 68% (same on low- and high-price days)
   T3 commitment         yesterday's price 0%    0%                      0%;  weekend -0.7 / -0.5 / -0.6 GW
   memory 1 / 7 / 28 d   0.77 / 0.48 / 0.27      0.83 / 0.66 / 0.51      0.93 / 0.85 / 0.70
   all four together     24% (left 1.19 GW)      47% (left 0.84 GW)      76% (left 1.24 GW); memory of what is left 0.64-0.70 at 1 day
  Reading per fuel. GAS: the shock is mostly heat (T3 must-run: 68%, the same slope whatever the price) and availability (54%; the two
  overlap, both are seasonal); together 76%. HARD COAL: availability and heat, about half. LIGNITE: three quarters unexplained by any of
  these; its shock is widest in the steep middle of the block and fades in weeks, which is the signature written down for T2 (its cost is
  off, or what it bids is not its cost) -- not shown, only not excluded. Commitment in the form "yesterday's price" has no support for any
  fuel; a weekend drop of 0.5-0.7 GW has. Availability and temperature are covariates that are known or forecast ahead: by the user's rule
  they belong in the structure.

21.35 Level M1c/d/e = M1 + what the theories found, in three switches (exp/market1c.py). Written BEFORE the fits.
  COV: on the logit of each fuel's utilisation, terms in published available capacity (lignite + hard coal for those two, gas for gas),
       temperature (Europe-wide, the forecastable series) and a weekend flag; all standardised, priors Normal(0, 1).
  MEM: a day-level shock per fuel with memory: level_d = rho level_(d-1) + kap (yesterday's mean surprise); rho ~ Beta(8, 2), kap ~ Beta(2, 6).
  BLK: lignite and hard coal answer al_f p_hour + (1 - al_f) p_day (as M1h).
  The test of 21.33's reading: fit COV+MEM+BLK. If the day's-mean weight (1 - al_f) falls from 0.5-0.6 toward zero once the day-level shock
  is there, the "block-order response" of M1h was persistence. If it stays, block orders are real and M1h's night failure has another cause.
  EXPECTED: (1 - al) falls to below 0.2 for both fuels; shock sds fall further (lignite 0.36 -> about 0.2, coal 0.68 -> 0.35, gas 0.56 ->
  0.3); gas's temperature coefficient clearly positive for cold (negative in the standardised temperature); kap around 0.5 or more (the
  day-level shock is learned fast), rho high.

21.36 User: "what about running a back of the envelope based on achieved prices and seeing whether strategic withholding can be observed?
  per plant or fuel or something else". Plan, written BEFORE looking (exp/withhold.py):
  The unit that can gain from withholding is the OPERATOR's portfolio, not the plant and not the fuel: holding back one unit raises the price
  earned by everything else the same owner runs. Envelope per hour: gain of holding back 1 GW = (owner's other output) x (price rise per GW
  taken out) - (price - cost) of the GW given up. Price rise per GW from the merit-order model's supply slope at the observed price.
  Three looks: (1) by fuel, all hours: capacity that is published as available, in the money (price above cost by 20%) and not running,
  against how tight the market is; technical reasons do not care about tightness, an incentive does. (2) the same idle capacity in hours
  where the envelope says holding back pays against hours where it does not, at the same price / cost. (3) per unit and operator for the two
  October fortnights with unit data (2025, 2026): load factor of each lignite unit in in-the-money hours.
  EXPECTED if there is withholding: idle in-the-money capacity rises with tightness and is larger where the envelope says it pays; if not:
  flat or falling. Operator shares are rough (lignite: the largest owner about half).
  RESULT of 21.36 (out/withhold.png). No sign of strategic withholding in what can be seen here; one unexplained pattern at unit level.
   - Price rise per GW taken out (merit-order model's supply slope at the observed price): 1.5 / 2.8 / 8.1 EUR/MWh (5-50-95%).
   - Lignite + hard coal, hours where price exceeds cost by 20% or more (19% of hours): capacity published as available and not running
     FALLS as the market tightens (5.0 GW at low demand to 1.0 GW at 60 GW) and falls as price / cost rises (2.8 GW at 1.3 to 0.8-1.0 GW
     above 2.5). An incentive to withhold would give the opposite slope; technical idleness (ramping, minimum load, units still starting)
     gives this one.
   - Envelope for the largest lignite owner (about half the fleet): holding back 1 GW that is in the money earns about 40 EUR/MWh on the
     rest of its lignite output in those hours and gives up 61 EUR/MWh or more on the GW itself. Positive in 0% of in-the-money hours.
   - Gas in the money: 13-46% of the 33 GW installed runs, more as the market tightens. The published gas availability covers only part of
     the fleet (output exceeds it in 26% of hours), so nothing can be said about idle gas.
   - Units (two October fortnights only, 142 and 270 in-the-money hours): lignite units run at a load factor of about 0.5 while 20% or more
     in the money. LEAG 0.53 in both years (57-62% of unit-hours below 0.6 load). RWE 0.77 in 2025 and 0.52 in 2026 (unit-hours below 0.6
     load: 12% -> 54%). This is the "throttling in 2026" noted in earlier sessions. Not explained: could be reserve and redispatch
     obligations, fuel or emissions limits, or commercial. Two fortnights cannot tell.
  CAVEATS: operator shares are rough; the published availability may itself be strategic; an envelope on one owner's lignite ignores its
  other fuels and its forward sales (which remove the incentive). To go further per operator needs unit data for the whole period
  (ENTSO-E per-unit generation), which is a download, not a modelling step.

21.37 Result of 21.35: day-level shocks, then block orders (exp/market1c.py; out/market1cmb_post.npz, market1cmbr_post.npz).
  Version 1, memory of EARLIER days only (COV+MEM+BLK): prior predictive passes (day-to-day correlation of each fuel's daily output inside
  the simulated range); recovery r-hat 1.004, 40 of 45 inside, memory and block weights recovered. Real fit r-hat 1.002, one mode,
  log density -137717 (M1h -161430). Share answering the hour: lignite 0.67, coal 0.65 (day's-mean weight 0.52 / 0.59 -> 0.33 / 0.35).
  Level memory: rho 0.78 / 0.85 / 0.98, gain 0.99 / 0.95 / 0.72 (lignite, coal, gas): the level is essentially yesterday's mean surprise.
  Logit shock sd 0.22 / 0.36 / 0.22 (M1: 0.36 / 0.68 / 0.56). Covariates on the logit scale (per sd): availability +0.05 / +0.17 / -0.01,
  temperature -0.07 / -0.01 / -0.08, weekend -0.11 / -0.36 / -0.27; gas centre m_g 1.40 -> 1.14.
  This version still lets a shock common to TODAY's hours pass as a response to today's mean price. Hence:
  Version 2, plus a shock shared by all hours of the same day per fuel (Gaussian, integrated out; DAYRE=1). Recovery: r-hat 1.008, 41 of 48
  inside; block weights recovered (0.81 for 0.80, 0.68 for 0.70); the same-day shock sizes come back slightly high (0.43 for 0.40, 0.33 for
  0.30) and coal's memory is off (rho 0.95 for 0.90). Real fit: r-hat 1.004, one mode, log density -124296.
     share answering the hour:   lignite 0.96 (0.94-0.98)     hard coal 0.74 (0.72-0.77)
     same-day shock (logit sd):  lignite 0.26, coal 0.36, gas 0.18;  hour-level sd 0.19 / 0.32 / 0.19
  EXPECTED (1 - al) below 0.2 for both: YES for lignite (0.04), NOT QUITE for coal (0.26, down from 0.59).
  Reading: lignite's "block-order response" was persistence within the day; it is gone once the day has its own shock. For hard coal about
  a quarter of capacity still answers the day's mean price, which is what block orders would give, but its recovery check was the weaker
  one. The same-day shock is larger than the hour-level noise for lignite and coal: within a day these plants sit above or below their
  block together. That is the day-level object the theories of 21.34 have to explain (availability explains 18% / 36% of it, heat 11% / 34%).
  NOT DONE: this version is not yet scored on the key metric (it needs a forecast of the day-level shock; the layer does that job for M1).

21.38 Step 2 and the per-unit download. Written BEFORE the results.

  Step 2. Reading ts_live3.py: Timestral's price forecast already takes the price history up to the start of the target day, so a "midnight

  blends follow by about half of that.

21.39 The day-shock model on the key metric (exp/m1c_layer.py; run m1cm). Merit order with availability, heat, weekend, day-level shocks and
  the fitted block weights (lignite 0.96 / coal 0.74 on the hour), with the layer in GW, midnight load forecast. Target day: availability
  carried from the day before, temperature from the forecast features, weekend from the calendar.
     MAE 00:00-06:00 ct: winter 0.73, summer 0.94, autumn 1.63  (M1: 0.69 / 0.83 / 1.50; paired +6% [-8..+21], +13% [-2..+31],
     +8% [-2..+21]; candidate's curve 0.77 / 0.70 / 1.31). Not distinguishable from M1, and not better.
  The pattern now holds four times (Europe-wide weather, block orders, spans, shock covariates): each addition describes the past better
  and leaves the night forecast where it was or slightly worse. With the layer learning the total shift from the latest prices, slow or
  day-level structure inside the blocks is already accounted for; what would move the night forecast has to be something the latest
  prices do NOT already contain.

     MAE 00:00-06:00 ct:   Timestral price 0.54 -> 0.51 (-6% [-23..+16]),  0.73 -> 0.65 (-10% [-18..0]),  1.27 -> 1.14 (-10% [-17..-3])

21.41 Lignite unit by unit, whole period (exp/entsoe_units_all.py, units_look.py; entsoe/units_all/, out/units_lignite.png). ENTSO-E actual
  generation per unit, every second day, March 2024 - November 2026: 502 days, 30 units, 12023 hours; the units' sum matches the fuel-level
  lignite series (ratio 0.993, correlation 0.99). Outage messages from entsoe/unavail_gen_DE_LU.parquet (per station, not cancelled).
  "In the money" = price at least 20% above lignite's cost in M1 (5539 hours).
   - WITHDRAWN from 21.36: "lignite units ran at about half load while in the money". That number averaged units that were off with units
     that were on. Units that run, run at 0.83-0.91 of capacity at every hour of the day (LEAG a little lower around midday).
   - The model's daily lignite shock is unit on/off state: 60% explained by the GW of units that are off (-0.54 GW of shock per GW off);
     part-loading of running units adds nothing (5% alone). This is the "three quarters unexplained" of 21.34: it was not cost (T2), it is
     availability in the wide sense (T1), which the fleet-level published availability series only caught 18% of.
   - Capacity not producing while in the money: 5.1 GW on average, of which planned outage 0.9, unplanned 0.2, NO OUTAGE MESSAGE 4.1 GW
     (units closed for good left out). Even on days whose mean price is 20% above cost: 3.9 GW without a message.
     By operator, no-message idle GW (2024 / 2025 / 2026): LEAG 2.7 / 2.4 / 2.7 (whole-day-in-the-money: 2.6 / 1.9 / 2.7);
     RWE 0.7 / 1.0 / 1.5 (0.6 / 0.9 / 1.5). Seasonal: April-September 5.3 GW, October-March 2.7 GW.
     Against tightness: 6.0 GW at the lowest demand band down to 1.8 GW at the highest. Load factor of the operator's live units in the
     money: LEAG 0.61 / 0.64 / 0.65, RWE 0.73 / 0.68 / 0.64; both drop to about 0.52 from April to July 2026.
  Reading. About 4 GW of lignite units are off without an outage message in hours when the hourly price would cover their cost. It is not
  the pattern strategic withholding would give (it shrinks as the market tightens, and it is largest in summer when prices are lowest).
  It fits units being taken off for stretches when the profitable hours are too few to justify running through the day and the week
  (start costs, minimum load through the solar hours), seasonal shutdowns, and reserve arrangements; and my cost for lignite is one number
  for a fleet whose older units cost more. RWE's rise from 0.7 to 1.5 GW over three years is the one trend. None of this is settled by
  these data. For the model: the state "which lignite units are on" is what its lignite shock is. ENTSO-E publishes per-unit generation
  only after some days (the lag was not checked here), so for a next-day forecast it is not a usable covariate as it stands; the layer
  recovers it from prices.

21.42 User: "clearly this shows we will need 2-D order book on supply side? stacking across fuels and stacking across run times??"
  Yes as a hypothesis; the test before building (exp/runtime_look.py), written BEFORE looking: if plant commits for a run of length W, its
  output should follow the price / cost AVERAGED over about W, not today's. For each fuel, daily output against the trailing W-day mean of
  price / own cost, W = 1 .. 56 days; and output as a surface over (today's price / cost, the W-day mean). EXPECTED: gas follows the day
  (W = 1 best, little gain from longer windows); hard coal a week or so; lignite weeks (W = 14-28 best), and at a given long-run mean
  today's price still matters somewhat (both dimensions).
  RESULT of 21.42 (out/runtime_look.png). The second dimension exists, but it is not a longer price average.
   - Share of the ordering of daily output explained by the trailing W-day mean of price / own cost, W = 1 / 7 / 28 / 56 days:
     lignite 0.65 / 0.23 / 0.13 / 0.10; hard coal 0.56 / 0.32 / 0.28 / 0.29; gas 0.47 / 0.26 / 0.26 / 0.38. Best at ONE day for all three.
     Surface over (today's price / cost, its 4-week mean): lignite moves 5.5 GW across today's price and 0.7 GW across the 4-week mean;
     hard coal 2.8 and 1.1; gas 5.4 and 3.9 (gas's long dimension is the season: heat).
     EXPECTED lignite to follow weeks and coal about a week: NO, both follow the day.
   - What the daily SHOCK (output minus block) follows, share explained: any average of price over the last or next 3-28 days: 0.00-0.03
     for lignite and coal; today's MINIMUM price 0.16 / 0.07 / 0.23 (lignite, coal, gas); all price terms together 0.19 / 0.17 / 0.34;
     yesterday's shock 0.68 / 0.73 / 0.87; the shock a week ago 0.36 / 0.51 / 0.73.
   - Run lengths from the unit data (22 live lignite units; every second day, so short spells are undercounted): units are on 65% of
     unit-days; completed on-spells last 4 / 8 / 18 days (quartiles), off-spells 2 / 4 / 6 days; half of all on-days lie in spells
     longer than four weeks, a third of off-days too; about 3.5 unit switches a day across the fleet.
  Reading. The user's two-dimensional supply is right in this sense: at a given cost there is a second coordinate, whether the unit is
  committed, and commitments last days to weeks. But the data do not support stacking that second coordinate by a price averaged over the
  run: which units are on is explained by yesterday (two thirds and more) and hardly by prices past or coming (a fifth at most, and of
  that the day's cheapest hour is the main part: a unit that is on has to run through the trough). So the second axis behaves as a STATE
  with inertia, not as a second price-ordered stack. In model terms that is what the day-level shock with memory already is; what the
  unit data add is its meaning (GW of units committed) and its time scale.

21.43 User: "cannot see reads to me like not yet modeled latent state?" Agreed. The commitment of units is a latent state with its own
  dynamics, not a residual with memory. Plan, written BEFORE looking (exp/commit_look.py), from the lignite unit data (state observed every
  second day): the two hazards, P(off unit is on two days later) and P(on unit is off two days later), against the price / cost of the
  days in between (mean and the cheapest hour), weekend, and operator. Then a generative check: a two-state model per unit with those
  hazards, simulated forward from the first day given only prices, against the committed capacity actually observed.
  EXPECTED: both hazards depend on price (on-hazard rises, off-hazard falls with price / cost), with a gap between them (a unit that is on
  stays on at prices where an off unit would not start: hysteresis); the free-running simulation tracks the seasonal shape of committed
  capacity but not its week-to-week level, because the state has memory that prices do not determine.
  RESULT of 21.43 (out/commit_look.png). The latent state has price-driven dynamics; my reading in 21.42 ("hardly by prices") was about the
  LEVEL of the shock, and it was the wrong question. 22 live lignite units, 10560 two-day transitions.
   - Off units: 23% are on two days later; the rate rises from 0.06 (price / cost about 0.35) to 0.34 (above 1.4).
     On units: 12% are off two days later; the rate falls from 0.31 to 0.04 over the same range. The two cross just below price = cost.
   - Against the cheapest hour of the two days: switching off 0.20 with a deep trough, 0.04 with none.
   - Logistic hazards (constant, mean price / cost, cheapest hour, weekend): on -1.53, +0.40, +0.41, -1.03; off -0.88, -1.23, -0.29, +0.80.
     At price = cost a unit that is on stays on about 21 days and one that is off stays off about 7: inertia in both directions.
   - Generative check: every unit as a two-state chain with these hazards, started from the first day's states and run forward on PRICES
     ALONE for 2.7 years: the median path explains 44% of observed committed capacity (mean 9.8 GW, sd 2.5), and the observed path lies
     inside the simulated 5-95% band on 81% of days. From the observed state two days earlier: 57%, MAE 1.24 GW; carrying the state
     unchanged: 26%, MAE 1.79 GW.
  EXPECTED hazards to depend on price with a gap (yes) and the free run to get the seasonal shape but not the week-to-week level (it gets
  more than that: 44%). So the commitment state is a modelled latent state now, with a meaning (GW of units on), dynamics (two hazards with
  inertia, price, the trough, the weekend), and a predictive gain over carrying it (MAE 1.79 -> 1.24 GW two days ahead).
  This is the user's second supply dimension: at a given cost, capacity is stacked by whether it is committed, and commitment moves by
  hazards, not by a static curve. NEXT LEVEL (M2): each fuel block = committed capacity (state) x utilisation of what is committed; state
  observed in the fit where unit data exist (lignite now; hard coal and gas units to be downloaded), filtered and propagated with the
  hazards in the forecast, jointly with the price (the price moves the hazards and the state moves the price).

21.44 The commitment model out of sample, and utilisation of what is committed (exp/commit_oos.py; out/commit_lignite.npz). Coal and gas
  unit downloads started in the background (exp/entsoe_units_cg.py, two workers).
  Hazards fitted on transitions before December 2025 only: on (-1.42, +0.34, +0.58, -1.35), off (-0.84, -1.45, -0.15, +0.77): close to the
  all-data fit. Committed lignite capacity two days ahead, MAE GW: fitting period 1.17 with the hazards against 1.72 carrying the state
  unchanged (R2 0.59 / 0.25); HELD-OUT year 1.44 against 1.96 (R2 0.52 / 0.24). The gain holds out of sample.
  Run forward from 1 December 2025 on prices alone through the held-out year: median path R2 0.44, MAE 1.79 GW, BIAS +1.15 GW, observed
  inside the 5-95% band on 68% of days (should be 90). In 2026 fewer units are on than these hazards expect at these prices: the same 2026
  shift seen before (21.27, 21.41), now located in the commitment state.
  Utilisation of committed capacity (lignite output / GW of units on), by price / cost: below 0.3: 0.46-0.47; 0.6-0.8: 0.56; 0.8-1.0: 0.70;
  1.0-1.2: 0.86; above 1.2: 0.91-0.93. So a committed unit runs between a minimum load of about 46% and about 93%, and moves between them
  as the price passes its cost. Hourly lignite output sd 3.02 GW; given the committed capacity and the price band 1.07 GW.
  LIGNITE BLOCK OF THE NEXT LEVEL (M2): output = K (GW committed, the state) x u(price / cost), u rising from 0.46 to 0.93.
  Test before building the full model: put this block into M1 with K GIVEN (an oracle) and score the key metric with the layer. EXPECTED:
  if knowing the state is worth anything beyond what the layer already recovers from prices, the night error falls; given that four
  earlier additions did not move it, a small gain or none is the honest expectation; a gain in the bare (no layer) error is expected.

21.45 Lignite commitment in the price model (oracle), the error budget of the night, and where the surprise sits. (exp/m2_layer.py,
  night_budget.py, night_blocks.py, m1_layer.py NBX=1, night_fig.py; out/night_budget.png; runs m2o, m1nb)
  (a) M1 with the lignite block = committed capacity x utilisation (0.47 to 0.95, centred at price / cost 0.91), the TRUE state of the
      target day given, with the layer: key metric 0.71 / 0.99 / 1.56 (M1: 0.69 / 0.83 / 1.50); bare, published load: 1.63 / 1.79 / 3.94
      (M1 1.60 / 2.01 / 3.71). EXPECTED a small gain or none: none. Knowing which lignite units are on does not help the night price.
  (b) Error budget of M1 + layer, night MAE ct (winter / summer / autumn): as is 0.69 / 0.83 / 1.50; with the published load known
      0.55 / 0.71 / 1.46; with the night's MEAN supply shift also known 0.27 / 0.34 / 0.60; with its hourly shape too 0.17 / 0.23 / 0.28
      (the rest is within the hour). So: load forecast 0.14 / 0.12 / 0.04; level of the night's shift 0.28 / 0.37 / 0.86; shape 0.10 / 0.11 /
      0.32; within the hour 0.17 / 0.23 / 0.28. The layer misses the night's shift by 1.8 / 1.9 / 2.4 GW on average.
  (c) Which block changes from one night to the next (night-mean shocks of M1, all days): sd of the change, GW: net imports 4.0, load
      (realised - published) 2.6, storage 1.5, unmeasured 1.5, lignite 1.5, gas 1.4, hard coal 1.0; total 5.3. Share of the variance of
      the total's change: net imports 0.56, gas 0.14, lignite 0.13, load 0.11, unmeasured 0.10, coal 0.07, storage 0.00. On the 146 target
      nights the layer's miss correlates +0.42 with the change in the import shock (lignite +0.18, others below 0.1); all blocks' changes
      together explain 40% of it.
      So the night-to-night surprise is mostly FOREIGN, not German plant. That is why the lignite state (and every other refinement of the
      domestic blocks) left the night forecast where it was.
  (d) What moves the import shock from one night to the next (sd 4.0 GW): neighbours' published residual loads, eight terms: 12% (out of
      sample 12%); Germany's own demand: 34%; both: 60%. In levels the neighbours explain 22%. Net imports follow the difference between
      German and neighbouring conditions, which a curve in the German price alone cannot say: market coupling.
  (e) First test: a linear term in the eight neighbours' residual loads in the import block (coefficients from the fitting period), the
      neighbours' PUBLISHED figures given for the target (an oracle: at midnight they are not yet out). Key metric with the layer:
      0.69 -> 0.65 (-6% [-16..+6]), 0.83 -> 0.76 (-8% [-15..-1]), 1.50 -> 1.50. With the German load also given 0.55 -> 0.51, 0.71 -> 0.66,
      1.46 -> 1.43. The first structural addition that moves the key metric, in winter and summer; small, and an upper bound until the
      neighbours' figures are forecast.

21.46 User: "wow thought we already had imports". We had net imports as a curve in the GERMAN price only. The neighbours' side was named as a

  country by country 12%; with German demand 60% (21.45 d). The neighbours pull in different directions and the sum cancels. My error.

  quantiles: clearing is monotone, so quantiles of u map to price bands). Control: Timestral PRICE given the same eight neighbour forecasts as

21.57 User: "first look into explanations for the extreme night misses". Plan (exp/miss_look.py): for every night, replace the forecast inputs by the
  truth one at a time and clear again: load forecast -> other plant -> neighbours -> supply shift; what is left is fuel cost / convention. Then
  split the shift surprise (GW) by where it sat (imports, lignite, coal, gas, storage, unmeasured: night-to-night change of each shock in
  market1n_rows). EXPECTED before looking: the large misses (night-level error above 1.5 ct, mostly autumn) are shift surprises, not load-forecast
  errors (load share under a quarter); within the shift, imports lead as in 21.45; a handful are wind-forecast busts.

21.58 Extreme night misses: results (exp/miss_look.py, miss_fig.py; out/miss_look.csv, miss_look.png). Autumn on the strict weather basis (v2).
  EXPLORATORY: 146 nights, 18 misses, and 18-20 September is one wind event supplying three of the six largest.
  a) 18 of 146 nights miss by more than 1.5 ct at night level (14 autumn, 3 summer, 1 winter) and carry 40% of all night-level error.
     Truth substituted input by input, signed share of the miss: load forecast 0.33, supply shift 0.66, other plant / neighbours / fuel cost
     ~0. All nights: 0.40 / 0.57. Against expectation (21.57): the shift leads as expected, but the load share is a third, not under a quarter;
     neighbours are nothing; imports do not lead (see d).
  b) Skew: five of the six largest misses are price FALLS forecast too shallow (20 Sep +5.0, 27 Jul +3.8, 4 Apr +3.3, 24 Sep +3.1, 19 Sep +2.9;
     the sixth, 6 Sep, is a rise forecast too small, -3.1). Sorted by the ACTUAL change the error is +1.6 ct on falls over 4 ct and -0.2 on
     rises, but that sorting is on the outcome (reviewer): any forecast looks like under-reaction there (slope 0.86).
  c) Sorted by what was known at issue (the FORECAST change): when a fall of more than 2 ct is forecast (28 nights) the absolute error is 1.3 ct
     against 0.6 otherwise; the signed error there is +0.5 +- 0.3, not clear of zero. So it is a volatility regime more than a correctable bias.
     Low forecast price level (under 6 ct, 9 nights): +0.6 +- 0.5. Timestral on the price shows the same pattern (misses the same nights,
     +0.35 / +0.44 in the two fall bins): not a property of the market model's structure.
  d) Bands: 90% band width 3.8 ct on the missed nights against 2.6 on the others (corr of |error| with width 0.44), so the risk was partly
     announced; but 14 of the 18 misses lie outside half the band width (the bands are too narrow where it matters).
  e) Within the 28 forecast falls: the 9 forecast more than 1 ct too high against the other 19. Residual load fell 20.3 GW against 12.5 (forecast
     18.1 against 13.1: wind under-forecast by 2.4 +- 1.4 GW); lignite +1.4, coal +1.6, pumped storage +1.8 GW ABOVE their curves relative to
     the night before (others about 0); net imports 4.7 GW below their curve (exports took the surplus); unmeasured the same in both. The true
     shift changed alike in both groups (-3.1 / -3.4 GW); the forecast expected a further 3.8 GW fall that did not come. Reading (conjecture):
     on nights when residual load collapses by about 20 GW, thermal plant and storage do not back down along their fitted curves (must-run,
     minimum load, units kept on through a short windy spell), the surplus is exported and the price falls further than a curve that lets
     thermal back down would say. Across all nights the six shock changes explain only 12% of the shift surprise, so this holds for the
     extreme falls, not in general. The thermal pattern on big rises is the mirror image (2.2 GW below curve) without a matching error.
  f) Calendar: 1 January and 4 April (Easter Saturday) are among the misses; ts_shift.py has no holiday flag, but Timestral price has one and
     missed both by 1.5 ct or more, so the flag is probably not the cause.
  Leads: (1) widen bands when a large fall of residual load is forecast (the regime is known at issue); (2) the latent commitment state
     (M2: units kept on) is the structural candidate for e), and it predicts the sign; (3) a third of the extreme misses is the wind forecast.

21.59 User: "prices are settled on forecast data as well... actual wind doesn't set epex price". Two consequences.
  a) Wording: the "actual" residual load in 21.58 IS the TSOs' published day-ahead forecast (rl_true = rl_de), i.e. what the auction had. The
     "wind forecast error" there is my midnight forecast against the market's own forecast twelve hours later: an information gap, not a gap to
     reality. Labels in out/miss_look.png say "actual" and should read "published".
  b) Substance: the plant quantities in 21.58 e) were REALISED output, which contains post-auction adjustment. Downloaded the day-ahead basis
     (exp/entsoe_da.py, entsoe/da/: total scheduled generation 14.1.C, day-ahead wind + solar forecast, day-ahead net position; one quarter of
     scheduled generation, Sep-Nov 2024, missing). exp/da_basis.py, out/da_rows.parquet, out/miss_look_da.csv.
     Scheduled dispatchable = scheduled generation - day-ahead wind and solar. Against realised lignite + coal + gas + other + storage
     generation: corr 0.95, mean abs difference 2.3 GW. Post-auction adjustment (realised - scheduled) has sd 2.9 GW and takes 0.47 GW per GW
     of realised-minus-published residual load (corr 0.55). Shock sd: 5.3 GW realised basis, 4.5 GW day-ahead basis (cleaner). Day-ahead net
     position against realised net imports: corr 0.96.
     The worst fall nights on the day-ahead basis (9 forecast >1 ct too high against the other 19 forecast falls; change from the night before):
       dispatchable against curves: realised +4.9 +- 1.0 | -0.1 +- 0.9 ;  SCHEDULED +3.7 +- 1.2 | -1.3 +- 0.8
       net imports against curve:   realised -4.7 +- 1.4 | -0.9 +- 1.0 ;  DAY-AHEAD -4.9 +- 1.6 | -1.1 +- 1.1
       post-auction adjustment +1.2 | +1.1 (the same in both groups); scheduled dispatchable itself -6.4 | -6.0 GW (level 18.6 | 19.6 GW);
       day-ahead net export position +11.0 +- 2.2 | +4.0 +- 1.0 GW.
     Reading: the finding survives on the auction's own basis. On the worst nights dispatchable plant was SCHEDULED about 5 GW further above
     the model's curves than on the other falls; scheduled dispatchable fell by the same 6 GW in both groups although residual load fell 20
     against 12.5 GW; the extra 7 GW went into exports. That looks like a floor under dispatchable output near 18-19 GW (must-run + minimum
     load of committed units) with the surplus priced on the export side. Still 9 nights; conjecture.
     The two day-ahead shock changes (dispatchable, imports) explain 87% of the true night-to-night change of the supply shift.
  c) User: "we can then use actual to infer latent state?" Yes: scheduled quantities define what the price was settled on (the observation
     model for the auction); realised per-unit output, known with a lag, reveals which units are on (the latent commitment state) and hence
     the floor under dispatchable output at the next auction. Next level M2 to be specified on that split.
  Housekeeping: the per-unit workers had died again (connection errors); restarted, 130 files so far.

21.60 Model description for external review written as a doc (title "German day-ahead price model: description for external review"). Writing it
  and its review turned up corrections to earlier notes:
  - 21.47 "prior predictive: observed summaries inside the 5-95% ranges" is WRONG for two: highest hard coal output 8.3 GW observed against a
    prior range of 9.4-12, highest gas output 17.5 against 23-33 (the log line had been cut at 400 characters). Fixed capacities 12 and 33 GW
    are far above anything the fleets produced; kappa for coal and gas mostly measures that gap. Same holds for M1.
  - 21.46 expected "the import slope x1 changes little": WRONG, x1 went from 9.48 (M1) to 15.11 (M1n).
  - Shock dependence measured on market1n_rows (fitting period). Daily-mean autocorrelation at 1 / 7 days: lignite 0.77 / 0.50, coal 0.83 / 0.66,
    gas 0.93 / 0.85, imports 0.53 / 0.20, storage 0.68 / 0.17, closing 0.56 / 0.32. Hourly lag-1 0.83-0.94. Cross-block, hourly: coal-gas 0.73,
    lignite-coal 0.57, lignite-gas 0.47, storage with thermal -0.21 to -0.31, imports with thermal 0.15-0.24. The likelihood assumes independence.
  - Reminder: giving M1 the TRUE lignite state made the night forecast worse (0.71 / 0.99 / 1.56 vs 0.69 / 0.83 / 1.50). M2 needs a reason to
    differ: day-ahead basis, a floor not a utilisation scaling, large falls only. Cheap test first (21.61).
21.61 Test before building M2, expectation written first: on the 28 forecast-fall nights, do quantities known at issue separate the 9 missed
  from the other 19? Candidates: size of the forecast fall of the demand axis, forecast demand level, the previous night's scheduled dispatchable
  level and its shock, previous night's day-ahead export position, "implied dispatchable" = previous night's scheduled dispatchable + forecast
  change of the demand axis (how far the fall would push plant if exports did not move), committed lignite capacity two days earlier.
  EXPECTED: the implied-dispatchable figure separates them (missed nights are those where the fall would push plant below about 18 GW);
  committed lignite capacity alone does not.

21.62 Result of the test in 21.61 (9 missed against 19 other forecast falls; Mann-Whitney p in brackets; all known at issue).
  Forecast fall of the demand axis -18.1 vs -13.1 GW (0.04); implied dispatchable (previous night's schedule + forecast change of demand)
  6.9 vs 12.5 GW (0.07); previous night's scheduled-dispatchable shock -2.5 vs +0.3 GW (0.06); previous night's net export position -4.5 vs
  -1.2 (0.06); committed lignite two days earlier 9.7 vs 11.3 GW (0.06); band width, forecast price change, previous price, weekend: nothing.
  Over all 146 nights: implied dispatchable below 14 GW (22 nights) -> mean error +0.83 +- 0.40 ct, absolute 1.55, against -0.10 and 0.63 on
  the other 124; Timestral on the price has the same bias there (+0.72). Thresholds 10 / 14 / 18 give +0.81 / +0.83 / +0.65. EXPECTATION
  partly met: the implied-dispatchable figure does separate, at a lower level than the 18 GW guessed; committed lignite separates about as
  well, which I did not expect, and in the direction of FEWER units committed on the missed nights. Small sample, thresholds looked at after
  the fact: a lead, not a result.
  Same question in the FITTING period (548 nights, none of them scored; scheduled basis):
  - Scheduled dispatchable at night does not go below about 15.5 GW even when the demand axis is under 5 GW (other 8.0, lignite 3.7, coal 1.7,
    gas 3.1; net exports 5.7 GW). The fitted must-run shares give 3.3 + 0.8 + 1.6 + 8.0 = 13.7 GW.
  - The curves already misfit by demand level in sample: scheduled dispatchable minus curves is +1.8 GW below 15 GW of demand, -0.5 at 15-20,
    about 0 at 20-25, +1.8 at 25-30, +3.5 above 30. So part of what looked like a state is a static misspecification of the curves at both
    ends of the demand range.
  - When the previous night's schedule plus the change of demand would put dispatchable below 14 GW (84 nights), the shock rises by +1.1 to
    +1.8 GW (se 0.5-1.0) from the night before; between 18 and 22 GW it falls by 1.6. After Dec 2025 the same bins give +1.1, +0.9, 0.0 (noisy).
  - A fall of the demand axis passes into scheduled dispatchable at a rate of about 0.4; the rest goes into exports. Stable across periods
    (0.42 / 0.39) and for falls of more than 15 GW (0.40 / 0.42).
  Reading: there are two things to separate before M2. (1) Static: a floor under dispatchable output about 2 GW above what the fitted curves
  allow, visible in the fitting period by demand level. That is a curve fix (must-run by fuel as a floor in GW, fitted on the scheduled
  basis), not a latent state. (2) Dynamic: the extra rise of the shock when a large fall arrives from a high schedule, which is the candidate
  for the commitment state. Do (1) first and see what is left for (2).

21.63 External review (ChatGPT, pasted by the user) of the log through 21.62. Accepted points: (1) "implied dispatchable" adds the WHOLE forecast
  demand change to the previous schedule although 21.62 itself estimates a pass-through of 0.4, so it may only flag large falls; compare the
  fall alone, the previous schedule alone, and previous schedule + 0.4 x fall; the incremental value of the previous schedule is what would
  support a state. (2) The scheduled series is an aggregate; a per-fuel floor is not identified; the curves misfit at BOTH ends; fit the
  aggregate scheduled curve over the full range first. (3) The shift is an accounting residual; the test that counts is whether information at
  midnight predicts the ERROR of tomorrow's shift forecast; the 33% / 66% shares came from one substitution order: reverse it. Also: recalibrate
  bands on earlier origins, score slot and night-mean coverage separately; show sensitivity to the 18-20 September event; freeze versions A
  (M1n), B (aggregate scheduled-dispatch curve), C (B + one lagged commitment measure), rebuild each version's shift history and retrain the
  same forecaster, evaluate on fresh origins; advance M2 only if C beats B there. Cheapest next run: on the 548 fitting nights, does a short
  trough after a high schedule leave more plant scheduled than a sustained trough at the same demand?
  Checks run now (exp/review_checks.py), EXPECTATIONS written first:
  a) Indicators: I expect the fall alone to do most of what "implied < 14" did; the previous schedule to add little once the fall is in
     (partial coefficient within 2 se of zero).
  b) Reversed order: shift share falls and load share rises by up to 10 points (the clearing curve is convex, so whichever is replaced first
     gets more).
  c) Without 18-20 September: the positive bias on low-implied nights shrinks by about a third but keeps its sign.
  d) Fitting period, low-demand nights: after conditioning on the night's demand, a higher demand the night BEFORE (arrival from above) and a
     higher demand the night AFTER (short trough) both leave more dispatchable scheduled; I expect about 0.05-0.15 GW per GW each, and net
     exports to take the other side.

21.64 Results of the review checks (exp/review_checks.py, path_fig.py; out/path_nights.csv, path_nights.png).
  a) Indicators (146 nights). Forecast fall of demand alone, > 15 GW (14 nights): mean error +0.94 +- 0.51 ct, absolute 1.72 (rest 0.67).
     "Implied < 14" (22 nights): +0.83 +- 0.40, absolute 1.55. Previous schedule + 0.4 x fall below 18 GW (17 nights): +0.81 +- 0.44.
     Previous schedule alone: nothing (-0.11 vs +0.15). Regression of the price error on fall and previous schedule, robust se: all nights
     fall -0.030 +- 0.011, schedule -0.033 +- 0.020; nights with a forecast fall of demand (76): fall -0.048 +- 0.022, schedule -0.091 +- 0.035.
     Shift-forecast error on the same: all nights schedule +0.024 +- 0.041; fall nights +0.141 +- 0.054. EXPECTATION met on all nights (the
     fall alone does the work; the schedule is within 2 se); within fall nights the previous schedule does add (2.6 se) - a subset chosen
     after the fact, so a lead for version C, no more. The reviewer was right that the 14 GW indicator mostly flagged large falls.
  b) Reversed substitution order: shares do not move (all nights load 0.40 | 0.41, shift 0.57 | 0.56; misses load 0.33 | 0.34, shift 0.65 |
     0.64). EXPECTATION (up to 10 points) wrong: the split is robust to the order.
  c) Without 18-20 September: implied < 14 gives +0.63 +- 0.36 (21 nights); the large-fall group is unchanged (none of the three is in it);
     misses 18 -> 15 carrying 0.34; autumn night-level MAE 0.99 -> 0.87 (Timestral price 1.08 -> 0.97). Sign kept, size down a quarter.
  d) Path dependence on the fitting nights, scheduled basis. Given the night's own demand (cubic): each GW by which the NEXT night's demand is
     higher adds +0.030 +- 0.012 GW of scheduled dispatchable and +0.036 +- 0.012 GW of net exports; each GW by which the night BEFORE was
     higher adds +0.035 +- 0.012. Under 15 GW of demand (145 nights): after +0.031 +- 0.017, before -0.038 +- 0.015 (sign flips). Short
     trough (next night > 8 GW higher, 74 nights) against sustained (< 3 GW higher, 43 nights) at the same demand (6.7 / 7.1 GW): scheduled
     dispatchable 18.0 against 17.5 GW, net exports 1.3 against 0.1 GW, price over cost the same (0.51 / 0.50). Dispatchable minus curves at
     the price: no path effect in low demand (+0.002 +- 0.022). EXPECTED 0.05-0.15 GW per GW: NOT MET, the effect is about 0.03 and half a
     GW in total. And the scatter shows no hard floor: scheduled dispatchable goes on falling to 9-12 GW when the demand axis is negative.
     The static misfit by demand level is larger: medians of scheduled dispatchable minus curves +2.3, +1.9, +1.8, +0.6, -0.4, +0.3, +2.2,
     +3.1, +4.4 GW in 5 GW steps of demand from -5 to 40.
  DECISION (following the review's rule): the path effect does not earn a commitment state now. Next is version B: one aggregate
  scheduled-dispatch curve fitted over the whole demand range, reconciled with the day-ahead net position; rebuild the shift history from it
  and retrain the same Timestral forecaster. Version C (B + one lagged measure: the previous schedule on forecast-fall nights) is the only
  state-like candidate with any support. Freeze A, B, C before scoring.
  FRESH NIGHTS: winter and summer were scored on every third day only; the two days in between (about 60 winter and 100 summer nights) have
  never been scored or looked at. They are interleaved in time with the used nights, not later, but untouched: the evaluation set for A/B/C.
  Needs inputs built for those origins (weather vintages, load and neighbour forecasts). Autumn has no unused nights.

21.65 Version B, specification (reviewer: price is endogenous; a plain regression of the shock on price is biased because extra supply lowers the
  price; use demand as the instrument). Two-stage fix on top of M1n, fitting period only, hourly, day-ahead basis (exp/vb_fit.py):
  scheduled dispatchable minus curves at the price = c_d(r) + shock, day-ahead net imports minus curve (with neighbour term) = c_x(r) + shock,
  r = price / cost index clipped to [-0.2, 2.2], c = constant + linear + hinges at r = 0.25, 0.6, 0.9, 1.2, estimated by two-stage least squares
  with instruments = linear and hinge functions of the demand axis (knots at its deciles). Supply of version B = M1n supply + c_d + c_x.
  Checks: residual flat across demand bins; total supply still increasing in price; with c = 0 the code reproduces A.
  A level change is absorbed by the shift, so B can only matter through the SLOPE where the market clears and through how forecastable its
  shift is. Test without Timestral, fitting nights only (the later nights stay sealed): carry the same hour's shift from the night before,
  clear under A and B with the published demand, compare price error by size of the night-to-night demand change.
  EXPECTED: c_d positive at both ends of r (U shape); B's supply less elastic at low prices; carry-forward price error lower under B by about
  10% on demand falls of more than 10 GW and unchanged elsewhere. If B does not win here, expect nothing on the fresh nights.

21.66 Version B, results (exp/vb.py, vb_fit.py, vb2_fit.py; fitting period only).
  First form (six basis functions of price / cost, two-stage least squares with demand hinges as instruments): INVALID. Supply falls with
  price in places (slope down to -0.18 GW per EUR), the import correction swings by +-5 GW between neighbouring prices, clearing with the true
  shift no longer returns the price (2 EUR off). Carry-forward test 1.37 against 1.09 ct for A. The instrument does not support that much shape.
  Second form (grouped instrument: demand deciles; correction = piecewise-linear curve through the groups' mean price / cost and mean misfit,
  flat outside): valid, supply monotone. Correction +1.0, +2.7, +1.4, -0.6, -1.4, -0.8, +0.2, +0.8, +1.8, +0.3 GW at price / cost -0.04, 0.35,
  0.64, 0.82, 0.94, 1.02, 1.08, 1.16, 1.27, 1.67 (dispatch part +1.3, +2.9, +2.1, +0.7, +0.1, +0.2, +0.8, +1.1, +1.3, +1.7; import part
  -0.3 .. -1.5). Slopes hardly move (at cost index 90: 0.19 -> 0.24 at r = 0.1, 0.35 -> 0.30 at 0.6, 0.51 -> 0.60 at 1.0).
  Carry-forward test, 638 fitting nights, shift of the night before, published demand: A 1.093, B 1.116 ct; B minus A +0.023 (-0.004 ..
  +0.049). On demand falls above 15 GW B is worse (2.08 against 1.63; both too LOW there, -0.98 and -1.67: with a carried shift the price
  falls too far, i.e. the shift itself falls when demand falls). EXPECTED a 10% gain on large falls: NOT MET, the opposite. By the rule
  written in 21.65, B does not go to the fresh nights. The aggregate misfit by demand level is real but it is a level, which the shift absorbs;
  A's slope is about right.
  Shift dynamics on the fitting nights (night means): change of the shift = +0.20 x demand change - 0.42 x yesterday's shift (R2 0.36): the
  shift reverts by about half each night and moves with demand. Adding yesterday's scheduled dispatchable: +0.31 +- 0.05 on all nights (R2
  0.40), but -0.30 +- 0.10 on demand falls above 5 GW and -0.33 +- 0.14 above 10 GW (R2 0.34 -> 0.37 and 0.36 -> 0.40). So the lagged schedule
  carries some information and its sign depends on the regime. That is enough to give version C one clean run, no more.

21.68 User: "but bayes has nice ways of handling endogeneity??" Yes: the full-information likelihood of the simultaneous system (the Jacobian
  term) is how M1/M1n already treat price as jointly determined; exogenous demand plays the instrument's role inside the model. Version B's
  two-stage add-on stepped outside that. The proper form is a refit on the day-ahead basis:
  Level M1d (exp/market1d.py), specification written BEFORE fitting. Per hour, observed: price p, scheduled dispatchable minus other plant
  T_d, day-ahead net imports X_d (= minus the net position), demand axis D = published residual load - other plant (PUBLISHED now also in the
  fit: the mixed-basis weakness goes away). Three shocks for three observations:
    T_d = sum_f K_f u_f(p) + K_g Phi((p/L - m_st)/s_st) + B_g + e_T        (fuel blocks as M1, storage GENERATION, unmeasured; e_T ~ t4)
    X_d = x0 + x1 (p/L - 1) - K_x [1 - Phi((p - c_z)/s_z)] + k . z + e_X   (as M1n; e_X ~ t4)
    D   = T_d + X_d + B_c - K_p [1 - Phi((p/L - m_p)/s_p)] + eps - K_s 1[p<0]   (closing: constant, pumping at low prices, shelf; eps logistic)
  Likelihood = density of e_T, e_X and eps times the slope of total supply in p (shelf as before). The aggregate schedule cannot identify
  three fuels on its own, so the fuel parameters get informative priors centred on the M1n posterior medians (costs sd 3 and 8 EUR, m_g 10%,
  must-run and responsive shares Beta with concentration 50 and 30, ramp widths 25%). Disclosed: this reuses the fitting-period prices once
  more through the prior. Everything else keeps the M1/M1n priors; K_g LogNormal(ln 3, 0.5), K_p LogNormal(ln 3, 0.5), B_c Normal(1, 2).
  EXPECTED: clean fit, one mode; the aggregate shock sd about 2.5-3 GW (the two-stage residual was 4.5 with realised-fitted curves);
  must-run shares move UP (the schedule showed about 2 GW more plant at low demand than the curves allow) and the gas responsive share up
  (the 3-4 GW misfit at high demand); import slope near M1n's 15; misfit by demand bin within +-0.5 GW; on the carry-forward test of 21.66
  at least as good as A (1.09 ct) and, unlike B, not worse on large demand falls.
  fresh_score.py written before the forecasts existed; sha256 first 12: 4ce2de7d4073

  C - A +0.018 (+0.004 .. +0.033) [winter -0.011, summer +0.036 (+0.016 .. +0.055)];  P - P0 -0.005 (-0.010 .. +0.001).
  Against the expectations of 21.67:
  - A level with P (within +-0.05, interval containing zero): MET. The structural route matches the black box on untouched nights too.

21.70 M1d results (out/market1d_checks.log, market1d_post.npz, market1d_rows.parquet).
  Prior predictive: nine of ten summaries inside (sd of net imports 5.5 observed against 5.8-12 just outside). Recovery: 28 of 29 parameters
  and 8 of 8 neighbour coefficients; the fuel parameters are recovered only as widely as their priors (the aggregate does not identify them).
  Real fit on 13,173 hours: r-hat 1.003, smallest effective sample 947, 3 divergent transitions, one mode.
  AGAINST EXPECTATION almost throughout:
  - The fuel blocks lost their identity. Hard coal cost 81.6 (prior centre 48.5), lignite 16.7 (9.2), gas cost multiple 0.96 (1.38); responsive
    shares coal 0.91, lignite 0.70, gas 0.08 with a ramp width of 0.08 (a step); must-run shares DOWN (0.11, 0.02, 0.03), not up. The storage-
    generation block became an 8.1 GW step centred at price/cost -0.1 (a floor that switches off below zero price) and the unmeasured constant
    went negative (-1.8). The closing block took a 5.9 GW constant and an 8.4 GW "pumping" swing centred at price = cost. With 13k hours
    treated as independent the informative priors do not hold: the aggregate schedule wants a different shape and the labels no longer mean
    what they say. This level is an aggregate supply curve, not a merit order by fuel.
  - Aggregate shock: t scale 3.06, sd 3.8 GW (expected 2.5-3). Import slope 12.7 (expected near 15); import shock scale 3.12 (3.45 in M1n).
  - Misfit by demand bin: dispatch +0.4, +0.9, +0.4, 0.0, -0.9, -1.1, -0.4, +0.5, +1.3 GW; better than M1n on this basis (up to +3.2) but not
    within +-0.5.
  - Does not transfer in level: after Dec 2025 the mean dispatch shock is -2.2 GW and the import shock -3.0 GW; bare price MAE 1.45 ct in the
    fitting period and 2.91 after.
  Carry-forward test (fitting nights, 545 common nights): A 1.113, M1d 1.060 ct; M1d minus A -0.053 (-0.082 .. -0.029). Gain on demand rises
  (0.99 vs 1.10, 1.15 vs 1.35, 1.44 vs 1.53) and none on falls above 15 GW (1.67 vs 1.61). Shift sd at night 5.5 against 6.1 GW. Slopes
  differ little (at cost index 90: 0.25 / 0.17 / 0.26 / 0.60 / 0.38 GW per EUR at price/cost 0.1 / 0.3 / 0.6 / 1.0 / 1.4 against A's 0.19 /
  0.22 / 0.35 / 0.51 / 0.42). EXPECTED "at least as good as A": MET in sample for both. "Not worse on large falls": about the same.
  Reading: the full-information fit on the auction's own basis gives a somewhat better aggregate curve for forecasting purposes and a worse
  physical description. Both facts stand.
  Version D = M1d curves + Timestral on its own rebuilt shift. Test on the 80 fresh nights of 21.69 (opened once, for A / C / P; nothing about
  M1d was chosen with them); the sealed third day stays sealed for whichever version is kept. Pre-registered: same primary metric, D - A
  paired. Decision rule: D replaces A as the forecasting level only if the D - A interval lies below zero. EXPECTED: D - A between -0.03 and

21.72 Reviewer's checks on 21.69-21.71.
  a) The day-ahead series do NOT reconcile one for one. Fitting hours: (scheduled dispatchable - net exports) = 4.5 + 0.81 x published residual
     load. The gap residual load - (schedule - exports) runs from -3.1 GW below zero price to +5.2 GW above price/cost 1.6 (by demand: -3.6 GW
     under 10 GW to +6.0 above 50). Pumping explains the low end (gap minus pumping there +0.7 to +1.4) but not the high end (+4.4, +5.2 GW
     with no pumping): at high prices the scheduled generation plus imports falls 4-5 GW short of the published residual load. Cause not
     established (plant outside the scheduled-generation aggregate, or the load forecast's coverage). M1d's "closing" block (5.9 GW constant,
     8.4 GW swing centred at price = cost) absorbed exactly this. So part of M1d's supply slope is a mismatch between two data sources that
     moves with price. M1d must be described as a mapping from published demand to price with a reconciliation term, NOT as "the auction's
     own basis". The label "pumping" for that block is wrong.
  b) Do not credit D's small edge to the treatment of endogeneity: D differs from B in the demand basis, the import refit and the closing
     block too, and its carry-forward win was on its home basis (A was fitted on realised load). On fresh nights D - A = -0.013 (-0.031 ..
     +0.003): not distinguishable from zero, on a set already opened once. 21.71's sentence "what the user's point about endogeneity bought"
     overstates; what can be said is that the fit inside the model produced a valid curve where the add-on did not.
  c) The blend A + P at 0.523 was not pre-registered and has no interval: not a result.
  d) Scope of the confirmation: winter and summer only (autumn, where the structural route looked best, has no fresh nights); the fresh nights
     sit one day after used ones; the benchmark look-ahead is closed for winter and summer, autumn was not rerun.
  e) The sealed third day is untouched by FORECASTS; aggregate diagnostics "after Dec 2025" (shocks, bare MAE, path dependence) include it.
  f) Fresh winter load error 1.93 GW: no single bad origin (median 1.62; five nights between 3.8 and 5.2 GW on different dates).
  g) ts_shift.py and shift_build.py were edited after the freeze to add version D (MOD=d); hashes now 28e2edb08c6a and 41017ab0e290. A and C
     ran on the frozen ts_shift.py (7a7b95971af0).
  Per-unit downloads: two workers alive, 280 files (coal and gas), off the critical path.

  to the source series before fitting another curve. Series as held (documented from the download scripts):
    forecast load, wind, solar used so far ("published residual load"): Energy-Charts public_power_forecast, country=de, forecast_type=
      day-ahead (exp/dl_fc.py). Country Germany, not the DE-LU bidding zone.
    ENTSO-E day-ahead load forecast, DE_LU (entsoe-py query_load_forecast; article 6.1.B) - downloaded in 21.59, not used yet.
    scheduled total generation, DE_LU (query_generation_forecast; 14.1.C "day-ahead aggregated generation").
    day-ahead wind and solar forecast, DE_LU (query_wind_and_solar_forecast; 14.1.D).
    day-ahead net position, DE_LU (query_net_position, dayahead=True; implicit allocations of the day-ahead coupling).
    realised generation by type, pumping, cross-border trading: Energy-Charts public power, Germany (net public generation).
  Publication vintages are NOT on disk (the downloads return the latest value per delivery interval, no publication timestamp); stated as
  the platform's rules, not verified.
  Identity expected on the scheduled side: forecast load = scheduled generation + net imports - pumping (+ whatever generation is in neither).
  PREDICTIONS written before computing, each a named quantity with sign and size:
   (1) Renewables: my gap used Energy-Charts wind + solar on the demand side and ENTSO-E wind + solar on the supply side. If they are the same
       publication the term cancels; I predict a small difference (|mean| under 0.3 GW) with no trend in price.
   (2) Zone: Energy-Charts load is Germany, the ENTSO-E series are DE-LU. Luxembourg's load is about 0.5-0.8 GW. Using the ENTSO-E DE-LU load
       forecast instead should RAISE the gap by that much, flat in price (wrong direction to explain +5).
   (3) Pumping: forecast load excludes pumping consumption, scheduled generation serves it. Sign negative, about -3 to -4.5 GW below price/cost
       0.3 and near 0 above 1.1 (realised pumping as proxy).
   (4) Generation outside the scheduled aggregate: the realised balance (21.1) has +2.9 GW of load not covered by public generation + trade
       (industrial and other own generation that the load figure includes). If scheduled generation has the same coverage, the gap carries
       the same term: positive, about +3 GW on average, and I predict it grows with price (own generation and small CHP run when prices are
       high): about +1.5 GW at low prices, +4 to +5 at high. Proxy: the realised uncovered amount hour by hour.
   If (3) and (4) are right, the gap after both is within +-1 GW in every price bin.
