  - imports = +6,785; after the uncovered amount +3,510. Ordinary hour 2025-07-20 07-08h (price 81): load 42,260 / 41,847; generation 34,499;
  wind + solar 16,301 / 16,216; net position -5,702; pumping -896; uncovered 1,508. Load - generation - imports = +2,059; after pumping and
  the uncovered amount +1,446.
  What is left after (4): a tilt of about 3.5 GW from low to high prices (-1.9 .. +1.6), sd 2.8 GW hour to hour.
  FIFTH candidate, prediction written before applying: the day-ahead net position (implicit allocations of the coupling) does not contain the
  Swiss border, which is allocated explicitly. Germany exports to Switzerland when German prices are low and imports when they are high, so
  the missing term has the sign of the remaining tilt. PREDICTED: scheduled exchange with Switzerland is about -1 to -2 GW (export) in the
  low-price bins and +1 to +2 GW (import) in the high ones; subtracting it leaves every bin within +-0.7 GW. Proxy: x_ch in star_data (sign
  to be fixed from its correlation with German net imports).

21.75 Fifth adjustment and conclusion (exp/recon_fig.py, out/recon_gap.png).
  Swiss exchange (x_ch in star_data, import +), median GW by the same bins: -0.75 -0.80 -0.78 -0.78 -0.35 +1.18 +2.14 +3.56. Independent
  support that the day-ahead net position lacks that border: realised trade (all borders) minus day-ahead net imports correlates 0.77 with
  x_ch (regression slope 0.88; 0.07-0.59 for the other borders). PREDICTED sign and rough size: MET at the high end and larger than said
  (+3.6 against +1 to +2), smaller at the low end (-0.8 against -1 to -2).
    (5) minus imports from Switzerland (g5)       -1.25 -1.57 -1.30 -0.34 -0.47 -1.34 -1.26 -1.50
  PREDICTED "every bin within +-0.7": NOT MET in level (six bins near -1.3), MET in shape: the tilt across prices is gone (range 1.2 GW
  against 8.4 before). A flat offset of about -1 GW remains, which no supply slope depends on; hour-to-hour spread 2.7 GW (3.5 before).
  Caveats: pumping, the uncovered amount and the Swiss exchange are REALISED series standing in for scheduled ones; the uncovered amount is
  itself a balance residual (realised load minus public generation minus trade), so its level carries whatever else does not close.
  So the 4-5 GW at high prices is named: about 3.5 GW of imports from Switzerland that the day-ahead net position does not contain, on top
  of roughly 3-4 GW of generation outside the scheduled aggregate (present at all prices, +3.0 rising to +4.4). At low prices the gap is
  pumping (-4.7 GW).
  Consequences. (i) Version A is not affected: its import block uses realised cross-border trading over all borders, and pumping is its own
  block. (ii) Version D is: its import block has no Swiss border and no pumping, and its closing block (5.9 GW constant, 8.4 GW swing centred
  at price = cost) is those two in disguise plus the uncovered generation. Slope of D's supply at cost index 90, GW per EUR, at price/cost
  0.3 / 0.6 / 1.0 / 1.4: total 0.17 / 0.27 / 0.60 / 0.37, of which the closing block 7% / 20% / 19% / 15%; A's total 0.23 / 0.35 / 0.51 /
  0.41. So up to a fifth of D's slope sits in a block with no physical label, though the response it stands for is real (pumping and Swiss
  imports do respond to price). D supports no block-by-block interpretation as fitted.
  (iii) A day-ahead-basis model worth interpreting needs net imports over ALL borders (day-ahead net position + scheduled Swiss exchange),
  a pumping block, and a constant for generation outside the schedule. Not built: D's edge over A is 0.013 ct and not distinguishable from
  zero, so this is about description, not forecast skill.

  weather basis), then the 80 fresh nights as a POST HOC secondary look (not in the pre-registration of 21.67).

  midday in summer (zero and negative prices, where the model has a shelf and a step and the quarter-hour solar ramp is not in the hourly
  curves) by 10-20%; A is about level with P in the morning and evening; night-to-day, the two routes' errors correlate less by day (about
  0.7) than at night (0.85-0.95), so a blend gains more by day than at night.

21.79 Below zero price: the model's supply against the fitting period's negative-price hours (exp/neg_look.py). Written BEFORE looking.
  The model has one block that switches off below zero (K_s = 1.4 GW, a shelf at exactly zero), an import step centred at -0.9 EUR
  (K_x = 6.8 GW, width 3 EUR) and the fuel and storage curves continuing smoothly. Nothing represents renewable plant that curtails at
  negative prices, which in the model's accounting is demand coming back as the price falls.
  EXPECTED: (a) just below zero the real market absorbs far more than the model allows: between 0 and about -10 EUR several GW of additional
  absorption (curtailment of directly marketed wind and solar, visible as realised residual load ABOVE the published one in those hours,
  by 2-5 GW), so the model, short of that volume, runs to very negative prices too early (as on 2 May 2026: -18 ct against -5.6);
  (b) further down the real curve becomes steep (little left to switch off), so when deep negatives occur the model, with its smooth
  curves, does not reach them (as on 26 Apr 2026: -4.9 against -24.6); (c) in the model's terms: the shift u rises steeply as the price
  goes from 0 to -10 EUR (by 3 GW or more) and flattens below.

21.80 Below zero price, results (exp/neg_look.py, out/neg_rows.parquet). Fitting period: 1,250 hours at or below zero, 766 below, 272 below
  -10 EUR, 57 below -50, lowest -250.
  Against 21.79: (a) "several GW of extra absorption just below zero, shift rising 3 GW from 0 to -10 EUR": WRONG. The mean shift is flat

  published residual load (curtailment proxy) does rise, but slowly: +0.8 GW at zero, +1.0 at -1..-5, +2.1 to +2.6 between -5 and -50, +3.4
  below -50. (b) "steep further down, the model does not reach deep negatives": RIGHT, and the cause is specific: below -50 EUR the shift

  The import block is linear in price/cost with no bound. Net imports by price/cost bin against the fitted curve (GW):

21.81 M1b, first form: FAILED its checks (out/market1b_checks_v1_failed.log). Prior predictive: the highest price sits at the solver's ceiling
  (2,000 ct) in the median prior draw - with imports bounded above, thermal capped by its responsive shares and no other response, supply
  can fall short of demand at the peak and the market has no clearing price. Simulate-and-recover on such data did not mix (r-hat 26, chains
  at log density -90,244 / -93,940 / -93,939 / -95,363; gas cost multiple wandering to 107). The real fit ran but its result was lost to a
  slip in a print statement. Lesson: a supply side bounded on every block needs something that still responds at the top (scarcity pricing,
  demand response). Second form: the bounded import curve plus a small linear remainder,
    X = x_lo + K_xx Phi((p/L - m_x)/s_x) + x_s (p/L - 1) - K_x [1 - Phi((p - c_z)/s_z)] + k . z + e_X,   x_s ~ LogNormal(ln 1.5, 0.6) GW per unit of p/L,
  which keeps supply increasing without limit at a slope a tenth of M1n's (15.1). Expectations of 21.80 unchanged; added: x_s between 1 and 3.

  640 days. Evening shift (17-21h) on evening demand alone: R2 0.04. Adding the same day's midday demand minimum (10-15h): +0.43 +- 0.03 GW

  This is the run-time dimension of supply the user asked for (21.3x), found within the day where it failed between nights (21.64: 0.5 GW).
  Quarter-hourly, all hours: shift on demand and trough depth (demand minus its minimum over the last k hours): R2 0.02 -> 0.11-0.16 for
  k = 4..24 h. The coefficient depends on the hour: 8-hour trough, by local hour 0..23 saved in out/trough_beta.npz (about -0.2 at night,
  near 0 at midday, -0.4 to -0.5 in the evening).
  Two versions, both using only the load forecast (known at issue), same forecaster, own outputs tsshift_*_e_* and tsshift_*_f_*:
    E  A + the 8-hour trough depth as a known covariate for the shift forecaster.
    F  structural: subtract beta(hour) x trough depth from the shift history (24 coefficients from the fitting period), forecast the
       remainder, add beta(hour) x forecast trough depth back, clear.
  The 8-hour window was chosen on physical grounds (start-up lead), not tuned (16 h had the highest R2).
  EXPECTED: evening block better by 5-10% in summer and autumn for at least one of the two, whole day better by about 0.03 ct, night
  unchanged; F at least as good as E if the effect is really linear by hour, E better if the forecaster needs the freedom. Possible null:
  Timestral already sees the demand path over the whole horizon and may have learned this.

21.88 User: "do the distribution at the peaks". Whole-day predictive distributions, five quantile levels (5, 10, 50, 90, 95%) that both routes
  have on disk (exp/day_dist.py). Scores: pinball loss per level and averaged (a proxy for CRPS), coverage and width of the 80% and 90%
  intervals, and on the top 2% of prices: share of slots at or below the 95% quantile and the pinball loss at 95%.
  Routes: A = shift quantiles mapped through the clearing (monotone; carries the shift's uncertainty only); A+ = the same with the load
  forecast's uncertainty added: the price depends on demand minus shift, so the two distances from the median are combined as independent
  (root sum of squares; the load forecaster's own wind, solar and load quantiles); P = Timestral's price quantiles; V = quantile-by-quantile
  average of A+ and P.
  EXPECTED, written before computing: (a) A's raw intervals under-cover more by day than at night: 90% interval about 70-75% over the whole
  day; A+ reaches 83-90%. (b) On the top 2% of prices A+'s 95% quantile does better than P's in summer and autumn (pinball at 95% lower by
  5-15%, and it contains more of those slots), because a symmetric uncertainty in GW becomes a long upper tail in price where the curve is
  steep; no such advantage in winter. (c) Averaged over the five levels, A+ is level with P, and V is better than either by 2-5%.

21.90 User: the quarter-hour price series is smooth "but also weird divots where one would think it should have been arbitraged away. What is
  going on?" Hypothesis written BEFORE looking (exp/divot_look.py; quarter-hour prices are genuine from 1 Oct 2025): the auction matches
  hourly, half-hourly and quarter-hourly orders together, and most supply (thermal plant, blocks, much of the import capacity) is offered
  flat over the hour. Within an hour only the thin quarter-hour-flexible layer (storage, batteries, some gas, demand response) can follow
  the ramp of residual load, so (a) the price deviates from its hourly mean in the direction of the within-hour residual-load deviation,
  with a slope in EUR per GW several times steeper than the slope between hourly means; (b) at the hour boundary the price jumps BACK
  against the trend (the next hour starts again below or above its own mean): a sawtooth with period one hour, largest in the ramp hours
  (06-09h and 16-20h) and the solar shoulder hours; (c) it is not arbitraged away because the day-ahead auction clears all 96 quarter-hours
  at once - "buy the cheap quarter, sell the dear one" needs a physical asset that can shift energy within the hour, and that layer is
  exactly what is thin; the spread is the price of that flexibility.
  EXPECTED numbers: within-hour slope 3-6 times the hour-to-hour slope; the step across the hour boundary negatively correlated (below
  -0.3) with the steps inside the hour; mean absolute boundary step larger than the within-hour steps.

21.91 The divots: results (exp/divot_look.py, divot_fig.py; out/divot_rows.parquet, divots.png). Quarter-hour prices Oct 2025 - Nov 2026, 38,792
  slots; the four prices of an hour differ in 99.7% of hours.
  - Slope of price on residual load WITHIN the hour (deviations from the hourly means): 8.9 EUR/MWh per GW (corr 0.73). BETWEEN consecutive
    hourly means: 4.3 (corr 0.74). Ratio 2.1 (expected 3-6: smaller than I thought, same direction).
  - The step across the hour boundary correlates -0.69 with the price move inside the hour before it, and goes AGAINST the change of
    residual load at 78% of boundaries (inside the hour: 10%). Mean absolute step 0.99 ct across the boundary, 0.83 inside (residual load:
    0.96 and 0.80 GW, i.e. the demand side is smooth across the boundary; the price is not). Expected correlation below -0.3: met.
  - Mean deviation from the hourly mean by quarter: 17h -2.26 / -0.65 / +0.71 / +2.21 ct; 09h +2.01 / +0.56 / -0.65 / -1.92; flat at 03h
    and 13h. Mean absolute spread between last and first quarter by hour: 0.3 ct at 03h up to 3.8-3.9 at 08-09h and 4.0-4.7 at 16-18h. Over
    hours the price spread correlates 0.76 with the residual-load ramp (0.91 ct per GW).
  - The edges of the block products (08:00, 20:00) are not special: steps of 1.8 and 1.3 ct against 1.8 at 09:00 and 1.3 at 19:00.
  Arithmetic: with a ramp of R GW per hour the hourly mean moves 4.3 R per hour while each quarter sits 8.9 x (its distance from the hourly
  mean demand). Three steps of +2.2 R inside the hour, then -2.4 R across the boundary: the sawtooth, with the sizes observed.
  Reading: the hourly level of the price is set on the whole supply curve (hourly and block orders are flat within the hour), the shape
  inside the hour on the much thinner set of offers that can differ between quarters. The spread is not an arbitrage left on the table:
  the auction clears all 96 quarter-hours at once, and taking the spread means delivering more in one quarter and less in another of the
  same hour, which needs an asset that can do that. The spread is the price of that sub-hourly flexibility, about 4.6 EUR/MWh per GW on
  top of the hourly slope.
  For the forecasts: both routes already reproduce it (within-hour part: correlation with the actual 0.77-0.83, error 0.26 / 0.46 / 0.49 ct
  on the used sets against 0.44 / 0.93 / 0.92 with no within-hour shape). Replacing their within-hour shape by a fixed rule (1.07 ct per GW
  fitted on Oct-Nov 2025, times the forecast ramp) is WORSE: whole-day MAE A 0.865 -> 0.939, 1.479 -> 1.587, 1.937 -> 1.978. The slope
  drifted to 0.88 afterwards. Nothing to add to the forecast; it explains the within-hour error component noted in 21.50.

  and 16-18h; in summer far fewer coal units start at all on days with a deep trough.

21.99 Step 2 results (exp/unit_day.py, unit_fig.py; out/unit_hours.parquet, unit_days.csv, units_day.png). 30 lignite units (17.8 GW), 34 hard
  coal (14.2 GW), 53 gas (16.3 GW; TransnetBW gas missing); 499 / 499 / 468 complete days; the units cover 88% of thermal output in the
  evening.
  Running capacity by hour, April-September / October-March, GW: lignite 8.8 / 11.1 at 03h, 8.5 / 11.1 at 12h, 9.0 / 11.3 at 19h (flat
  within the day); hard coal 3.0 / 5.2, 2.4 / 5.2, 3.2 / 5.6; gas 4.7 / 7.2, 2.6 / 7.8, 6.2 / 9.4 (gas cycles: in summer 2.6 GW at noon,
  6.2 GW at 19h). Start-ups in summer are an afternoon event: 14-20h gas 3.8 GW a day, hard coal 1.0, lignite 0.6; 04-08h gas 0.6.
  Evening running capacity on midday running capacity (and evening demand): lignite 0.87 +- 0.02 (R2 0.92), hard coal 0.81 +- 0.02 (0.86),
  gas 0.63 +- 0.02 with +0.10 GW per GW of evening demand (0.83). Evening OUTPUT given evening running capacity: R2 0.95-0.96; the evening
  price/cost adds +0.78 GW per unit for lignite, +0.39 for hard coal and nothing for gas (+0.07 +- 0.09). Output in the evening is set by
  which units are on.
  April-September by depth of the midday trough (shallow / middle / deep; evening demand 27.3 / 28.8 / 29.7 GW):
    lignite   running at midday 9.2 / 8.6 / 7.7, in the evening 9.3 / 8.9 / 8.4, output 7.8 / 7.3 / 6.7, loading 0.82 / 0.80 / 0.79, started 0.3 / 0.6 / 1.0
    hard coal 2.9 / 2.4 / 2.1,                   3.2 / 3.1 / 3.4,                2.4 / 2.2 / 2.5,         0.69 / 0.67 / 0.70,         0.5 / 0.9 / 1.6
    gas       3.8 / 2.6 / 1.9,                   6.1 / 5.8 / 5.3,                4.3 / 3.8 / 3.4,         0.66 / 0.63 / 0.62,         3.2 / 4.2 / 4.3
  Against 21.98: "mostly units off, not held back": YES - loading of running units is the same in all three groups; what differs is how
  much is running. "Mostly hard coal and gas": HALF WRONG - it is lignite (1.0 GW less running, 1.1 GW less output) and gas (0.8 / 0.9 GW);
  hard coal comes back fully (it restarts 1.6 GW after a deep trough). "Evening follows midday one for one for coal, less for gas": 0.81 and
  0.63, and lignite highest at 0.87. "Start-ups cluster at 05-07h and 16-18h": the afternoon cluster yes (16-19h), the morning one only for
  gas and small. "Far fewer coal units start on deep-trough days": WRONG, more start.
  Unit output falls 1.9 GW from shallow to deep although evening demand is 2.4 GW higher.
  Reading: the within-day run-time effect of 21.86 is commitment in the literal sense. After a deep midday trough about 1 GW of lignite
  that went off does not return for the evening and gas does not fully make up for it; the units that do run are loaded as usual. The
  curve's shortfall is a quantity of capacity that is not there, which a price-only curve cannot represent.

21.100 CORRECTION to 21.99 and to the reading of 21.86 (reviewer's check). Running capacity on the SAME days before the trough, April-September,
  shallow / middle / deep midday trough, GW:
    lignite    03-06h 9.30 / 8.93 / 8.27 | midday 9.24 / 8.55 / 7.74 | evening 9.34 / 8.93 / 8.38 | evening two days earlier 9.05 / 9.16 / 8.37
    hard coal  03-06h 3.22 / 3.08 / 3.02 | midday 2.94 / 2.41 / 2.11 | evening 3.18 / 3.05 / 3.42 | two days earlier 3.17 / 3.24 / 3.22
    gas        03-06h 5.09 / 5.03 / 4.27 | midday 3.78 / 2.57 / 1.94 | evening 6.11 / 5.83 / 5.28 | two days earlier 5.80 / 6.30 / 5.19
  On deep-trough days lignite is ALREADY 1.0 GW lower before dawn and was 0.7 GW lower two days earlier; gas is 0.8 GW lower before dawn and
  0.6 GW two days earlier. The evening gaps (lignite 1.0, gas 0.8) are those same gaps. Lignite dips 0.5 GW at midday and is back at its
  morning level by evening; gas cycles down and up fully; hard coal ends higher. So "about 1 GW of lignite that went off does not return
  for the evening" was WRONG. The evening shortfall is a lower level of committed capacity that lasts for days, not a within-day failure to
  restart. The groups are also not alike: 53 of the 82 deep-trough days are in 2026 (14 of 82 shallow), weekend share 0.34 against 0.17. The
  table mixes years and is description only.
  Consequence for 21.86: the statistical finding stands (at equal evening demand the evening has about 0.43 GW less supply per GW of midday
  trough depth; evening thermal output follows midday output), but the mechanism named there, "plant that is off at midday is not started
  for a few evening hours", is not what the unit data show. Days with deep midday troughs are days in spells with less plant committed
  (and more often in 2026). This is the between-day commitment state of 21.43, seen through the day's midday demand. It also fits 21.87:
  a forecaster that sees the last 85 days already knows the current level of commitment, which is why handing it the trough added nothing.
  What stays true from the unit data: output in the evening is set by how much capacity is running (R2 0.95-0.96), units that run are
  loaded alike, gas does cycle within the day (2.6 GW at noon, 6.2 GW at 19h in summer), start-ups are an afternoon event.

21.107 End-to-end check in the real app (scratchpad apprun/debug_run.py, shift_cmp.py, shift_cmp2.py).
  The live run could not exercise the new path: in this sandbox the Open-Meteo forecast host answered 429 and then reset the connection, the
  grid-weather backfill was not finished when the run was stopped, so there was no weather after the last known price and no residual load
  after 10 Nov. No fetch failure was logged, so the 429 as cause is likely, not proven. Instead the app's own objects were driven at a
  pretend cut-off of 7 Nov 22:45 UTC, where the published residual load covers all 192 future quarter hours: both Timestral runs returned
  192 finite values, the three-way mix gave exactly a third each, and the log was written and read back. Live, the second day comes from
  the tree forecast of the residual load on grid weather, which was not exercised here.
  Two defects found by this run and fixed: (a) the forecast log wrote rows of different width when a forecaster was missing in one issue and
  present in the next, and could not be read back - now fixed columns; (b) the app's supply shift differed from the research one by 0.65 GW
  mean absolute, although prices, residual load and the daily gas price were identical: the research code uses the cost index of two days
  before (shift(192)), the app used the same day's for the past and the last known for the future. With the two-day lag in the app the
  difference is 0.003 GW (the first two days of the window, which have no older price).
  One day only, not a result: on 8 Nov the market route had MAE 1.16 ct and the price run 1.74 before fix (b).
  Forward-test hygiene: three things after 9 Nov were printed incidentally - one price (18 Nov 18:00, 20.989 ct), the mean price of
  November to date (23.9 ct) and daily means up to 9 Nov. None was scored or used for a choice.

21.114 User: "yep keep going, test the price effect". Test on the research autumn set (66 days, midnight issue, target day = first day after

  locations the app uses, 1-27 Nov, one request each. apprun/fill_bs.py: only the hours AFTER each store's last value were appended to a
  copy of the stores (live_data), scaled to the store's own history on the overlap (wind and radiation by a factor through the origin,
  median 1.3-1.65 for German wind; the others by an offset; a field missing at a station = mean of the other locations). Correlation with
  the stores' history on the overlap: radiation 0.75-0.9, pressure 0.9-1.0, wind 0.2-0.8. So this is a stand-in for the plumbing, not
  weather of forecast quality: the forecasts of this run are not to be scored.
  apprun/run_live.py: the real service (uvicorn, all 8 regions, real clock 18 Nov 21:15 UTC) with only the weather download switched off.
  It fetched prices and published figures itself (both to 19 Nov 22:45), trained, and answered /prices in 71 s. Log: "Timestral forecast
  of the residual load for 240 quarter hours after 2026-11-19T22:45", "blended timestral (192 quarter hours), market (192 quarter hours)".
  forecastlog: 192 rows, all four columns filled, first quarter hour 18.7455 / 17.6606 / 17.6369 -> issued 18.0143 = a third each.
  loadforecastlog: 192 rows, trees and timestral. Peak memory of the service 3.1 GB. No error, no warning beyond the missing ENTSO-E key.
  This is the first run of the whole new path through the real service loop at the real time, including the second forecast day from
  the tree load forecast on future weather. A first attempt showed a harness artefact, not a defect: a freshly copied store counts as
  up to date for an hour, so the published figures were not refreshed until the file was marked as old.
  Hygiene: prices of 10-19 Nov are in that store; none was printed or scored.

21.122 User: "ok where is structural model in all of this?" -> answer: the curves are a change of coordinates around a black box; the shift
  is the one place with known, unused structure (multi-day commitment level, within-hour sawtooth). User: "Try it".
  Step 0, no new runs: where is the error of the existing shift forecast (slim route, Timestral)? Split the shift forecast error of each
  target day into (a) the daily mean (level; what a commitment state would have to forecast), (b) the hourly profile around it, (c) the
  quarter-hour pattern within the hour (sawtooth). All five used sets.
  EXPECTED before looking: level about half of the squared error (45-60%), hourly profile 30-45%, within-hour 5-10%.

21.123 Step 0 result (exp/shift_decomp.py). Share of the squared shift-forecast error, all five sets: level 35%, hourly profile within the
  day 55%, within hour 10% (per set 33-46 / 44-57 / 9-11). Against 21.122: level 45-60%: NOT MET (35%); profile 30-45%: higher (55%);
  within hour 5-10%: met. So the claim made to the user ("the shift moves with multi-day commitment") names the smaller part. Level MAE:
  Timestral 2.2-2.6 GW against 3.0-3.9 for yesterday's level. Within-hour part: Timestral's forecast is too flat (sd 1.5-2.6 against
  1.9-3.3 true) with correlation 0.68-0.81.
  Step 1, still no new runs: a structural form for each part, fitted on the 56 days before each issue only, against Timestral's forecast
  of the same part, and the hybrid that swaps a part in:
    within hour:  u_qh - mean_hour(u) = b * (x_qh - mean_hour(x)), x = demand: supply follows the hourly product, not the quarter hour.
    hourly profile: mean_hour(u) - mean_day(u) = k_h + l * (mean_hour(x) - mean_day(x)): plant does not follow the daily swing fully.
    level: mean_day(u) = a + r * yesterday's level + g * (mean_day(x) - yesterday's mean_day(x)) + weekend step.
  EXPECTED: within hour - the structural form beats Timestral's (error of that part down 10-25%); profile - the structural form alone is
  worse than Timestral's, their average level or slightly better (0-5%); level - structural worse than Timestral (5-15%), average level.
  Whole shift error with the best hybrid down by 2-5%. If a hybrid lowers the whole shift error by 3% or more in at least four of five
  sets, it goes on to the price test; otherwise the result is that Timestral already has this structure, and that is reported.

  level +6 / +15 / +25 / +39 / +22% (worse); hourly profile +25 / +29 / +15 / +18 / +23% (worse); within hour +15 / +23 / +9 / +13 / +19%

  about 0 (the shift's hourly profile does not follow the demand swing); level r 0.5-0.6.

  as expected, but the averages do not help either. Rule (3% in four of five sets): NOT MET. Timestral already forecasts these three
  parts better than the linear state forms; nothing goes to the price test.
  One more question before closing this: is what Timestral gets wrong predictable from what is known at the issue but not given to it?
  It sees only the residual load. The curves also see only the residual load, so the COMPOSITION (how much of it is solar, wind, load) is
  in neither. Structural reason it could matter: solar and wind are must-take with their own price floor behaviour, and a low residual
  load from high solar is a different market than one from low load. Check without new runs: regress Timestral's shift-forecast error on
  the forecast solar, wind and load (deviations from the residual load's own effect), out of sample by blocks within each set.
  EXPECTED: out-of-sample R2 of the error 2-8% in the summer sets, about 0 in winter. If it is 3% or more in at least three sets, one
  Timestral run per set with solar and wind as further covariates is the test; otherwise closed.

21.125 Composition check, result (exp/shift_comp.py). Out-of-sample R2 of Timestral's shift error from the forecast residual load, solar
  and wind: winter -14%, summer +6.7% (residual load alone +5.1%), autumn -5%, fresh winter -2%, fresh summer -7%. Against 21.124:

  SUMMARY of "structure in the shift" (21.122-21.125): the shift forecast's error is 35% daily level, 55% hourly profile, 10% within hour.
  Linear state forms for each part, fitted on the 56 days before the issue, are worse than Timestral on every part in every set; mixing
  them in does not help; the error is not predictable from the solar / wind / load mix. With an 85-day context and the demand forecast as
  input, Timestral already uses whatever regularity these forms describe. Nothing was built into the application.
  What this does not rule out: structure that needs data the model does not see (plant outages and availability, the neighbours'
  schedules after the auction, intraday state). The earlier tests of neighbours and lagged schedules (21.55, fresh set C) found no gain
  there either. Figure: out/shift_structure.png.

21.126 User: "your model is not right: think of timestral as a sponge which absorbs structure; we are building a diamond core of the sponge.
  So beating timestral is not quite the right goal, rather structurally matching timestral and then timestral sponging a thinner layer."
  This changes the criterion. 21.123-21.125 compared structural forms with Timestral as rivals (and with swaps and averages); the
  arrangement that matters was not run: the core carries what it can, Timestral forecasts only the remainder. Success is now
    (1) how far the core gets alone, level by level (a ledger of the price error as structure is added);
    (2) how thin the layer left to Timestral is (variance of the remainder against the shift it replaces);
    (3) the final forecast is not worse than with Timestral on the whole shift.
  Core levels, all supply-side terms in GW that are functions of the calendar and the demand forecast, fitted on the 85-day context:
    C0 curves + a constant shift (mean of the last 7 days)          C1 + hour-of-day pattern (working day / weekend)
    C2 + within-hour response to the quarter-hour demand ramp       C3 + response to the daily demand swing and to the demand level
    C4 + persistence of yesterday's remaining level (structure alone, no Timestral)
    then: Timestral on the remainder after C3, added back (new runs), against Timestral on the whole shift (the slim route as built).
  EXPECTED before looking: the terms C1-C3 explain 35-50% of the shift's variance in the context (the layer left to Timestral is 50-65% of
  the variance, i.e. 70-80% in GW); in the price ledger the core alone (C4) closes 50-65% of the gap between C0 and the full route;
  Timestral on the remainder matches Timestral on the whole shift within 1.5% over the whole day in every set, no interval clear of zero.

21.127 Ledger, static core (exp/core_fit.py, core_ledger.py). Variance of the shift left after C1 / C2 / C3 in the 85-day context: winter
  89 / 82 / 75%, summer 78 / 69 / 66%, autumn 84 / 78 / 75% (the terms explain 25-34%, not the expected 35-50%). Out of sample the static
  terms do not beat C0, the 7-day mean (price MAE whole day C0 / C3 / C4 / Timestral: winter 1.118 / 1.197 / 1.068 / 0.890; summer 2.177 /
  2.213 / 1.970 / 1.562; autumn 2.589 / 2.767 / 2.613 / 2.000; fresh winter 1.515 / 1.475 / 1.388 / 1.094; fresh summer 2.187 / 2.171 /
  1.929 / 1.531). Gap C0 -> Timestral closed by C4: 22 / 34 / -4 / 30 / 39%; expected 50-65%: NOT MET. The only term that helps is the
  persistence of yesterday's level. Reading: the shift is mostly a moving state, not a function of calendar and demand. A core for it has
  to be a state model, and its remainder for Timestral is the day-ahead innovation.
  State core, each day's forecast made from the days before only:
    K0  the level each quarter hour usually has: exponentially weighted mean over the previous days of the same type (working day /
        weekend), half-life 5 days
    K1  + response to demand: lambda * (demand forecast - its own K0-type typical value), lambda fitted on the context's innovations
    K2  + persistence: rho * yesterday's mean innovation
  Thickness = variance of the day-ahead innovations of K2 against the variance of the shift.
  EXPECTED before looking: K2 closes 50-70% of the gap C0 -> Timestral over the whole day in every set; innovations 35-50% of the shift's
  variance; lambda negative (supply follows demand surprises more than the curves say) between -0.2 and -0.6; rho 0.4-0.7.

  The night is where the core is furthest behind: it has no memory of where the shift stood in the hours before the forecast starts.
  K3 = K1 + carry-over: per hour of the target day, a coefficient on the mean innovation of the last hour before midnight and one on
  yesterday's mean innovation (replaces rho), fitted on the context.

  input as before), the core is added back, the market cleared. exp/ts_core.py.

  against 4.9-8.0 for its forecast of the whole shift: the layer is thinner.
  Core + Timestral on the innovations against Timestral on the whole shift, price MAE: whole day +5.7 / +4.6 / +1.4 / +9.2 / +12.2% (clear

  So criterion (1) and (2) hold and (3) fails with this way of joining them. Suspected reason: a day-ahead innovation series has a break
  at every midnight (the core's terms change there), and Timestral's strength at night is continuing the last hours smoothly.
  Two other joins, same core, fixed before running:
    J1  Timestral forecasts the shift less K1 only (the usual level and the demand response; no carry-over term, so the series is
        continuous apart from K0's slow change), K1 added back. The carry-over is left to Timestral.
    J2  Timestral forecasts the whole shift and gets the core's forecast (K3, day-ahead through the context and for the target day)
        as a further known input beside the demand. The core is inside the sponge.
  EXPECTED: J1 within 3% of the whole-shift route over the day and within 5% at night; J2 within 2% over the day and at night, possibly
  better over the day. Thickness for J2 is read as how far its output is from the core (sd of output minus core).

  it reclassifies them: the state core (usual level from the last days, carry-over) is short memory, re-learned every few days - a faster
  sponge, not a durable layer. Durable = fixed meaning and coefficients that hold: the supply curves (fitted once, Mar 2024 - Nov 2025) and
  any term of the shift whose coefficient is stable over time.

  durable: the import block (a drift the model has no variable for), and the lignite and hard-coal blocks once the price level leaves the
  range of the fit (their costs are constants; hard coal has no coal price). The forecasts were not hurt by this because the shift
  absorbs it and Timestral follows the shift - which is the sponge doing the structure's job. A durable core would need: hard coal's cost
  from the coal price, the import block tied to something that moves (the neighbours' fuel mix and capacities, or at least a trend
  state), and a refit or a check of the price range. Figure: out/durable.png.

  only) + ONE slow level state hold their relative accuracy for 2.7 years, through a doubling of the price level. Without the level
  state the same frozen structure loses half its accuracy in the last two quarters. So the durable layer is "shapes"; the one thing that

  curves, and Timestral following the shift, level included), which is why it did not break in 2026. What it lacks is the split made
  explicit: a core forecast that can stand without Timestral, and a check that prices are inside the fitted range. Figure:
  out/durable2.png.

21.144 External review (ChatGPT, pasted by the user): demand is held fixed (price-inelastic residual load); with only clearing points, many
  pairs of supply and demand curves pass through each point, so a demand slope is not identified without bid data; call it "a merit-order
  model that forecasts prices conditional on residual load", not "a structural model of the German auction"; first upgrade: evidence on
  the demand slope near clearing - does the fixed-demand approximation materially change the predicted price response to a 1 GW shock?
  Aggregated bid curves are not available here (paid EPEX data). What the data do identify: weather moves the residual load for reasons
  unrelated to the price, so the price response to weather-driven residual-load deviations is the slope of the NET curve (supply minus
  any price-responsive demand), which is what the forecast uses. Price-responsive demand that is visible (pumping, exports, plant that
  stops below zero) is already in the model, on the supply side as negative supply.
  Test (exp/net_slope.py): hourly, March 2024 - November 2026. Price and demand (published residual load less other plant) residualised
  on day and on hour-of-day x month x day type (so the slope comes from weather-driven deviations, not the daily cycle). Empirical slope =
  regression of price on demand. Model slope = the same regression on prices from the curves fitted once (M1n, full form), cleared with
  that day's mean shift (so only the curve's own slope enters). Ratio empirical / model by half year and by the day's price level.
  EXPECTED: ratio 0.85-1.15 in the fit window; in 2026 0.7-0.9 at low and middle price levels (flatter: growing flexible demand and
  batteries the model lacks), about 1 at high levels. If the ratio stays within 0.85-1.15 throughout, the fixed-demand approximation does
  not materially change the response to a 1 GW shock on these data.

21.145 Net slope, result (exp/net_slope.py; out/net_slope.csv). Observed / model slope of price on weather-driven demand deviations, by half
  year 2024 H1 .. 2026 H2: 1.05, 0.97, 1.01, 0.95, 0.92, 0.72 (observed 3.0-3.2 EUR/MWh per GW, 2.7 in 2025 H2, 3.2 and 3.8 in 2026; model
  2.8-3.5, then 5.2 in 2026 H2). By the day's price level in 2026 H2: low 0.87, middle 0.78, high 0.54.
  Against 21.144: fit window 0.85-1.15: MET (0.95-1.05). 2026 flatter at low and middle levels, about 1 at high: WRONG in where - the gap
  is largest at high levels. So until mid 2026 the fixed-demand approximation does not materially change the price response to a 1 GW
  shock; in 2026 H2 the curves overstate it by about 40%.
  Candidate supply-side cause, to rule out before calling it demand: the import block responds to p/L, so when the cost index L rose by
  about 60% in 2026 H2, the model's import response per EUR fell by the same factor; 21.135 found flows follow price DIFFERENCES in EUR.
  Counterfactual (no refit): the import block and the storage block read p / L_ref, L_ref = the fit window's mean cost index, instead of
  p / L. EXPECTED: ratio in 2026 H2 rises to 0.85-1.0 overall and the high-level gap closes most; fit window unchanged within 0.05.

21.146 Counterfactual, result. With imports and storage reading p / L_ref (L_ref = 94 EUR/MWh, the fit window's mean; 2026 H2 mean L 161), no
  refit: observed / model 1.02, 1.05, 1.07, 0.94, 1.04, 1.01 by half year (was 1.05 ... 0.72). By level in 2026 H2: low 1.29, middle 1.13,

  and low and middle now too flat in the model by 13-29%); fit window within 0.05: within 0.07.
  READING for the review: on weather-driven variation, the curves with fixed demand reproduce the net price response to a 1 GW shock within
  about 5% from March 2024 to mid 2026. The 40% overstatement in 2026 H2 is explained by the import block's form (response to price over the
  gas cost index), not by missing demand response: with an import response in EUR the overall gap is gone. This identifies the NET slope
  only; a demand slope cannot be separated from the supply slope without bid curves, and some of the remaining level pattern (too flat at
  low levels, too steep at high in 2026 H2) could be either. Cross-border exchange is the part that needs an explicit treatment, as the
  reviewer said and as 21.135 found from the flows. Candidate change (not made): the import block in EUR, refitted, tested on forecasts.

21.147 User: "Missing data is latent state, bayes handles missing data quite well!!!" Right: the demand slope is a latent parameter, and
  whether the data identify it is checked by simulate-and-recover and prior-to-posterior contraction, not ruled out in advance.
  The lever in the data: the published day-ahead residual load (rl) is made before the auction and is price-blind; the realised residual
  load (rlreal) includes whatever responded to the price (flexible demand, and wind and solar curtailed at negative prices). Their gap,
  against the price, carries the slope; forecast errors that happen after the auction are noise independent of the price, except where
  the forecast level itself predicts its error (controlled below by the published level).
  Step 1, description (exp/demand_gap.py): mean gap (realised - published) by price band, after removing its mean by decile of the
  published residual load x hour of day. Raw: mean +0.16 GW, sd 3.42, correlation with price 0.03.
  EXPECTED: the gap is higher at negative prices by 1-3 GW (curtailment raises realised residual load; flexible demand comes on), flat
  within +-0.3 GW from 30 to 150 EUR/MWh, slightly lower at the highest prices (-0.3 to -1 GW, demand that stops). So a slope that is
  concentrated at the low end, not a linear one.

21.148 Step 1, result (exp/demand_gap.py). Gap (realised - published residual load) net of published-level x hour means, by price band:
  below -20 EUR/MWh +1.58 (se 1.93), -20..0 -0.08, 0..1 -0.45, 1..30 -0.57, 30..60 -0.07, 60..90 -0.15, 90..120 +0.01, 120..150 +0.11,
  150..200 +0.19, above 200 +0.79 (se 0.36). Linear slope over 30-150 EUR/MWh: +0.03 GW per 10 EUR/MWh (wrong sign for demand that backs
  off, and economically nil against a supply response of about 3 GW per 10 EUR/MWh). By half year the extreme bands flip sign.
  Against 21.147: negative prices higher by 1-3 GW: only below -20 (+1.6, not clear); flat 30-150: MET; lower at the highest prices: WRONG
  (+0.8). No price-responsive demand visible in this gap.
  Step 2, level M1e (exp/market1e.py), the demand slope as a latent parameter, with the import block in euros:
    auction:   D0 - eta * (p - 90) / 10 = supply_curve(p) + u,  D0 = published residual load - other plant (price-blind), u ~ logistic
    realised:  Dreal = D0 - eta * (p - 90) / 10 + e_fc,  e_fc ~ Student-t (forecast errors after the auction)
    blocks:    fuel outputs, imports, storage around their curves as M1n; imports and storage read p / L_ref (L_ref = fit-window mean of
               L), not p / L
    Jacobian:  supply-curve slope + eta / 10;  eta ~ Normal(0, 1) GW per 10 EUR/MWh;  sd_d ~ HalfNormal(8) (u is now the whole shift)
  Note the change in u: M1n built the balance from REALISED outputs (u sd about 2 GW); M1e clears the curves against published demand, so
  u is the supply shift itself (sd 5-8 GW, autocorrelated - treated as independent here, as before).
  EXPECTED: prior predictive inside the observed summaries; simulate-and-recover with eta = 0.5 recovers it inside its 90% band with a
  posterior sd below 0.15 (identified); real fit: eta between -0.1 and +0.2, posterior sd below 0.15 (prior sd 1: contraction > 6x), so
  the fixed-demand approximation is supported by the posterior, not assumed; the import slope per EUR about the fit-window value of M1n
  (x1 / L_ref = 15.1 / 94 = 0.16 GW per EUR/MWh).

21.149 M1e, results (out/market1e_recover.log, market1e_fit.log; posterior out/market1e_post.npz).
  Simulate and recover (7293 hours, eta = 0.5 true): eta 0.50 (0.49 .. 0.52), sd_fc 3.00, sd_d 5.01; 28 of 30 inside their 90% bands (B_g
  and phi_c just outside), 8 of 8 neighbour coefficients; r-hat 1.002, no divergences. The data identify a demand slope of this size.
  Real fit (15359 hours to Nov 2025): r-hat 1.001, ess 1852, no divergences. eta = 0.04 (0.04 .. 0.05) GW per 10 EUR/MWh, posterior sd 0.005
  against prior sd 1.0 (200x contraction). Forecast-error scale sd_fc 2.58 GW; shift scale sd_d 6.29 GW. Import slope x1 8.90 GW, i.e.
  0.095 GW per EUR/MWh at L_ref 94 (M1n: 15.1 / 94 = 0.16); import step K_x 8.6 GW (M1n 6.8); gas responsive share kap_g 0.28 (M1n 0.38,
  prior percentile 2.9 - near a conflict); m_g 1.15 (M1n 1.37).
  Against 21.148: eta in -0.1 .. +0.2 with sd below 0.15: MET (0.04, sd 0.005). Recovery: MET. Import slope per EUR about 0.16: NOT MET (0.095).
  Held-out year (Dec 2025 - Nov 2026), bare curves without the shift: M1e price MAE 4.39 ct, imports minus curve -6.07 GW; M1n 3.04 ct,
  -3.49 GW. Imports in EUR fix the SLOPE (21.146) but make the LEVEL worse at the 2026 price level: at 144 EUR/MWh the EUR form predicts
  high imports, while the neighbours' prices rose too. Neither p / L nor p / L_ref is the right variable; the price difference to the
  neighbours is (21.135).
  Caveat on eta: hours are treated as independent; errors are strongly autocorrelated, so the posterior sd is too narrow by a large factor.
  The point estimate, about 1% of the supply response, is the result; "tightly identified" is not.
  Incident: market1e.py, copied from market1n.py, wrote its per-hour table to out/market1n_rows.parquet, overwriting M1n's (read by
  da_basis, eve_look, m1b_fig, miss_look, neg_look). Rebuilt from the saved M1n posterior with exp/rebuild_m1n_rows.py; the fitting-period
  summary matches the original log exactly. market1e.py now writes out/market1e_rows.parquet.
  Next check: the net slope (21.144) with the M1e posterior. EXPECTED: observed / model 0.9-1.1 in every half year including 2026 H2.

21.150 Net slope with the M1e posterior (MOD=e exp/net_slope.py; out/net_slope_half_e.csv). Observed / model by half year 2024 H1 .. 2026 H2:
  0.96, 0.96, 0.95, 0.84, 0.92, 0.81 (M1n: 1.05, 0.97, 1.01, 0.95, 0.92, 0.72; M1n with imports in EUR, no refit: 1.02 .. 1.01).
  Against 21.149: 0.9-1.1 in every half year: NOT MET (2025 H2 0.84, 2026 H2 0.81). The refit lowered the import slope (x1 15.1 -> 8.9)
  because the EUR form must also match import LEVELS, which it cannot at the 2026 price level; the curve became steeper in every period.
  Better than M1n in 2026 H2, worse inside the fit window.
  CONCLUSIONS for the review:
  (1) Demand: with the slope as a latent parameter the posterior puts it at 0.04 GW per 10 EUR/MWh, about 1% of the supply response, and
      simulate-and-recover shows a slope of 0.5 would have been found. The fixed-demand approximation is supported by the data, not assumed
      (with the caveat that the posterior width is overstated by treating hours as independent).
  (2) Imports: neither p / L nor p / L_ref is right. The EUR form fixes the slope only with the old parameters; refitted it trades level
      against slope. The variable that holds is the price difference to the neighbours (21.135), which needs their prices: the coupled model.
  M1e is not used in the application and not proposed for it.

21.156 Joint recovery design (sbi_sim2.py), written before the run. Free: markup, pi (offered share of available thermal),
  forced_mean (new, opt-in: mean of the day's forced-outage share, prior Beta(4, 56), mean 0.067; day share ~
  Beta(32 m, 32 (1 - m)); without the option the old Beta(2, 30) is used and old draws are unchanged). Offered thermal =
  pi * (capacity - planned) * (1 - forced): pi and availability enter only as a product, so prices alone give a ridge.
  Published unavailability is public (REMIT, ENTSO-E outages); withholding is not. Each day therefore also carries the thermal
  available capacity per zone with 3% noise. Two fits on the same 5,000 datasets: with and without those two summaries.
  pi and forced_mean are fitted on the logit scale (no draws outside [0, 1]).
  EXPECTED. Without availability: markup contraction >= 0.6; pi and forced_mean each < 0.3; their posterior correlation on
  the observed set positive and > 0.5 (the ridge: more withholding needs less outage). With availability: forced_mean >= 0.8,
  pi >= 0.5, markup unchanged. Coverage 85-95% for all in both fits.
  Counterfactuals (ATC 500 with the same orders: change in B's mean; gas 52 with bids regenerated: change in A's mean), common
  day shocks, for prior draws, both posteriors and the truth: the posterior spread is smaller than the prior's; the ridge
  (no availability) moves the gas effect by less than 1 EUR/MWh across the 90% posterior because the product is pinned;
  both parameter spreads are smaller than the two-book difference on the demo day (2.0 EUR/MWh), which stays the larger
  source of counterfactual uncertainty.

  book family (two-book range) adds to it.

21.161 Follow-ups from a review of 21.158-21.160 (before reporting).
  (a) Historical window, the diagnosis in 21.159 was untested. Realised generation in the window (recon_hourly): lignite mean
  8.5 GW (max 12.6), coal 2.6 (max 4.5), gas 6.4 - against the slice's 18 + 12 GW nominal. Ablations (hist_window.py ABLATE,
  same 13 days and draws; median error = observed minus prior median, robust to scarcity spikes):
                         median error   inside 90% band   quarters with prior median >= 500 (observed: 0)
    base                   +65.1          0.22              0.0%
    flex0 (no flex demand) +53.0          0.31              0.0%
    fleet (13 / 5 GW)      +29.8          0.56              3.3%
    fleet + flex0          +19.5          0.61              4.6%
  The fleet size explains most of the level gap (night error +65 -> +17 to +23); flexible demand bidding at a willingness to
  pay set for gas at 40 explains a further ~10. With the smaller fleet the slice runs short at the evening peak and hits the
  price cap in 3-5% of quarters, which never happened: the missing neighbour (imports up to 13 GW) now shows as false
  scarcity. Remaining +20 is consistent with missing exports and the 75 carbon / 12 coal constants, untested. The figure's
  third title overstated ("the exports it cannot carry"): generation gap 3.7 GW vs mean net export 2.4 GW; retitled.
  (b) Two books on the SAME three days as the posterior counterfactuals (twobook3.py), at the truth: ATC-500 change in B's mean
  book 1 -5.61 (days -2.5, -12.3, -2.0), book 2 -0.73: difference 4.9 EUR/MWh, against the posterior 90% range 6.2 (with
  availability) to 8.1 (without). Same order of magnitude; 21.160's "exceeds the two-book difference (2.0)" compared different
  days and is withdrawn. The demo day (+2.0) is another day; across days the effect ranges -2 to -12.
  (c) Joint coverage figures in 21.160 rest on 400 held-out sets: binomial 95% about +-0.03.
  (d) verify.py check 4 under VOLUME_RULE=max_ab and max_ba: all 300 markets pass brute force, audit, surplus identity and
  monotonicity. surplus_lp docstring corrected. Block limits on the demo day rechecked: 107.7 and 111.7 (unchanged).
  (e) Gas in hist_window.py is the last stored value dated before noon the day before; the app lags gas two days; not matched.

21.162 Fleet first, neighbour second (design after a review: building both at once would repeat the free-everything problem).
  Plumbing (vslice.py, opt-in, old seeds and demo reproduce exactly): Info.tech / coal_price / storage / fixed_netpos /
  wtp_scales_with_gas; outages may be per-quarter arrays.
  Data dated before the window (de_fleet.py): market fleet = sum over units of the largest hourly output in the 12 months
  before (units that made > 50 MW at least once). Oct window (fleet from Oct 2025 - Sep 2026): lignite 22 units 14.6 GW
  (unit data covers 99.8% of lignite output), coal 24 units 10.8 GW (96%; at most 7.1 GW ran at once), gas 53 units
  16.1 GW covering 72.8% of gas output -> scaled to 22.1 GW. Pumped storage: max generation 6.5 GW, pumping 6.3 (slice 2).
  Published unavailability known before noon the day before, latest revision, cancelled dropped, per unit max of overlaps,
  matched to the fleet by name (unmatched: 1 lignite, 2 coal unit names): lignite ~0, coal 0.1-1.2 GW, gas 0.1-2.3 GW per day.
  Note: lignite ran 8.5 GW on average (max 12.6) in the window although 14.6 GW are in the fleet and almost nothing is
  published as unavailable - either economic, or unavailability that is not in this file.
  Costs: carbon in the window 83-90 EUR/t (daily EUA series, out/eua_daily.parquet), not 75. No coal price series: prior
  lognormal(log 12, 0.25) per draw. Flexible demand: flex_wtp is read as the value at reference costs (gas 40, carbon 75)
  and scaled by today's gas-fired cost (opt-in), since at gas ~90 an unscaled N(60, 15) sheds every hour.
  Runs (hist_window.py switches), 13 Oct days, 40 prior draws/day:
    R1 COND           spec fleet; line 0; observed net position as a price-taking order (conditional: an auction outcome)
    R2 COND+FLEET+COSTS
    R3 FLEET+COSTS    synthetic neighbour as before (reference)
    R4 COND+FLEET+COSTS on a second window, 10-23 June 2026, fleet and outages from data before 10 June
  EXPECTED: R1 scarcity < 0.5% of quarters, median error still +40 to +60 (the coal step too long). R2 inside the 90% band
  55-80%, median error within +-25, scarcity < 1%, slice generation - (load + net position) within +-2 GW on average.
  R3 scarcity 3-10%, median error within +-40. R4 inside 50-80%, median error within +-30, worst at the solar hours.

  June's misfit is the daily SHAPE, not the level (figure hist_window_june_cond.png): the slice's median is flat near gas
  cost (100-120) through the evening, while DE-LU rises to 150-550 after sunset; at midday the slice sits near +10 while
  DE-LU clears at 0 to -50. Two named candidates, untested:
  (i) evening: no steep part of the supply curve above gas - all 22 GW of gas share one cost ladder (+-15% of one heat
      rate), no older/peaking units, oil, start costs per day, or scarcity markups; the evening ramp is where real offers
      are steepest; (ii) midday: "other" (10 GW biomass, run-of-river, waste) bids at its cost (~5) plus markup, ~8-10;
      in reality most of it is price-insensitive (feed-in tariffs, run-of-river), and renewables bid below zero.
  Reconciliation holds throughout: observed scheduled generation = load forecast + net position within 31 MW in October
  and -465 MW in June; the slice's generation is 0.7-0.9 GW below that in the conditional runs (storage losses, netting).
  Neighbour (not built yet): ENTSO-E day-ahead NTC exists for DE-DK1 (3.5 GW), DE-CH, partly DE-NL; none for the
  flow-based Core borders (FR, PL, CZ: NoMatchingDataError). An aggregate limit cannot be read off; runs at 2-3 values.

21.165 The shape: evening and midday (design from data before the windows, written before the runs).
  Evidence (recon_hourly, June window and the 3 months before it, evenings 19-22 h by price band): at prices above 200 DE
  ran gas 8.3 GW, lignite 8.8, coal 3.1 out of fleets of 22 / 14.6 / 10.8 GW, and IMPORTED 11 GW; gas output rises only
  from 6.6 to 8.3 GW between 140-200 and 200+. The effective German supply curve is steep above gas cost; in the slice all
  22 GW of gas sit at one cost, so with imports fixed the evening price stays flat. "Other" output is 6-9 GW at any
  price (midday mean 6.8-7.0, nights 7.5-7.6): price-insensitive.
  Change (hist_window.py SHAPE=1; vslice.py opt-in Info.price_insensitive, Info.peak_factor, tech "gaspeak"; old seeds
  reproduce): gas offered at cost = its 99th percentile of hourly output in the 3 months before / 0.84 (Oct 11.8 GW,
  June 12.4, Feb 19.6); the rest of the unit fleet as "gaspeak" at gas cost x peak_factor ~ lognormal(log 1.6, 0.25) per
  draw (prior, not fitted); "other" offered in full at -5, sized at its 3-month mean output / 0.93 (Oct 7.6, Jun 8.3,
  Feb 8.9 GW). Used with FLEETMODE=output (fixed now as the configuration under test).
  Third window, untouched by any choice so far: 10-23 February 2026 (winter; gas, lignite and coal more at the margin).
    S1 June (development)   COND+FLEET+COSTS+output+SHAPE
    S2 October (development) the same
    T1 February (test)       COND+FLEET+COSTS+output, no SHAPE
    T2 February (test)       the same + SHAPE
  EXPECTED: S1 evening (20-24 h) median error from +40 to within +-20, midday (12-16 h) from -24 to within +-15, inside
  the 90% band 60-75%. S2 inside >= 0.75 and median error <= +28 (no worse than R2b). T2 inside >= T1 + 10 points,
  |median error| <= 20, inside 55-80%; scarcity < 1% in all.

21.166 Results of 21.165.
                                     inside 90%   median error   scarcity   median error by 4 h                 
    S1 June + SHAPE (development)       0.60          +6.1          0.0%      +12 +11  +1 -29  +7 +36
    S2 Oct + SHAPE (development)        0.87          +5.9          0.0%      +25 +18 +10  +6  +2 -10
    T1 Feb, no SHAPE (test)             0.61          -8.2          1.8%      -13 -10  -1  -9  +2  -8
    T2 Feb + SHAPE (test)               0.54         -12.2          3.9%      -16 -14  -7 -13  -4 -11
  Against 21.165: S1 inside 60-75% MET (0.60) but evening within +-20 NOT MET (+36) and midday within +-15 NOT MET (-29): the
  change widened the band without fixing the two errors it was meant for. S2 MET (0.87, +5.9). T2 inside >= T1 + 10 points
  NOT MET (-7 points), |median error| <= 20 MET, inside 55-80% NOT MET (0.54), scarcity < 1% NOT MET (3.9%).
  DECISION: SHAPE is not adopted. It improves the windows it was designed on and makes the untouched window worse.
  The configuration that stands is the one fixed before February: unit fleet + output-sized lignite/coal/other + costs,
  conditional on the net position. On the untouched February window it reaches 61% inside the band with a level error of
  -8 and false scarcity in 1.8% of quarters.
  February's picture (hist_window_feb_cond.png): the median tracks the observed price (~100) closely, but the band's upper
  tail reaches the cap in 489 of 1,344 quarters: in tight winter hours (load ~70 GW, exports up to 12 GW), draws with a low
  offered share pi (prior Beta(18, 2), 5% quantile 0.77) run out of supply. The prior's lower tail for pi is implausible for
  winter; that is a finding about the prior, to be confronted with the posterior rather than tuned away here.
  Reconciliation: observed generation - (load forecast + net position): Oct +0.03 GW, June -0.47, Feb -4.56 GW. In winter
  the load forecast exceeds scheduled generation net of exports by 4.6 GW (self-generation outside 14.1.C, or the load
  forecast's definition); the slice, fed the load forecast, therefore generates 2.5 GW MORE than observed in February.
  What remains open on the shape: June evenings (+36) and middays (-29) are still wrong with and without SHAPE; candidates
  not tested: the evening price reflects imports' price (neighbours' evening scarcity) rather than German supply - which the
  conditional design cannot represent, since it fixes the import volume but not its price; and solar's day-ahead bids.

21.167 User: "Gas split should use latent structure to learn diff price elas of supply?" - yes: 21.165 fixed the shape by
  hand (share from output, multiple from a prior), nothing was learned. Earlier finding (section 1 of these notes): gas
  utilisation against (price - 1.85 gas - 0.37 carbon) is one curve mid-2024 .. Nov 2026, so the curve is durable.
  Model (gas_curve.py, NumPyro NUTS, direct likelihood, hourly, no clearing):
    m_t = p_t - (1.85 gas_d + 0.37 co2_d)
    gasg_t ~ StudentT(nu, F_month(t) + A * sigmoid((m_t - mu_b(t)) / s_b(t)), sigma)
    F_month: must-run floor (CHP heat), random walk over months; A: dispatchable capacity; b = time-of-day block (0-6,
    6-10, 10-16, 16-20, 20-24); mu_b ~ N(mu, tau_mu), log s_b ~ N(log s, tau_s) - partial pooling = hour-specific price
    elasticity of supply. Baseline: one mu, one s (pooled).
  Fit: 12 months to 11 Aug 2026. Test (untouched): 12-25 Aug 2026, gas output predicted from observed prices, using the
  last month's floor carried forward. Identification caveat: price and output are joint; relies on weather-driven demand
  moving along a stable curve.
  EXPECTED: the blocks differ (|mu_b - mu_b'| > 20 EUR/MWh for some pair, or s_b differing by a factor > 1.5); on the test
  window the block model's MAE beats the pooled model's by >= 10%; its 90% predictive intervals cover 85-95%.

21.168 Result of 21.167 (gas_curve.py; out: gascurve/gas_curve.json, gas_curve_draws.npz). 8,750 fit hours, 336 test
  hours (12-25 Aug 2026). Both models: 0 divergences, r-hat <= 1.005, ESS >= 389. 90% intervals in EUR/MWh / GW.
    pooled: half-utilisation margin mu 10.0 (8.6-11.4), width s 23.2 (22.1-24.4), dispatchable A 7.14 GW (6.94-7.34)
    blocks: mu_b 0-6h 8.6, 6-10h 17.7, 10-16h 5.1, 16-20h 18.6, 20-24h 15.0;
            s_b 20.2, 22.3, 17.8, 25.3, 30.2;  A 7.55 GW (7.35-7.78)
    must-run floor by month Aug 2025 .. Aug 2026 (GW): 0.1 0.9 2.6 5.5 5.3 8.4 8.1 3.3 2.9 1.5 1.2 1.0 0.8
    test (floor of the last month carried forward): MAE pooled 1.131 GW, blocks 1.106 (gain 2.2%); bias +0.57 / +0.54;
    90% coverage 0.958 / 0.961.
  Against 21.167: blocks differ - widths by a factor 1.7 (midday 17.8 vs late evening 30.2) MET, locations by 14 (< 20)
  NOT MET; test MAE gain >= 10% NOT MET (2.2%); coverage 85-95% NOT MET (0.96, slightly wide).
  Reading: the hour-specific elasticity is real (late-evening gas responds to price over a 1.7x wider range than midday
  gas) but adds little out of sample. What the latent curve says that matters more for the slice: only ~7.5 GW of German
  gas responds to price, spread smoothly from about cost - 40 to cost + 70 (mu +- 2.2 s), on top of a must-run floor that
  is ~1 GW in summer and ~8 GW in Jan-Feb (CHP heat). The slice offers 22 GW at cost +- 7%. The winter floor also explains
  why February's gas p99 (16.4 GW) was so much higher than summer's (~10).
  Next: replace the slice's gas offers by the learned curve (floor as price-taking, the dispatchable part as a ladder at
  cost + logistic quantiles, parameters drawn from the posterior), and a predictor for the floor (temperature / heat
  demand) so that it can be carried into a new season rather than from the last month.

21.169 User: is the per-hour elasticity really min run times / start costs? Test (gas_curve2.py), written before running.
  If intertemporal constraints drive the block differences of 21.168:
   (a) static block-model residuals rise with (mean margin over +-3 h - current margin): slope > 0.02 GW per EUR/MWh;
   (b) hysteresis: at the same margin, residuals higher when the margin is falling than rising (by > 0.2 GW);
   (c) with m_eff = m + lambda (m_win - m), lambda ~ Beta(1, 1) latent: lambda clearly > 0 (90% interval above 0.2), the
       block widths' ratio max/min falls from 1.7 to below 1.3 and the location spread from 14 to below 8 EUR/MWh;
   (d) out of sample (12-25 Aug 2026) the dynamic pooled model beats the static block model.
  If (a)-(c) fail, the hour differences are not explained by this commitment proxy.

21.170 Result of 21.169 (gas_curve2.py; out: gascurve/gas_curve2.json). Same 12 fit months, same Aug test window.
  (a) static block residuals against (m_win - m): slope 0.035 GW per EUR/MWh (by block 0.085 0.048 0.083 0.031 0.049) MET.
  (b) hysteresis, falling minus rising margin at the same margin: +0.02 GW NOT MET (no sign of it in this crude form).
  (c) lambda: pooled 0.84 (0.81-0.87), blocks 0.93 (0.89-0.96) - gas output follows the +-3 h mean margin far more than
      the current hour's - MET. Block differences shrink but do not vanish: location spread 14 -> 9.3 (NOT below 8;
      early evening +18.6 -> +12.6, morning +17.7 -> +8.3), width ratio 1.7 -> 1.57 (NOT below 1.3; late evening 26.2 vs
      midday 16.7). Both fits: 0 divergences, r-hat <= 1.01.
  (d) test MAE: static pooled 1.131, static blocks 1.106, dynamic pooled 1.015, dynamic blocks 0.967 GW (-14.5% against
      static pooled); 90% coverage 0.946 / 0.958. MET.
  Reading: mostly yes. The largest single gain out of sample is the multi-hour margin, not the hour blocks, and most of
  the morning and early-evening location differences were commitment in disguise. What remains is a flatter response in
  the late evening (wider curve), which this proxy does not explain. Limits: m_win is a symmetric +-3 h mean, a crude
  stand-in for start costs and minimum run times (all 24 hours clear at once, so using later hours is legitimate for a
  day-ahead bid); no hysteresis showed. A direct test would use the per-unit data (entsoe/units_all) to count starts and
  run lengths, and check whether units started for the evening run through the following hours at negative margins.
  Implication for the slice: gas offers should respond to a multi-hour margin - block orders spanning several hours with
  start costs, i.e. the structure the slice already has for coal and gas peak blocks, rather than hour-by-hour steps.

21.171 Unit-level commitment test (delegated; research/coupled-auction/unit_commitment.py, notes/unit_commitment.md,
  unit_commitment.png; reviewed against the files). Pre-set overall criterion (E1 + three of E2-E5) NOT MET: E1, E3 met.
  50 gas units >= 100 MW, 15.2 GW, median 72% of system gas; alternate days only (187 single-day stretches).
  - E1: with margin, clock hour and season fixed, output rises 0.057 GW per EUR/MWh of (m_win - m) (0.051-0.072); 80%
    (77-83%) of it is capacity switching on/off, 12% load factor. The current-margin response is also 75% on/off.
  - E2 (narrow miss): 24.2% of run-hours at negative margin (threshold 25%); run-average margin exceeds the start-hour
    margin by 16 EUR/MWh; 40% of starts at negative margin; margin -31 three hours before a start, +7 at it, +22 over the run.
  - E3: 7.4% of runs under 3 h; Kaplan-Meier median run 12 h, median down-time 8 h.
  - E4 NOT MET, and a CORRECTION to 21.168/21.170: the wider late-evening curve (1.6x) is not in the unit data, and system
    gas output gives the same 0.78 or 1.28 evening/midday width ratio under simpler fits; the 1.6x was a property of the
    hierarchical shared-amplitude specification, not of the plants.
  - E5 NOT MET: no hysteresis (falling - rising -0.03 GW, -0.28 .. +0.45); capacity on responds equally to the next and
    the previous 3 h (0.028 / 0.027): the window is centred.
  For the slice: commitment blocks of ~6-12 h, minimum run and down times ~3-4 h, centred window, no hysteresis term;
  start cost only bounded (per-run gross margin median 55 EUR/MW, lower quartile 7). Caveats: midnight censoring of the
  single-day stretches; the 1.85 heat rate is a CCGT number; 20% threshold counts low-load CHP as off.
